Anomalous shift and optical vorticity in the steady photovoltaic current
Penghao Zhu, A. Alexandradinata

Abstract
Steady illumination of a non-centrosymmetric semiconductor results in a bulk photovoltaic current, which is contributed by real-space displacements (‘shifts’) of charged quasiparticles as they transit between Bloch states. The shift induced by interband excitation via absorption of photons has received the prevailing attention. However, this excitation-induced shift can be far outweighed ( ≪ \ll ) by the shift induced by intraband relaxation, or by the shift induced by radiative recombination of electron-hole pairs. This outweighing ( ≪ \ll ) is attributed to (i) time-reversal-symmetric, intraband Berry curvature, which results in an anomalous shift of quasiparticles as they scatter with phonons, as well as to (ii) topological singularities in the interband Berry phase (‘optical vortices’), which makes the photovoltaic current extraordinarily sensitive to the linear polarization vector of the light source. Both (i-ii) potentially lead to nonlinear conductivities of order m ​ A ​ V − 2 mAV^{-2} , without finetuning of the incident radiation frequency, band gap, or joint density of states. A case study of BiTeI showcases the anomalous shift and optical vorticity in a realistic material.



I
Introduction

Light that is harvested for large-scale power transmission needs to be rectified, i.e., converted from electromagnetic waves at solar frequencies to a direct or low-frequency current. Rectification in a non-centrosymmetric, non-magnetic semiconductor results in a bulk direct current that is proportional to the radiation intensity, in the lowest order response. This
bulk photovoltaic current
has a contribution attributed to an asymmetry in the fermionic quasiparticle distribution
Danishevskii et al. 1970
;
Grinberg 1970
;
Belinicher and Sturman 1980
and a second contribution attributed to the real-space displacements (or ‘shifts’) of quasiparticles as they transit between Bloch states; cf. Fig.
1
.
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Belinicher et al. 1982

The shift induced by interband
exc
itation via absorption of photons – in short, shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
– has received the prevailing attention. However, the steady photovoltaic current is also contributed by a shift induced by
rec
ombination of electron-hole pairs, as well as a shift induced by
intra
band relaxation via scattering with phonons or impurities. Both shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
and shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
have been emphasized by Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman (henceforth referred to as BIS) in their 1982 kinetic theory of the shift current, which accounts for the steady, non-equilibrium quasiparticle distribution.
Belinicher et al. 1982
In contrast, shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
and shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
have been ignored in all recent literature, which either (a) disregarded relaxation completely,
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
;
Parker et al. 2019
;
Young and Rappe 2012
;
Tan et al. 2016
;
Wang et al. 2017
;
Ibañez Azpiroz et al. 2018
;
Ahn et al. 2021
or (b) were agnostic about the nature of the relaxation mechanism, e.g., by naive relaxation-time approximations,
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
Matsyshyn and Sodemann 2019
;
Holder et al. 2020
;
Hornung and von
Baltz 2021
or (c) adopted relaxation mechanisms that are unrealizable in experiments, e.g., by scattering with a ‘fermionic bath’
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
;
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016b
;
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
.
1
1

1

(d) Barik and Sau have considered electron-phonon scattering as a relaxation mechanism for the shift current.
Barik and Sau 2020
However, they
assumed without justification that phonon-mediated scattering does not result in a shift. A detailed criticism of the recent literature is provided in App.
C.3
.
The one-sided interpretation of shift currents as a dissipation-less, ‘hot carrier effect’
Tan et al. 2016
cannot explain the vanishing photocurrent in the low-temperature polar phase of organic charge-transfer complexes.
Nakamura et al. 2017
To recapitulate, excitation, recombination and relaxation induce shifts which may counteract or synergize, and a complete model of the kinetic processes is required to quantitatively predict the steady photovoltaic current.
Sturman 2020

Figure 1:
(a) The kinetic processes of excitation, relaxation and recombination in a steady state of a homogeneously illuminated semiconductor. (b) These kinetic processes can be recast as loops in energy-momentum space, if one views a hole as an electron going backward in time. To each transition between Bloch states we associate a shift of a wavepacket in real space, as illustrated representatively by the dashed arrows in panel (c); the net shift over all possible loops results in the steady shift current.

While the shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
/shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
currents have been explored for simplified models of piezoelectrics and pyroelectrics,
Belinicher et al. 1982
there has not been an attempt to relate the shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
/shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
currents to notions of quantum wave function geometry which have revitalized the condensed matter field. Here, we identify scenarios (unimagined by BIS) in which the shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
or shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
current dominates over the shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
current by an order of magnitude. Such dominance is attributed to two quantum geometric properties of the Bloch wave function, namely (i) time-reversal-symmetric,
intra
band Berry curvature [cf. Fig.
2
(a)], which results in an anomalous shift of quasiparticles as they are scatter with phonons, and (ii) topological singularities in the
inter
band Berry phase known as ‘optical vortices’ [cf. Fig.
2
(c)], which makes the photovoltaic current extraordinarily sensitive to the linear polarization vector of the light source.

Both effects (i-ii) will be demonstrated in model Hamiltonians with generic values of the joint density of states and without assuming a semimetallic band gap. The nonlinear conductivities in our models are of order
mAV^{-2}
without finetuning of the incident radiation frequency,
2

Tan and Rappe 2016
or vanishing
Ahn et al. 2020
;
Chan et al. 2017
;
Yang et al. 2018
band gaps, which makes for a questionable application to solar cells.

3

0.05mAV^{-2}
when only shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
is accounted for.
Young and Rappe 2012

Panels (a-b) derive from a model Hamiltonian with intraband Berry curvature, and (c-d) from a different model Hamiltonian with optical vortices. In panel (a), a time-reversal-symmetric distribution of intraband Berry curvature is plotted as a color field, with yellow (blue) representing positive (resp. negative) curvature. , which dominates the total current.

I.1
Outline of paper

•

As a preliminary step to substantiating these results, Sec.
II
first reviews BIS’s kinetic theory for the shift current
Belinicher et al. 1982
and several salient properties of the non-equilibrium distribution of photo-excited carriers.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
In addition, we will formalize an underappreciated distinction between the transient and steady shift currents; in particular, the transient shift current in intrinsic semiconductors will be shown to be identical to the current calculated by Kubo-type perturbation theories (e.g., by Kraut and Baltz,
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
and by Sipe and Shkrebtii
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
), which assume a weak perturbation from thermal equilibirium. The difference between the steady and transient shift currents will turn out to be the sum of the shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
and shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
currents.

•

Sec.
III
demonstrates the relevance of shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
in the presence of time-reversal-symmetric intraband Berry curvature.

•

Sec.
IV
demonstrates the relevance of shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
in the presence of optical vortices. Because Sec.
III
also introduces our method of calculating the shift current via loops, we recommend that Sec.
III
be read before Sec.
IV
.

•

Sec.
V
showcases the importance of both shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
and shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
in the 3D polar semiconductor BiTeI, which has an appreciable Berry curvature as well as optical vorticity.

•

Finally, Sec.
VI
summarizes our results, gives directions to finding photovoltaic material with the desired wave function geometry, comments on experimental discrepancies between the transient and steady photovoltaic current, elaborates on the notion of loop currents, and discusses the potential of shift-current materials for solar cell applications.

II
Kinetic theory of the shift current

The BIS kinetic theory presupposes that carrier-optical-phonon scattering (rather than carrier-carrier scattering) is the dominant mechanism of energy relaxation for photo-excited carriers in the ‘active region’. A carrier is said to be in the active region if its energy (defined with respect to the conduction/valence band extremum for an electron/hole) exceeds the optical phonon threshold:
E>\hbar\Omega_{o}
, as illustrated by the yellow energy intervals in Fig.
3
(b). The dominance of carrier-optical-phonon scattering over carrier-carrier scattering occurs for not-too-high carrier densities, which is typical of most continuous-wave laser experiments.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
;
Zakharchenya et al. 1982

Figure 3:
(a) The excitation process in a transient state of a homogeneously illuminated semiconductor. White dots against a black background represent hole carriers. The purple ellipse should be understood as a cross-section of an ellipsoidal excitation surface. Panel (b) represents the steady state; yellow and blue energy intervals indicate the active and passive regions respectively. (c) A representative illustration of the quasiparticle distribution
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
in the conduction band, with
f_{E}
being the average of
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
for all
\boldsymbol{k}
satisfying
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=E
. Inset displays the same function
f_{E}
in the active region, but with a much finer scale for the horizontal axis. The shape of this distribution is supported by theoretical models [cf. App.
B.2
] and hot-carrier photoluminescence spectroscopy [e.g., Fig 25 in Ref.
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
].

The BIS formula for the shift current can be compactly expressed as:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=&\;-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\bigg({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\bigg);\;\;\;\;B=(b\boldsymbol{k}),\;\;\;\;B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\;\;\;\;m=(\boldsymbol{q}p),}

(1)

with
|e|
the absolute value of the electron charge,
{\cal V}
the volume of the medium, and
B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
a collective label for a Bloch state in band
b
with wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}
.
\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}
sums over all possible quasiparticle transitions (
B^{\prime}\leftarrow B
) mediated by a boson of mode
m
;
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
is specified by a bosonic wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
and a bosonic branch/band
p
.
\boldsymbol{S}
and
{\cal A}-{\cal E}
, which appear in the summand of
\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}
, will be explained in turn.

The shift vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
is the real-space displacement of a Bloch quasiparticle as it transits from
B
to
B^{\prime}
, by way of absorbing/emitting a boson of mode
m
. For phonons,

\displaystyle{Phonon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\arg V^{m}_{B^{\prime},B}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}},}

(2)

with
V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}
being the electron-phonon matrix element [cf. Eq. (
84
)],
\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
the Berry connection
3
3

3

u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
denotes the cell-periodic component of the Bloch function; the inner product
\big\langle x\,|\,y\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
involves an integral over the intracell coordinate; cf. Eq. (
72
).
, and
-m=(-\boldsymbol{q}p)
being the momentum-reversed partner of
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
.
4
4

4

In the BIS paper, all mode indices were omitted from their phononic shift, and their electron-phonon-matrix element was never explicitly defined, but one may guess that
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k},p}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}
in our notation corresponds to
\boldsymbol{R}_{b}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k})
in their notation.

For a photonic mode
m
with linear polarization vector
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}
,

\displaystyle{Photon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(3)

We have assumed that the photon wavelength greatly exceeds the lattice period; within the dipole approximation, photon-mediated transitions are
vertical

\equiv\,\boldsymbol{k}
-preserving [cf. red and blue lines in Fig.
3
(b)], and the shift vector depends on
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
only through
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}
. For this reason, we often use
\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}
synonymously, when
m
is photonic. Eq. (
3
) is henceforth referred to as the
photonic shift vector
, and Eq. (
2
) as the
phononic shift vector
. In either case, the sign of the shift vector is inverted if the Bloch labels are interchanged and the bosonic wavevector simultaneously inverted:
\boldsymbol{q}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{q}
, reflecting that forward and backward transitions (between the same pair of Bloch states) result in opposite shifts.

For either type of boson,
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
(resp.
{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}
) is the transition probability rate for absorbing (resp. emitting) a boson of mode
m
. As explicitly written in Eqs. (
27
)-(
28
), both
{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
have the golden-rule forms that are familiar from Dirac’s perturbation theory.
Dirac 1927
In particular,
{\cal A}^{m}\propto N_{m}
and
{\cal E}^{m}\propto(N_{m}+1)
, with
N_{m}
the average occupancy of the boson
m
. For phonons,
N_{m}
is assumed to follow the Planck distribution with lattice temperature
T_{l}
; for photons,
N_{m}
is a sum of thermal and non-thermal contributions, with the latter being generated by the light source. Additionally, both
{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
depend on the quasiparticle distribution functions
f_{B}
in a manner consistent with Pauli’s exclusion principle:
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\propto(1-f_{B^{\prime}})f_{B}
and
{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\propto(1-f_{B})f_{B^{\prime}}
. Consequently,
\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{j}[f_{B},N_{m}]
depends on
f_{B}
and
N_{m}
through
{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
; however, the dependence on
N_{m}
will subsequently be made implicit:
\boldsymbol{j}[f_{B},{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{N_{m}}$}\hskip-16.72108pt\rule[3.41666pt]{16.72108pt}{1.0pt}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{N_{m}}$}\hskip-16.72108pt\rule[3.41666pt]{16.72108pt}{1.0pt}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{N_{m}}$}\hskip-13.72487pt\rule[2.39166pt]{13.72487pt}{1.0pt}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{N_{m}}$}\hskip-12.5638pt\rule[1.70833pt]{12.5638pt}{1.0pt}}}]
, to simplify notation.

Our expression for the shift current is derived in App.
C.1
and is slightly more general than the expression presented in the BIS paper,
Belinicher et al. 1982
in that ours allows for interband, phonon-mediated transitions while theirs do not.

Let us consider three scenarios for the quasiparticle and bosonic distributions:

(I-no source) Without photo-excitation by a source, quasiparticles, phonons and photons are all thermalized with an equilibrium temperature
T_{0}
, and Eq. (
1
) manifestly vanishes owing to detailed balance:
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}={\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}
; cf. Eq. (
38
).

(II-transient state) This balancing is disrupted when the light source is switched on. At the onset of radiation, the quasiparticle distribution retains its equilibrium value (the Fermi-Dirac function
f^{T_{0}}_{B}
) but the non-thermal photons drive a transient current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B}]
, which is purely attributed to vertical, interband transitions throughout the
excitation surface
, as illustrated in Fig.
3
(a). The excitation surface (
ES
) is defined as the surface in the Brillouin zone where the difference in conduction- and valence-band energies equals the source photon energy:
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}=\hbar\omega
. The meaning of being ‘purely attributed’ is that the sum over all quasiparticle transitions [in Eq. (
1
)] is contributed nontrivially only by vertical transitions throughout the excitation surface, i.e., the value of
\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B}]
does not change if the summation
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is restricted such that
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
lies on the excitation surface:
5
5

5

This is proven more elaborately in App.
E
.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B}]=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B}]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}.}

(4)

If the non-thermal photons are well approximated by a classical electromagnetic wave, then
\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B}]
reduces to the Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shkrebtii formula
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
calculated by Kubo-type perturbation theory, as demonstrated in App.
C.3
.

(III-steady state) For
t\gg\tau_{\text{rec}}\sim 1ns
(a typical time scale for radiative interband recombination
Esipov and Levinson 1987
;
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
), the transient current evolves to a steady current:
\boldsymbol{j}[f_{B}]
, with the non-equilibrium distribution
f_{B}
being the steady solution to a kinetic equation encoding all the processes in Fig.
1
(a); cf. App.
B.2
. The difference between the equilibrium
f_{B}^{T_{0}}
and the non-equilibrium
f_{B}
is caricatured in Fig.
3
(a) vs (b).
Henceforth,
\boldsymbol{j}[f]
and
\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}]
will be our shorthand for the steady and transient currents, respectively.

While in principle the BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)] for
\boldsymbol{j}[f]
sums over all possible quasiparticle transitions, it is worth in practice to identify the
predominant
transitions that make an outsize contribution to the summation; throughout this work, our use of ‘predominant’ should be understood as significantly contributing to the
steady
, non-equilibrium shift current.

It is simplest to consider the predominant transitions in an intrinsic, direct-gap semiconductor, with
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
minimized at a single wavevector:
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=E_{g}
. The band gap is assumed to exceed the optical phonon energy (
E_{g}\gg\hbar\Omega_{o}
), such that phonon-mediated transitions are intraband; cf. green curves in Fig.
3
(b). A typical electron-optical-phonon scattering time is
\tau^{o}\sim 100fs
.
Lundstrom 2010
;
Na et al. 2019

The lattice temperature is assumed to be small (
k_{B}T_{l}\ll E_{g},\hbar\Omega_{o})
, so that the emission of optical phonons outweighs the absorption. Supposing that carriers are optically excited into the active region with energy
E_{\text{exc}}
, then the transitions illustrated in Fig.
3
(b) predominate. Indeed, the vast difference in relaxation time scales:
\tau^{o}\ll\ll\tau_{\text{rec}}
,
Esipov and Levinson 1987
;
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
favors fast, intraband transitions by emission of optical phonons with energy
\geq\hbar\Omega_{o}
; carriers quickly relax into a
passive region
, defined as the energy interval near a band extremum where the carrier energy
E<\hbar\Omega_{o}
[cf. blue interval in Fig.
3
(a)]; carriers in the passive region can no longer relax via optical phonons, and remain in the passive region till they are annihilated in the slower process of radiative recombination.
6
6

6

Auger recombination empirically occurs at much higher photo-excited carrier densities than the present consideration.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
The majority of photo-excited carriers are thus contained within the passive region, with a steady distribution that depends on whether electron-electron scattering or electron-acoustic-phonon scattering is the dominant mechanism for energy relaxation in the passive region [Fig.
3
(c)]. However, fine-grained details about the carrier distribution within the passive region do not matter to estimating the shift current, because the optical phonon threshold
\hbar\Omega_{o}
is typically a small fraction of the band gap
E_{g}
, and
E_{g}
is the energy scale for significant variations of the energy-dependent shift vectors.
7
7

7

The majority of recombination transitions occur at
\boldsymbol{k}
points close to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
and contained within the passive region. Each recombination transition is associated with a photonic shift
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
3
)], which may as well be approximated as
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
, because the variation of the photonic shift vector within the passive region is small. Likewise, the current induced by phonon-mediated transitions within the passive region is outweighed by the current induced by phonon-mediated transitions outside the passive region, assuming that the active region is much bigger than the passive region. This assumption holds for most radiation frequencies, because
\hbar\Omega_{o}
is a tiny fraction of the band width. To formalize this discussion, one may split the line integral in Eq. (
6
) to a short-line integral within the passive region and a long-line integral within the active region; the long-line integral dominates, because the Berry curvature typically varies on the scale of
E_{g}\gg\hbar\Omega_{o}
.
For additional details on the predominant relaxation mechanisms in a direct-gap semiconductor, we refer the reader to App. A and B.

By decomposing the BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)] according to the three classes of transitions sketched in Fig.
1
(a), one obtains
\boldsymbol{j}[f]=\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
, which is the precise meaning of the shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
, shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
and shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
currents mentioned colloquially in our introduction. To clarify, the
intraband current
is extracted from Eq. (
1
) by restricting the band summations
\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}
by the condition
b=b^{\prime}
:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{b=b^{\prime}}
; the
excitation-induced current
is extracted by restricting the wavevector summations
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
with the condition that
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
lies on the excitation surface:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}
; the
recombination-induced current

\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
restricts
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
to lie outside the excitation surface. Explicit expressions for the threefold decomposition of
\boldsymbol{j}
are given in App.
A.4
.

Let us argue that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
in a steady state is well approximated by
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}
in a transient state. Our argument relies on the following property of
the non-equilibrium quasiparticle distributions: for all

\boldsymbol{k}\in ES,f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\ll 1
and
(1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}})\ll 1
, as illustrated by the inset in Fig.
3
(c).
The smallness of
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
and
(1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}})
originates from the slowness in optical excitations compared to the fastness of inelastic collisions by carrier-carrier and carrier-phonon scatterings. In other words, despite the continuous generation of electron-hole pairs by photon absorption, inelastic scattering processes are so efficient that the non-equilibrium carrier distribution (over the excitation surface) never builds up to significance; this statement is derived rigorously in App.
E
. Thus for the purpose of computing the excitation-induced current
(\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES})
in a non-equilibrium state, one may as well input the equilibrium distribution:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}\approx\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}
, since it also holds that
f^{T_{0}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\ll 1
and
(1-f^{T_{0}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}})\ll 1
, assuming
k_{B}T_{0}\ll E_{g}
. Recalling a similar expression for the transient current in Eq. (
4
), we deduce that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}\approx\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}},
implying that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
is precisely what is missed from previous Kubo-type theories
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
that purport to calculate a steady shift current.

III
Anomalous shift

In connection to
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
, our first main result is that the phononic shift induced by small-angle, intraband scattering is expressible in terms of the intraband Berry curvature:

\displaystyle{Phonon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{b;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}+O(\delta k^{3}),\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{b;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k},}

(5)

with the curvature defined as
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}=\nabla\times\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}
;
\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}=({\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})/{2}
and
\delta\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}
are the average and difference in quasiparticle wavevectors, and
\delta k=||\delta\boldsymbol{k}||
. The
anomalous shift
(
\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}
) is purely a geometric property of the quasiparticle wave function, and is insensitive to the nature of the electron-phonon coupling; such coupling affects the shift current only through the transition rate; cf. Eq. (
27
).

Our use of ‘anomalous’ evokes a comparison with the anomalous velocity correction in the semiclassical equation of motion,
Chang and Niu 1996
;
Sundaram and Niu 1999
which gives an anomalous displacement:
\delta{\boldsymbol{r}}_{ano}=\Omega_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\times\delta{\boldsymbol{k}}
for a wave packet of Bloch states in band
b
. In the photovoltaic context,
\delta{\boldsymbol{k}}
is driven by a phonon-induced electric field rather than an externally applied field. Indeed, phonons in non-centrosymmetric semiconductors induce macroscopic electric fields, which cause the electron-phonon matrix element
V^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}
to diverge as
\delta\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{0}
.
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
In the self-consistent-field approximation,
Vogl 1976

V^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}=f^{m}_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
plus asymptotically irrelevant terms;
f^{m}_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}
diverges as
1/\delta k
for ‘polarization scattering’
Fröhlich 1937
;
Fröhlich and Mott 1939
with optical phonons, and as
1/\delta k^{1/2}
for ‘piezo-acoustic scattering’
Meijer and Polder 1953
with acoustic phonons.
8
8

8

The explicit expressions for
f^{m}
can be found in equation (3.12) of Ref.
Vogl 1976
in the case of ‘polarization scattering’, and in the sum of (3.15) and (3.16) for the case of ‘piezo-acoustic scattering’. In the general case,
f^{m}
may have an anisotropic dependence on
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
, but this does not affect the power exponent of the divergence. In the case of optical phonons, the divergence is cut off by a minimal
\delta k_{cut}
which is determined by the minimal optical phonon energy
\hbar\Omega_{o}
; because
\hbar\Omega_{o}
is much smaller than typical band widths,
\delta k_{cut}
is much smaller the Brillouin-zone period. The author of Ref.
Vogl 1976
, P. Vogl, dropped the factor
\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
from all their long-wave-length expressions for the electron-phonon matrix element [including (3.12), (3.15) and (3.16)], based on the fallacious belief that
\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=1+O(\delta k^{2})
can be chosen as a gauge choice for the wave function. The error in this belief is explained in App.
J
.

(With the possible exception of small-gap semiconductors, the polarization and piezo-acoustic scatterings typically dominate
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
over the deformation scattering.
Bardeen and Shockley 1950
) Let us substitute
V^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
2
) with its asymptotically dominant contribution. Since the symmetrized derivative of any function of
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
vanishes, we are led to evaluate

(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\arg\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}(\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k})+O(\delta k^{3}).
Subsequently applying the identities
(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\times\boldsymbol{A})\times\delta\boldsymbol{k}=(\delta\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}})\boldsymbol{A}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}(\boldsymbol{A}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k})
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=(\delta\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}})\boldsymbol{A}+O(\delta k^{3})
, one obtains the anomalous shift in Eq. (
5
).
9
9

9

App.
I
describes an alternative derivation of the anomalous shift by identifying
-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k})+\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
as a line integral of the Berry connection over an infinitesimally thin parallelogram centered at
\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}
. This somewhat demystifies the appearance of the Berry curvature. Just as the intraband anomalous shift vector is expressible in terms of geometric quantities over an intraband loop in momentum space, so is the excitation shift vector (generalized to non-vertical transitions) expressible in terms of geometric quantities over an interband loop
Shi et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2022
.

The anomalous shift induces a large
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
if the excitation surface encloses a time-reversal-symmetric distribution of Berry curvature. The minimal model to demonstrate this effect is quasi-two-dimensional, meaning that the band energies and cell-periodic wave functions
|\,u_{B}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
are approximately independent of one component of
\boldsymbol{k}
, say,
k_{z}
. Let us consider a quasi-2D excitation surface that encircles a
2\pi
-quantum of Berry flux (
2\pi=\int\int\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}dk_{x}dk_{y}
) in the positive-
k_{x}
half plane [yellow region in Fig.
4
(d)]; by time-reversal symmetry, the same excitation surface must encircle a
(-2\pi)
-quantum of Berry flux in the negative-
k_{x}
half plane [cyan region in Fig.
4
(d)]. In short, we simply say that the excitation surface encloses a
time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux
of
2\pi
.
10
10

10

In the quasi-2D context, the time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux is defined as
\int\int\Omega_{c}^{z}\Theta(k_{x}>0)dk_{x}dk_{y}
, with
\Theta(k_{x}>0)
a projector to positive values of
k_{x}.

How do
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}},\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
compare in this minimal model? Before getting too quantitative, one may gain some qualitative insight from comparatively evaluating shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
, shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
and shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
for the representative electron-hole trajectory in Fig.
4
(a), which describes the photoexcitation of an electron-hole pair at
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
on the excitation surface, the relaxation of the excited electron (hole) in the conduction (resp. valence) band, and recombination at
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
. Viewing a forward-moving hole as a backward-moving electron, this electron-hole trajectory becomes an oriented electron loop, which we denote as
loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]
. This loop concatenates two interband links with two intraband pathways,
p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
and
p_{v}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
, which correspond respectively to the conduction and valence band; cf. Fig.
4
(b). The net shift associated to this loop, which we call the
shift loop

\equiv\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}
, is the summation of shift vectors over all one-electron transitions that make up the loop:
11
11

11

A general definition of the shift loop is given in Eq. (
171
) which applies beyond direct-gap semiconductors.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}=&\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra},\boldsymbol{k}}{\\
}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{\text{exc},{\boldsymbol{k}}}=\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}},{\\
}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}=\;\frac{\int d\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}\sum_{p=1}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}}{\int d\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}\sum_{p=1}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}},{\\
}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra},\boldsymbol{k}}=\;\int_{p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k})}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times d\boldsymbol{k}+\int_{p_{v}(\boldsymbol{k})}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{v}\times d\boldsymbol{k}.}

(6)

The first line [Eq. (
6
)] represents the threefold decomposition of the shift loop into its excitation, recombination and intraband components. Assuming the light source is linearly polarized with polarization vector
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
, the shift loop depends on
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
through the excitation-induced
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{exc}}
; cf. Eq. (
6
) with Eq. (
3
). The recombination shift
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
[Eq. (
6
)] is an average of the photonic shift vector over all possible modes of the spontaneously-emitted photon: that is to say, fixing the photon energy by
\hbar c|\boldsymbol{q}|=E_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
, one averages over all directions for
\boldsymbol{q}
(parametrized by the solid angle
\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}
) and over all transverse polarizations
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}
;
this average is weighted by the transition rate, which is proportional to the square of the interband Berry connection by the golden rule; cf. Eq. (
28
). Finally, we have taken the liberty of approximating the summation (over small momentum jumps) as line integrals over
p_{c}
and
p_{v}
.

Let us argue for our minimal model that no symmetry enforces
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra}}=0
. Being quasi-two-dimensional implies that only the z component of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c/v,\boldsymbol{k}}
is nonzero. As illustrated in Fig.
4
(d), both relaxation pathways (
p_{c}
and
p_{v}
) lie in the
\boldsymbol{k}
-region with positive Berry curvature (
\Omega^{z}_{c}
) for the conduction-band states. In the two-band approximation, the Berry curvature of conduction- and valence-band states sum to zero:
\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-\Omega^{z}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
,
Alexandradinata et al. 2014
but this does not imply a cancellation in Eq. (
6
), because
p_{c}
and
p_{v}
are oppositely oriented. There is also no cancellation with the time-reversed loop, which is indicated by
T\circ p_{c}
and
T\circ p_{v}
in Fig.
4
(e): under time reversal,
\Omega^{z}_{c}\rightarrow-\Omega^{z}_{c}
, but the orientation of
T\circ p_{c}
is opposite to that of
p_{c}
. In the absence of symmetry-enforced cancellations, one expects that an anomalous shift current is a generic consequence of enclosed, time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux. This does not violate any symmetry principle, because optical excitation creates a non-equilibrium state with an arrow of time; this arrow manifests in the orientation of our loops. Our argument for the anomalous shift is widely generalizable: one may imagine a greater variety of enclosed time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux for which the line integrals in Eq. (
6
) are nonvanishing, and such imagination need not be restricted to semiconductors.

Figure 4:
Viewing a closed electron-hole trajectory [panel (a)] as an electron loop [panel (b)]. (c) An electron loop with a nonunique relaxation pathway. (d) Top-down view of the same electron loop in panel (b). The excitation surface encloses a
2\pi
flux of Berry curvature (
\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{z}
) in the yellow region, and a negative
2\pi
flux in the cyan region. (e) The time-reversed counterpart to the loop in panel (d). (f) Approximating the relaxation pathways as geodesic paths.

Moving beyond qualitative arguments, we would like to quantify the current for our quasi-2D semiconducting model. However, a brief diversion is required to explain the calculational method we invented. Our method introduces the
affinity shift loop
as a figure of merit for the shift current:

\displaystyle{ASL_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}\;=\big\langle\;f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}\;\big\rangle_{\omega};\;\;\;\;f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}},}

(7)

with
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\omega}
meaning to average over all
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface:

\displaystyle{\langle\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})\rangle_{\omega}=&\;\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\frac{\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)}{JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}}\;\Xi(\boldsymbol{k});\;\;\;\;E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(8)

JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}
stands for the joint density of states for quasiparticles of one spin orientation:

\displaystyle{JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}=&\;\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega).}

(9)

What is being averaged in Eq. (
7
) is the shift loop weighted by the rate of optical excitations, with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
the polarization vector of the light source; by Fermi’s golden rule, this rate is proportional to
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}
, which we will refer to as the
optical affinity
. The shift loop is defined in Eqs. (
6
)-(
6
), with
p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
(resp.
p_{v}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
) chosen to be the unique oriented path that (i) connects
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
), and (ii) is tangential to
\boldsymbol{v}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{v}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
) at all points along the path. We refer to
p_{b}
(with
b=c
or
v
) as a
geodesic path
.
12
12

12

Construct the four-momentum
(P^{0},P^{1},P^{2},P^{3})=(E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}/||\boldsymbol{v}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}||,\hbar k_{x},\hbar k_{y},\hbar k_{z})
and introduce the Lorentzian metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, with
g_{11}=g_{22}=g_{33}=-g_{00}=-1
. Then
p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
can be viewed as a path
P^{\mu}(\lambda)
that minimizes the action
\int_{0}^{1}\surd[-g_{\mu\nu}\tfrac{dP^{\mu}}{d\lambda}\tfrac{dP^{\nu}}{d\lambda}]d\lambda
, given that the end points of the path are fixed to
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
.
If
E_{B}
is an isotropic function of
\boldsymbol{k}
, then the geodesic path is simply the Euclidean-straight path connecting
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
; cf. Fig.
4
(f). The motivation for geodesic paths is that the predominant relaxation pathways [Fig.
4
(d)] do not deviate far from being geodesic [Fig.
4
(f)]: each time a quasiparticle in the conduction band emits an optical phonon, the likeliest transition involves the smallest wavenumber change
\delta k
[Fig.
4
(a)], since the electron-phonon matrix element diverges as
1/\delta k^{2}
;
Vogl 1976
minimizing
\delta k=||\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}||
with the constraint
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\hbar\Omega_{o}
is approximately equivalent to
\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
being parallel to
\boldsymbol{v}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
, given that the optical phonon energy
\hbar\Omega_{o}
is small compared to typical band widths.

Defining the
shift conductivity
through
\boldsymbol{j}[f]=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\epsilon_{s},\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}
, with
\boldsymbol{j}[f]
the steady shift current given by the BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)], and [
\boldsymbol{{\cal E}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}{\cal E}_{\omega}e^{i(\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-i\omega t)}+
complex conjugate] being the incident electric wave, the shift conductivity relates to our figure of merit through

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}\approx-1.53mAV^{-2}\;\frac{ASL_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}}{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\;\frac{2JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}}{({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}eV)^{-1}},}

(10)

with
1.53mAV^{-2}=2\pi|e|^{2}/\hbar V
in SI units, and
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
being the volume of the primitive unit cell.
13
13

13

Eq. (
10
) applies to direct-gap semiconductors in which a single conduction and a single valence band are optically excited in the vicinity of a single extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
.
In direct-gap semiconductors with multiple valleys/pockets (indexed by
v
), such as transition metal dichalcogenides, the total shift conductivity is proportional to
\sum_{v}ASL_{v}JDOS_{v}
.
Because the shift loop is threefold decomposable according to Eq. (
6
), one may likewise decompose

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega},}

(11)

with
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}
matching the Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shkrebtii formula
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
from Kubo-type perturbation theory; cf. App.
C.3
.

Our relation between the shift conductivity and the affinity shift loop [Eq. (
10
)] holds at low temperature (
k_{B}T_{l}\ll\hbar\Omega_{o},E_{g}
) and for small optical phonon energy (compared to the band gap and the largest energy of photo-excited carriers).
14
14

14

The largest energy of photo-excited electrons is represented as
E_{\text{exc}}-E_{c,\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
in Fig.
3
(c).
The right-hand side of Eq. (
10
) should be understood as an approximation to the BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)]; the major error in this approximation originates from fixing
p_{b}
to be a geodesic path, hence we refer to Eq. (
10
) as the
geodesic approximation
to the shift conductivity. In reality, an electron excited at
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
follows multiple relaxation pathways [as caricatured in Fig.
4
(c)] which deviate from being geodesic and narrow. The geodesic approximation is therefore justified to the extent that small-angle scattering dominates over large-angle scattering.
15
15

15

The BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)] reduces asymptotically to the geodesic approximation [Eq. (
10
)] in the limit of vanishing scattering angle, as proven in App.
D.5
.
A benchmarking of the approximation will shortly be presented.

While other groups have attempted to optimize the shift conductivity by maximizing the JDOS,
Cook et al. 2017
we adopt a wave-function-centric approach in maximizing the affinity shift loop. Assuming a generic value for
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}eV)^{-1}
,
||ASL_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}||\sim{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
implies a conductivity of order
mAV^{-2}
.

Returning to our quasi-2D model, we now demonstrate that the intraband component [cf. Eq. (
6
)] of the affinity shift loop is indeed comparable in magnitude to
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
:

\displaystyle{\bigg|\bigg|\;\big\langle(f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra},\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\omega}\bigg|\bigg|\sim{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}.}

(12)

We adopt two heuristic approximations for a back-of-the-envelop calculation, namely that (i) the excitation surface is circular with radius
k_{r}=\pi/2a
(assuming
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=a^{3}
), and that (ii) in the absence of unusually small band gaps,
16
16

16

The Berry curvature only exhibits significant variations over a length scale that is comparable to the inverse of the band gap.
the
2\pi
Berry flux is roughly homogeneous over the yellow semicircle enclosed by the excitation surface; cf. Fig.
4
(d). Because
\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-\Omega^{z}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
(in the two-band approximation) and
p_{c}=-p_{v}
(presuming an electron-hole symmetry
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
), the anomalous contribution to the shift loop simplifies to
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra},\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}=2\int_{p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})}\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\vec{z}\times d\boldsymbol{k}
, with
p_{c}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
a straight path connecting
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}=(0,0,0)
.
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra}}(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})=-16a\vec{y}/\pi
if
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}=(k_{r},0,0)
and vanishes if
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}=(0,k_{r},0)
; from this one deduces that the average of
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra},\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}
over all
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
on the excitation surface is comparable to
-a\vec{y}
.
Assuming that
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}\approx 1
[cf. Sec.
II
], and that the interband connection is generic-valued:
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}|^{2}\sim a^{2}
, one finds
\langle|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{intra}}\rangle_{\omega}\sim-{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}\vec{y}
, leading to Eq. (
12
).

It is of interest to demonstrate that our crudely-derived conclusion holds true in a precise calculation for a model Hamiltonian:
17
17

17

This
\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}
model can be extended to a tight-binding model by recognizing certain terms as Taylor-series coefficients for trigonometric functions.

\displaystyle{H(\boldsymbol{k})=-E_{o}(z^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}z)\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\;\;\;z=\begin{pmatrix}{\tilde{k}_{x}-\tilde{k}_{x}^{3}/6}\\
{\tilde{k}_{y}+i(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{k}_{x}^{2}-\tilde{k}_{y}^{2})/2}\end{pmatrix},\;\;\;\;{E_{o}}=\frac{1}{\tilde{P}}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{{m_{f}}a^{2}}.}

(13)

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{3})
is the vector of Pauli matrices;
\tilde{k}_{j}=k_{j}a
(for
j=x,y,z
) is a dimensionless wavenumber, with
a=5\mbox{\AA}
a generic value for the lattice period;
m_{f}
is the free-electron mass;
\tilde{P}
and
\tilde{Q}
are dimensionless Hamiltonian parameters. Assuming that
\tilde{Q}<2
, Eq. (
13
) is the Hamiltonian of a direct-gap, quasi-2D semiconductor with band gap
E_{g}=E_{o}\tilde{Q}^{2}/2
, effective masses
m_{x}=m_{y}=\tilde{P}m_{f}/(2-\tilde{Q})
,
m_{z}=\infty
(for both electrons and holes), and
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx\tilde{P}/(8-4\tilde{Q})
in units of
({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}eV)^{-1}
. We choose
\tilde{Q}=1
and
\tilde{P}=4
, such that
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx 1
is generic-valued.

The model Hamiltonian has been chosen because it realizes a time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux of
2\pi=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Omega_{c}^{z}dk_{x}dk_{y}
,
Alexandradinata et al. 2021
;
Nelson et al. 2021
;
Nelson et al. 2022
as illustrated in Fig.
5
(a). The time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux enclosed by the excitation surface varies from roughly
0.7(2\pi)
[for

\hbar\omega=2.4E_{0}
] to
0.2(2\pi)
[for

\hbar\omega=0.8E_{0}
]; cf. Fig.
5
(b). The case of
\hbar\omega=2.4E_{0}
is not unlike the caricature we drew in Fig.
4
(d).

Figure 5:
Characterization of a quasi-2D model [Eq. (
13
) with
\tilde{Q}=1
] with a time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux. Panel (a) plots the Berry curvature as a colored background, with a color legend (on the right) specifying the value of
\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{z}/({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}})^{2/3}
; the black ellipses represent excitation surfaces for two source photon energies:
\hbar\omega=2.4E_{0}
and
0.8E_{0}
, with
E_{o}\approx 76meV
. Panel (b) shows the
\omega
-dependence of the time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux enclosed by the excitation surface. The black curve in panel (c) represents the polarization-averaged affinity shift loop [
(1/2)\sum_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\in\{\vec{x},\vec{y}\}}ASL^{y}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}/{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
] vs
\omega
; the non-black curves represent the three-fold decomposition of the polarization-averaged affinity shift loop: excitation (red), intraband relaxation (green), and recombination (blue). The shift conductivity
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
(and its threefold decomposition) is calculated in the geodesic approximation in panel (d), and in the iso-energy symmetric approximation in panel (e).

The quasi-two-dimensionality and reflection symmetry (
x\rightarrow-x
) of our model imply that only the y-component of the shift current can be nontrivial. We have numerically computed the y-component of the affinity shift loop via Eqs. (
6
)-(
7
) for the chosen model parameters.
18
18

18

The computation was simplified by setting
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=1
in Eq. (
7
), for reasons explained at the end of Sec.
II
. We have also approximated the geodesic paths as straight [cf. Fig.
4
(f)], since the energy-momentum dispersion is roughly isotropic.

Fig.
5
(c) plots the polarization-averaged affinity shift loop
[(1/2)\sum_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\in\{\vec{x},\vec{y}\}}ASL^{y}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}]
with respect to
\hbar\omega
in the interval
[0.8E_{0},2.4E_{0}]
; the threefold decomposition of

(1/2)\sum_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}ASL^{y}
is also illustrated.
For a broad range of frequencies (
\hbar\omega>1.3E_{0}
) where the encircled time-reversal-symmetric Berry flux exceeds
0.4(2\pi)
, the intraband component not only exceeds the excitation component by an order of magnitude, but also carries an opposite sign. These values for the affinity shift loop translates [via Eq. (
10
)] to a shift conductivity
\approx 4mAV^{-2}
for an unpolarized light source, as illustrated in Fig.
2
(b) for the same frequency range.

To benchmark the geodesic approximation that has been used in all conductivity calculations thus far, we also computed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
via the more traditional method of numerically simulating a steady quasiparticle distribution
f_{B}
that sets the collisional integral to zero, and then inputting
f_{B}
into Eq. (
1
); the detailed procedure is described in App.
C.2
. Because this procedure is numerically intensive, we resorted to approximating
f_{B}
as an
iso-energy symmetric
function of
\boldsymbol{k}
, meaning
f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
is constant over iso-energy
\boldsymbol{k}
-surfaces of
E_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
. Fig.
5
(d) and (e) show the same quantity
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
calculated in the geodesic and iso-energy symmetric approximations, respectively. It is reassuring to see semi-quantitative consistency in the values of the shift conductivity and all its components, especially at lower photon frequencies where the iso-energy symmetric approximation is better justified.
19
19

19

The
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent transition rate for optical excitation [cf. Eq. (
28
)] becomes increasingly iso-energy asymmetric at higher frequencies: the standard deviation of
|A^{x}_{cv}|^{2}
(over the excitation surface at frequency
\omega
) increases from 8.6 percent (of
\langle|A^{x}_{cv}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}
) at
\hbar\omega=0.8E_{0}
to 23.8 percent at
\hbar\omega=1.5E_{0}
.

IV
Optical vorticity

Having demonstrated the dominance of the intraband current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
in the presence of time-reversal-symmetric
intra
band Berry curvature, this section will demonstrate the dominance of the recombination-induced current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
in the presence of optical vortices – topological singularities in the
inter
band Berry phase.

Before discussing vortices properly, we first consider a vortex-less scenario where the
photon-mediated current components cancel out:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}\approx 0
. In understanding how this cancellation happens, it will become apparent that vorticity is one route to prevent such a cancellation. Let us then hypothesize a scenario where the photonic shift vector is roughly independent of the light polarization, and roughly homogeneous in the
\boldsymbol{k}
-region enclosed by the excitation surface. This would imply that the shift vector at excitation [Eq. (
6
)] is opposite to the shift vector at recombination [Eq. (
6
)], leading to a cancellation of the excitation- and recombination-induced currents; cf. Eqs. (
7
)-(
11
).

The contrapositive implication of this thought experiment is that for
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
to be significant, the photonic shift at excitation must differ from the photonic shift at recombination. With great circulation comes great differences! The circulation of the photonic shift vector is defined by integrating Eq. (
3
) over a loop
\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
in
\boldsymbol{k}
-space:

\displaystyle{\oint_{\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\frac{d\boldsymbol{k}}{2\pi}=-\oint_{\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}]\cdot\frac{d\boldsymbol{k}}{2\pi}+\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\big(\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\,\big)\cdot\frac{d^{2}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{2\pi}.}

(14)

The last term is derived by Stokes’ theorem, and corresponds to a generically nonquantized
20
20

20

With the inclusion of crystallographic symmetry and for a symmetric choice of
\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, it is possible for the Berry-flux term to be integer-quantized, which makes the circulation of the shift vector a topological invariant.
Alexandradinata 2022

circulation associated to the intraband Berry phase, as illustrated in Fig.
6
(a) for our model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
), with
\tilde{Q}=1
. Beyond our model Hamiltonian, the possibility exists for an integer-quantized circulation stemming from the preceding term which involves the interband Berry connection.
Alexandradinata 2022
;
Fregoso et al. 2017
One is led to consider an optical vortex – a line in three-dimensional
\boldsymbol{k}
-space where (i)
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=0
, and (ii) the argument/phase of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
winds nontrivially as
\boldsymbol{k}
is varied along any infinitesimal loop linked to the vortex line, as representatively illustrated by the black curve in Fig.
6
(b). It is worth distinguishing between
\vec{x}
-vortices (where
A_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{x}=0
) and
\vec{y}
-vortices (where
A_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}=0
). Because
A_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{x}=0
does not generally imply
A_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}=0
, one type of vortex may occur independently of the other.

Figure 6:
(a) Circulation of the photonic shift vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
associated to the intraband Berry curvature for our model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
), with
\tilde{Q}=1
. The curvature is represented with the color legend in Fig.
5
(a). (b) The magenta line represents a singularity in the interband Berry phase, i.e., an optical vortex. (c) Optical vortices appear as points (represented by magenta crosses) in a 2D closed
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold parametrized by
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

Consider the shift current in a quasi-2D model where
\vec{y}
-vortices are absent [Fig.
7
(b)], but the excitation surface encircles a pair of
\vec{x}
-vortices which are mutually related by time reversal and mirror reflection [Fig.
7
(a)].
21
21

21

That optical vortices come in time-reversed pairs was proven in Ref.
Alexandradinata 2022
.
Fig.
7
is derived from the model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
) with a different set of parameters:
\tilde{Q}=-1
and
\tilde{P}=12
, but fixed
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx(eV{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}})^{-1}
.

Figure 7:
Characterization of a quasi-2D model with a pair of
\vec{x}
-vortices, whose
\boldsymbol{k}
-locations are indicated by pink dots in panels (a,c,e). Panels (a) and (b) depict the photonic shift vector field
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
respectively. In panels (c) and (d), the red ellipse represents the same excitation surface; the arrows on the ellipse represent the vectors
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface. In panels (e) and (f), the size of the red dots indicates the magnitude of
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}
for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface; the jagged blue line represents a predominant relaxation pathway from the excitation surface to the extremal wavevector:
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Just as for the model studied in Sec.
III
, only the y-component of the shift conductivity (
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
) is symmetry-allowed to be nonzero.

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
for an
\vec{x}
-polarized light source is calculated via Eqs. (
6
)-(
10
), and is shown in Fig.
2
(d) to be dominated by its recombination component. The same figure shows the difference between
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}
to be of order
mAV^{-2}
. This represents an extraordinary sensitivity of the shift current to the source polarization, and motivates
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x},\omega}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{y},\omega}
) as an experimental indicator of
\vec{x}
-vorticity. More generally, for a quantity
B(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s})
that depends on the source polarization, we refer to
B(\vec{x})\neq B(\vec{y})
as a
linear disparity
in
B
.
22
22

22

In contrast, linear birefringence results from a linear disparity in the first-order-in-
{\cal E}_{\omega}
conductivity, which is associated to an alternating current.
The remainder of this section aims to demystify the large linear disparity in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}
, and the dominance of the recombination-induced current when
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
.

Let us first understand the linear disparity in the excitation component of the conductivity; cf. Eq. (
11
). Recall that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}^{\text{exc}}
is proportional to a weighted average of the photonic shift vector over the excitation surface, which we have denoted as
\langle\,|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\omega}
; cf. Eqs. (
6
)-(
11
). A major effect of
\vec{x}
-vorticity without
\vec{y}
-vorticity is that the orientation of
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{x}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
, viewed as a vector field over
\boldsymbol{k}
-space, tends to be more disordered than the vector field
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{y}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{y}}_{c\leftarrow v}
.
23
23

23

The vector norm of
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{x}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
is not singular: as
\boldsymbol{k}
approaches the vortex center, the quantized circulation implies
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow\infty
, but this divergence is compensated by
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{x}|^{2}\rightarrow 0
.
Alexandradinata 2022

In particular, along the excitation surface, the orientation of
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{x}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
exhibits rotations which are more pronounced than that of
|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{y}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{y}}_{c\leftarrow v}
, as comparatively illustrated in Fig.
7
(c-d). Ceteris paribus, the average of a rotational vector field is smaller than the average of an irrotational vector field, hence
||\langle|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{x}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}\rangle_{\omega}||<||\langle|\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}^{y}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{y}}_{c\leftarrow v}\rangle_{\omega}||
and
|\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{x},\omega}|<|\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{y},\omega}|
.

A different argument is needed to understand the linear disparity of the intraband conductivity:
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra},y}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}
in Eq. (
11
). For an
\vec{x}
-polarized source, the photon-mediated transition rate depends anisotropically on the orientation of
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
; in particular,
|A_{cv}^{x}|^{2}
is suppressed on segments of the excitation surface that are closer to the vortex, where
A_{cv}^{x}=0
; cf. Fig.
7
(e). For a
\vec{y}
-polarized source, the photon-mediated transition rate is also anisotropic but in the opposite sense:
|A_{cv}^{y}|^{2}
is suppressed near the mirror-invariant line (
k_{x}=0
), where
A_{cv}^{y}=0
by a dipole selection rule; cf. Fig.
7
(f).
24
24

24

The conduction-band states transform in a different representation of mirror than the valence-band states, as detailed in App.
F
.
The opposite senses of anisotropy imply that the predominant intraband relaxation pathways are roughly parallel to the
k_{y}
axis for an
\vec{x}
-polarized source [Fig.
7
(e)], and parallel to the
k_{x}
axis for a
\vec{y}
-polarized source [Fig.
7
(f)]. Ceteris paribus, this implies a larger
\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{y},\omega}
for a
\vec{y}
-polarized source, because the y-component of the anomalous shift [Eq. (
5
)] is proportional to the x-component of the momentum transfer:
\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}=\Omega^{z}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\delta k^{x}
.

A final argument explains the dominance of the recombination-induced current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
over the excitation-induced current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
, for an
\vec{x}
-polarized source. Unlike
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
is insensitive to the vortex-induced disordering in the orientation of
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
. To understand why, recall that the majority of recombination transitions occur at
\boldsymbol{k}
points close to the extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
and contained within the passive region; cf. Sec.
II
and Eq. (
50
). Each recombination transition is associated with a photonic shift
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}
, which may as well be approximated as
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
, because the passive region typically occupies a tiny fraction of the Brillouin-zone volume. For the same reason, it is presumed that the optical vortex does not intersect the passive region. It follows that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
depends on
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
but not on the vortex-induced disorder in
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
.
To wrap up the argument, the vortex-induced orientational disorder in
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
diminishes
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{x},\omega}
but not
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{x},\omega}
; ceteris paribus,
||\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{x},\omega}||>||\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{x},\omega}||.
This explains how ‘great differences’ (between the excitation and recombination shifts) result from a ‘great circulation’ (induced by a vortex).

To recapitulate, we have qualitatively argued that
\vec{x}
-vorticity leads to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{x},\omega}
dominating over
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{x},\omega}
, as well as brings about a linear disparity of both
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
.
25
25

25

Vorticity also results in a linear disparity of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
due to the absorption coefficient being proportional to
\langle|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}
, but this is not a large effect in our model.
These arguments are quantitatively supported by model calculations detailed in App.
F
; here, we will just summarize the salient conclusions:
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x},\omega}
is dominated by the recombination-induced current;
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{y},\omega}
is dominated by the excitation-induced and intraband currents; the signs of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}
differ over a broad range of frequencies; the linear disparity in the conductivity is large:
|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}|\sim mAV^{-2}
[cf. Fig.
2
(d)], assuming a generic value for the JDOS; the current response to unpolarized light is slightly smaller:
|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}|/2\sim 0.1mAV^{-2}
; all these results hold without finetuning of the incident radiation frequency.

To find optical vortices in model Hamiltonians and realistic materials, let us develop the close relationship between vorticity and Berry curvature that has been suggested by
Eq. (
14
): for any closed 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
(which can be a two-toroidal or two-spherical cut of the 3D Brillouin zone), we establish a general theorem relating the Chern numbers (
C_{v},C_{c}
) of the valence and conduction bands to the net optical vorticity
(Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})
:

\displaystyle{\text{Chern-vorticity theorem:}\;\;\;\;C_{c}-C_{v}=Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\sum_{\text{vortex}}\oint_{\partial\text{vortex}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}]\cdot\frac{d\boldsymbol{k}}{2\pi}.}

(15)

Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
is the net circulation of the interband Berry phase over all
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
-vortex points in
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and
\partial\text{vortex}
is an infinitesimal loop surrounding each
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
-vortex point as illustrated in Fig.
6
(c).
26
26

26

An equivalent and manifestly gauge-invariant expression is
Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\sum_{vortex}\oint_{\partial\text{vortex}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot{d\boldsymbol{k}}/{2\pi}
, with the photonic shift vector defined in Eq. (
3
). This expression differs from Eq. (
15
) only in the line integral of
\boldsymbol{A}_{cc}-\boldsymbol{A}_{vv}
over
\partial vortex
; this integral vanishes because
\partial vortex
is an infinitesimal loop and
\boldsymbol{A}_{cc}-\boldsymbol{A}_{vv}
is smoothly defined at the vortex point; cf. Fig.
17
in App.
G
.
This theorem is derived by setting
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
to be a closed manifold in Eq. (
14
), such that the area integral of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}
simplifies to the Chern number
C_{c}
of the conduction band, and that of
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{v}
to
C_{v}
; the line integral of the shift vector over
\partial\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
vanishes, but the line integral of the interband Berry phase is contributed by the circulation around each vortex point, as elaborated in App.
G
. The next section employs the Chern-vorticity theorem to identify vortices in
\rm{BiTeI}
.

V
Case study of
\rm{BiTeI}

To demonstrate the effects of the anomalous shift and optical vorticity in a realistic material, we present a case study of
\rm{BiTeI}
, a 3D polar, layered semiconductor with P3m1 space group symmetry
Shevelkov et al. 1995
;
Ishizaka et al. 2011
. The large atomic number of
\rm{Bi}
correlates with a large Rashba-type spin-orbit coupling
Ishizaka et al. 2011
and the promixity of
\rm{BiTeI}
to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological insulator
Das and Balatsky 2013
. A previous study
Tan and Rappe 2016
of
\rm{BiTeI}
by Tan and Rappe exhibited the enhancement of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}}
for
\vec{z}
parallel to the polar axis, assuming the band gap
|E_{g}|
were made small (by hydrostatic pressure), and further assuming the photon frequency were finetuned to be comparably small:
\omega\approx|E_{g}|/\hbar
. This enhancement of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}}
originates from the divergence of the band-edge optical affinity at the phase transition (
E_{g}=0
) between the trivial and topological insulator; across this transition,
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}}
changes sign.

Our case study demonstrates that:

(i) The just-mentioned topological phase transition guarantees the existence of large Berry curvature [cf. Fig.
8
(a)]
and optical vortices [Fig.
8
(b)], as per the Chern-vorticity theorem in Eq. (
15
).

(ii) For photon frequencies such that the excitation surface is close to the optical vortex, the phonon-mediated
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
dominates over the photon-mediated
\vec{z}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})
[Fig.
8
(c)], owing to the excitation surface enclosing a larger volume of one-quasiparticle Bloch states with nontrivial the Berry curvature.

(iii) Conversely for smaller frequencies (
\omega\approx|E_{g}|/\hbar
) such that the excitation surface encloses a negligible amount of Berry curvature, it is
\vec{z}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})
which dominates over
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
.
The net current is nonvanishing despite
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
and
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
opposing each other [Fig.
8
(d)], owing to an asymmetry of the photon polarizations in the excitation and recombination processes.

(iv) The recombination shift strongly depends on the symmetry of the Hamiltonian at the wavevectors of recombination. For BiTeI, a chiral symmetry reduces
|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{rec}|
to about a third of the lattice period, which makes the recombination shift current smaller than the other two components.

(v) Because the
\boldsymbol{k}
-locations of optical vortices depend on the light polarization [Fig.
8
(b)], we find that
\vec{x}
-vortices suppress the anomalous shift more effectively than would
\vec{z}
-vortices, resulting in a linear disparity of the shift conductivity (
|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{z}}-\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x}}|\sim 0.1mA/V^{2}
) at higher frequencies.

(vi) If the topological phase transition is induced by tuning the band gap
|E_{g}|
to zero at a fixed photon frequency, the discrete change in wave function topology manifests as a sign change of the steady shift current
\boldsymbol{j}[f]
. The reason for this sign change is that
\boldsymbol{j}[f]
is dominated by the phonon-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
which changes sign [Fig.
8
(e)]; the previously-calculated sign change of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
(by Tan and Rappe
Tan and Rappe 2016
) is irrelevant. The sign change of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
is concomitant with a divergence of the band-edge intraband Berry curvature at the phase transition, which results in an approximate
(1/E_{g})
-divergence of the low-temperature
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
[Fig.
8
(f)], with negative (resp. positive)
E_{g}
referring to a semiconductor (with band gap
|E_{g}|
) on the topologically trivial (resp. nontrivial) side of the phase transition. The
(1/E_{g})
-divergence of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
is cut off when
E_{g}
becomes comparable to the thermal energy (
k_{B}T_{l}
) or to an energy scale representing trigonal warping; the latter scale is estimated
Tan and Rappe 2016
to be about
10meV
.

It may be seen from (ii-iii) that the winner in the competition (between shift
{}_{\text{intra}}
, shift
{}_{\text{rec}}
and shift
{}_{\text{exc}}
) depends sensitively on the photon frequency, to the extent that the net shift conductivity changes sign in the transition from a photon-dominated shift current (at low frequency) to a phonon-dominated shift current (at high frequency); cf. black curve in Fig.
8
(c-d). This exemplifies a general principle: because band wave functions can strong depend on energy, so can the shift current sensitively depend on the photon frequency. Point (v) exemplifies a general principle that optical vorticity makes the shift current sensitive to changes in light polarization. Point (vi) suggests the bulk photovoltaic effect can provide smoking-gun evidence of the
topological phase transition in BiTeI. Such evidence is presently lacking: though it has been alleged that BiTeI is pressure-tunable to a phase transition, the experimental corroborations of this allegation (namely, a mininum of the resistivity
Qi et al. 2017
or variations of the quantum oscillation frequency
Ideue et al. 2014
) cannot be directly interpreted as a change in wave function topology.

Figure 8:
Panel (a) shows the Berry curvature vector field in the
k_{z}=0
plane, for the model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
16
) with Hamiltonian parameter
m^{\prime}=m-A\lambda^{2}=\pm 0.044
eV. Panel (b) plots the photonic shift vector fields (
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{z}}_{c\leftarrow v}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\leftarrow v}
) in the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane for
m^{\prime}=-0.044
. The shift vectors circulate around optical
\vec{z}
-vortices (
\vec{x}
-vortices), which are represented by magenta (navy) crosses. (c,d) For
m^{\prime}=-0.044
eV, we plot the shift conductivities (
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{z}}
and
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x}}
) and their threefold decomposition. Panel (e) plots the
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{z}}
and its intraband component versus
E_{g}
. Panel (f) plots
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{z}}
against
1/E_{g}
, with negative
E_{g}
corresponding to the trivial side of the topological phase transition.

To substantiate our results, we employ an effective Hamiltonian for the four low-energy, spin-split bands of
\rm{BiTeI}
:

\displaystyle{H_{\text{BiTeI}}=&\;\hbar v\big(\;\lambda\tau_{1}\sigma_{3}-\tau_{2}\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\;\big)+M\tau_{3}\sigma_{0};\;\;\;\;M=(m-Ak^{2});\;\;\;\;k^{2}=k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2},}

(16)

with
\tau_{1,2,3}
and
\sigma_{1,2,3}
being Pauli matrices for the orbital and spin degrees of freedom respectively;
\tau_{0}
and
\sigma_{0}
are identity matrices, and
A=0.5eV\mathring{A}^{2},\ \hbar v=0.7eV\mathring{A},\ \lambda=0.25/(\hbar v)=0.357\mathring{A}^{-1}
are ab-initio-derived
Tan and Rappe 2016
parameters. The spectrum of this model is given by

E=\pm\sqrt{M^{2}+(\hbar v)^{2}\big[\;k_{z}^{2}+(k_{\perp}\pm\lambda)^{2}\;\big]};\;\;\;\;k_{\perp}\equiv\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}},

(17)

with each choice of either
\pm
determining four energy levels:
E_{1}\leq E_{2}<E_{3}\leq E_{4}
as illustrated in Fig.
9
, with corresponding eigenstates:
|\,u_{1\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
,
|\,u_{2\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
,
|\,u_{3\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
, and
|\,u_{4\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
. When
m
is tuned to the critical value
m_{c}=A\lambda^{2}
(possibly by hydrostatic pressure
Tan and Rappe 2016
), the band gap vanishes with a concomitant energy degeneracy (
E_{2}=E_{3}
) along a loop defined by
k_{\perp}=\lambda
and
k_{z}=0
. The circular shape of this loop reflects the O(2) rotational symmetry of the Hamiltonian.
27
27

27

The SO(2) rotational symmetry of the Hamiltonian manifests as
\hat{R}_{\theta}H_{\text{BiTeI}}(\boldsymbol{k})\hat{R}_{\theta}^{-1}=H_{\text{BiTeI}}(g_{\theta}\boldsymbol{k})
, with
\hat{R}_{\theta}=e^{i\theta\tau_{0}\sigma_{3}}
, and with
g_{\theta}\boldsymbol{k}
obtained from
\boldsymbol{k}
by a rotation of angle
\theta
around the z axis. This SO(2) rotational symmetry is approximate; we neglect a trigonal warping whose energy scale is estimated
Tan and Rappe 2016
to be about
10meV
. Time reversal symmetry is represented by
\hat{T}=i\tau_{3}\sigma_{2}K
, with
K
implementing complex conjugation. The Hamiltonian term proportional to
\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}
breaks both mirror
[z\rightarrow-z;\hat{M}_{z}=i\tau_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}]
and
[(x,y,z)\rightarrow(-x,-y,-z);\hat{P}=\tau_{3}]
parity symmetries.

Figure 9:
On the left: the box is a 3D Brillouin zone containing a toroidal excitation surface (outlined in red), a pair of
\vec{z}-
optical vortex loops (magneta dotted lines) and a pair of
\vec{x}-
optical vortex loops (navy dotted lines); the left-most
E
-vs-
k
plot shows the band structure of
\rm{BiTeI}
with the kinetic processes of excitation (red arrows), intraband relaxation (green) and recombination (blue). On the right: an O(2) rotation symmetry allows us to focus on the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane, which contains a massive Dirac fermion in two momentum dimensions; the cross-section of the toroidal excitation surface is a circle (colored red) in the half-plane; the cross section of vortex loops are vortex points (indicated by magenta and navy crosses). The dark blue and purple arrows represent diametrically opposed geodesic paths for intraband relaxation.

Away from the topological phase transition (
m\neq m_{c}
), the Berry curvature of
|\,u_{2\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
is a circulating vector field illustrated in Fig.
8
(a), with the circulation flipping in orientation when
m^{\prime}=m-m_{c}
changes sign. Because this circulation is
O(2)
