-rotation symmetric, it may be understood by focusing on a single 2D slice of the Brillouin zone, say, the slice with
k_{y}=0
and
k_{x}>0
, which we henceforth call the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane [see Fig.
9
]. This half-plane is intersected by the energy-degenerate loop at
\mathbf{k}_{0}=(\lambda,0,0)
; by Taylor expanding
H_{\text{BiTeI}}
around
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
and projecting onto bands
2
and
3
, we obtain an effective Hamiltonian for a massive Dirac fermion in two momentum dimensions:

H^{\prime}=(m^{\prime}-\hbar v^{\prime}q_{x})\gamma_{3}+\hbar v(q_{x}\gamma_{1}+q_{z}\gamma_{2});\;\;\;\;\hbar v^{\prime}=2A\lambda;\;\;\;\;q_{x}=k_{x}-\lambda,\;q_{z}=k_{z},

(18)

with
\gamma_{1,2,3}
being Pauli matrices of the reduced Hilbert space. Detailed derivations of
H^{\prime}
can be found in App
H
.
It is known that the massive Dirac fermion is characterized by a large Berry curvature
\Omega^{y}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-\Omega^{y}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
, for
\boldsymbol{k}
in a ‘hot spot’ of width
\text{(band gap)}/\hbar v
; assuming this width is small compared to the linear dimension of the Brillouin zone, the Berry flux through the half-plane changes by
2\pi
when
m^{\prime}
changes sign:
\int_{half-plane}\Omega^{y}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}dk_{x}dk_{z}=\pi\text{sgn}{m^{\prime}}

with Taylor L. Hughes 2013
.

This jump of the Berry flux indicates the presence of optical vorticity on at least one side of the phase transition, meaning for either
m^{\prime}>0
or
m^{\prime}<0
. This is because as
m^{\prime}
is tuned from
0^{-}
(negative infinitesimal) to
0^{+}
(positive infinitesimal), the net vorticity
Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
of the half-plane must change discontinuously by
+2
for any
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, according to the Chern-vorticity theorem in Eq. (
15
).
28
28

28

Strictly speaking, the half-plane is not a closed 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold, which precludes a direct application of the Chern-vorticity theorem. However, nearly the same logical considerations apply: the eigenstates
|\,u_{b\mathbf{k}}\,\rangle
continuously depends on
m^{\prime}
except for
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
(the band-touching point), thus when
m^{\prime}
is tuned from
0^{-}
to
0^{+}
, the photonic shift vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
is invariant for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the boundary of the half-plane. This implies that the change in
2\int_{half-plane}\Omega^{y}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}dk_{x}dk_{z}/(2\pi)
equals the change in
Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
, across the topological phase transition.
This topological argument is verified by our numerical calculations: As shown in Fig.
8
(b), for
m^{\prime}<0
, two
\vec{z}
-vortices (
\vec{x}
-vortices) are observed on the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
plane, as highlighted by magenta (navy) crosses. This indicates the presence of a pair of
\vec{z}
-vortex (
\vec{x}
-vortex) loops as illustrated in Fig.
9
when
m^{\prime}<0
.
29
29

29

Due to the SO(2) symmetry,
\vec{y}
-vortex loops are related to
\vec{x}
-vortex loops because
|\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}(R_{4}\boldsymbol{k})|=|\vec{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}(\boldsymbol{k})|
where
R_{4}
is the four-fold rotation in momentum space
While the locations of
\vec{x}
-vortex loops can only be determined numerically, direct calculations reveal that the
\vec{z}
-vortex loops reside at

\lambda=k_{\perp},\ k_{z}=\pm k_{z\star},\ \operatorname{with}\ k_{z\star}=\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-m/A}.

(19)

In contrast, there is no vorticity for
m^{\prime}>0
. The circulation of the photonic shift vectors in Fig.
8
(b) imply that both
\vec{z}
-vortex (
\vec{x}
-vortex) loops have vorticity
-1
, which is consistent with the theorem’s prediction that
Vort_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
changes by
+2
.

The combination of Berry curvature and optical vorticity results in the three-fold decomposition of the shift current being highly sensitive to the photon frequency and polarization. Focusing first on
z
-polarized light, the only nonzero component of the shift current (allowed by
O(2)
symmetry) is the z component. Fig.
8
(e) illustrates the threefold decomposition of the shift conductivity
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{z}}
for a range of frequencies, including low frequencies that are comparable to
|E_{g}|/\hbar
, as well as higher frequencies where the excitation surface approaches the pair of
\vec{z}
-vortex loops, as illustrated in Fig.
9
.

We focus first on the high-frequency regime, where the optical affinity
|\vec{z}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv}|^{2}
is reduced in the vicinity of the zeroes of
\vec{z}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv}
. Ceteris paribus, a reduction of the affinity would depress each of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}},\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{z}}
and
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{z}}
, according to Eq. (
7
).
This depression is observed in Fig.
8
(e) for
both
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}}
and
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{z}}
; in contrast,
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{z}}
is enhanced rather than depressed, for two reasons:

(a) The non-uniformity of the optical affinity (over the excitation surface) favors
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{z}}
. On one hand, Fig.
9
shows that the optical affinity is more greatly reduced for large
|k_{z}|
(closer to the vortex) than it is for small
|k_{z}|
(further from the vortex), implying that the predominant relaxation paths are proximate to the
k_{x}
axis.
On the other hand, only those relaxation paths (for which
d\boldsymbol{k}
is perpendicular to
\vec{z}
) result in a large anomalous shift:
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times d\boldsymbol{k}
. Bringing both hands together,
\vec{z}
-vorticity preserves the horizontal relaxation paths which have a large anomalous shift, and deactivates the vertical relaxation paths which anyway have a negligible anomalous shift.

(b) A higher photon frequency implies that the excitation surface encloses a larger volume of quasiparticle Bloch states with nontrivial Berry curvature, and this results in a larger anomalous shift. For an intuitive understanding, consider reformulating the intra-conduction-band
30
30

30

The total interband contribution to the affinity shift loop is twice of Eq. (
20
), owing to
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\approx-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
contribution to the affinity shift loop [Eq. (
7
))]

\displaystyle{\sum_{\mathcal{P}}\bigg(|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{-\mathcal{P}}\int_{-\mathcal{P}}\;+\;|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{{\cal P}}\bigg)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k},}

(20)

with
{\cal P}
and
-{\cal P}
representing diametrically opposed geodesic paths in a cross section of the torus enclosed by the excitation surface (as representatively illustrated by arrows in the blue and yellow semicircles of Fig.
9
);
|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{\pm\mathcal{P}}
is the optical affinity evaluated at the intersection of
\pm\mathcal{P}
with the excitation surface, and
\sum_{{\cal P}}
integrates over all pairs of
\pm{\cal P}
such as to entirely fill the torus. From Eq. (
20
), one deduces that the anomalous interband contribution (to the affinity shift loop) increases with increasing photon frequency, because one integrates the Berry curvature over increasingly wider paths.
31
31

31

The increase of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}
(with respect to frequency) saturates when the excitation energy (measured from the conduction-band minimum) becomes comparable to the band gap:
E_{exc}-E_{c,\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}\sim|E_{g}|
, as illustrated in Fig.
8
(c-d). After all, this energy interval contains the Berry curvature ‘hot spot’.

Conversely, for smaller frequencies (
\omega\approx|E_{g}|/\hbar
), the excitation surface lies closer to the extremal wavevectors
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
but further away from the vortex loops; then it is the photon-mediated
\vec{z}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{z}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}})
which dominates over the phonon-mediated
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{z}}
[cf. the trends in Fig.
8
(e)], owing to the Berry dipole moment vanishing as the volume (enclosed by the excitation surface) shrinks [Fig.
8
(g)].

\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{z}}
and
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\vec{z}}
oppose each other but do not cancel out, because the excitation shift
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{c\leftarrow v}^{\vec{z}}
is larger in magnitude than
the recombination shift
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{rec}
.
32
32

32

This is explained by
\boldsymbol{S}_{rec}
being a weighted average of
\boldsymbol{S}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}^{{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}}
over all possible polarization vectors
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
of the spontaneously emitted photons [cf. Eq. (
6
)]. Moreover,
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}^{\vec{x}}=\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}^{\vec{y}}=0
owing to a chiral symmetry [
\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}H_{\text{BiTeI}}(k_{x},k_{y},0)\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}=-H_{\text{BiTeI}}(k_{x},k_{y},0)
] which is elaborated in App
H
. This exemplifies a general principle that the recombination shift strongly depends on the symmetry of the Hamiltonian at the wavevectors of recombination. For BiTeI, chiral symmetry reduces
|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{rec}|
to about a third of the lattice period, whereas for the two-band model in Eq. (
13
), the symmetries of reflection and quasi-two-dimensionality enhance
|\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{rec}|
by precluding an orientational-disordered average, as explained in App.
F
.

In comparing the shift conductivites for
\vec{z}
- vs
\vec{x}
-polarized light [Fig.
8
(e) vs Fig.
8
(f)], the starkest difference is that
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\vec{x}}
is a non-monotone function which is suppressed at higher photon frequency.
33
33

33

A minor difference between Fig.
8
(c) and Fig.
8
(d) is that
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\vec{x}}
is also non-monotonic and remains small at low frequencies. This occurs because
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}^{\vec{x}}=0,
owing to an emergent chiral symmetry at the band edge, as elaborated in App
H
.
This is because, in contrast to
\vec{z}
-vortices and the above-mentioned point (a),
\vec{x}
-vortices lie on the
k_{x}
axis [c.f. Fig.
9
] and reduces the optical affinity
|\vec{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}
for small
|k_{z}|
. Thus for high frequencies,
\vec{x}
-vortices deactivate the horizontal relaxation paths which have the largest anomalous shift (
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times d\boldsymbol{k}
); this effectively suppresses the intraband shift current and leads to a linear disparity (
|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x}}-\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{z}}|
) of order
0.1mAV^{-2}
.

Let us close this section by explaining the
(1/E_{g})
-divergence (and concomitant sign change) of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{\text{intra}}
across the topological phase transition, as illustrated in Fig.
8
(e,f). It suffices to show that the affinity shift loop also has a
(1/E_{g})
-divergence, according to the proportionality relation in Eq. (
10
). The contribution to the affinity shift loop [cf. Eq. (
20
)] by a pair of diametrically-opposed geodesic paths (
\pm\mathcal{P}
) can be further decomposed as

\text{Aff}_{\text{ave}}\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}+\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}+\delta\text{Aff}\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}-\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k},

(21)

with
\text{Aff}_{\text{ave}}\equiv(|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{-{\cal P}}+|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{{\cal P}})/2
and
\delta\text{Aff}\equiv(|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{-{\cal P}}-|\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}_{{\cal P}})/2
. The term proportional to
\text{Aff}_{\text{ave}}
is asymptotically irrelevant as
|E_{g}|
approaches zero, owing to an emergent left-right symmetry of the massive Dirac fermion [Eq. (
18
)] about the extremal wavevector.
34
34

34

This left-right symmetry is explained in App.
H.3
.
What remains is to evaluate the asymptotic behavior of the term proportional to
\delta\text{Aff}
: the integral (
\int_{-\mathcal{P}}-\int_{\mathcal{P}}
) of the anomalous shift vector diverges as
1/E_{g}
, because: (i)
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}/|d\boldsymbol{k}|
diverges as
E_{g}/|E_{g}|^{3}
at the band extremum, which is a well-known type of divergence for massive Dirac fermions [cf. Eq. (
240
)], and (ii) the width of the Berry curvature hot spot is of order
|m^{\prime}|/\hbar v\propto|E_{g}|
. Combining both (i) and (ii), the second integral in Eq. (
21
) is estimated as (extremal value of curvature)
\times
(hot-spot width), which is proportional to
E_{g}/|E_{g}|^{3}\times|E_{g}|=1/E_{g}
. Because this divergence applies to any pair of diametrically opposed geodesic paths, the affinity shift loop must likewise diverge as
1/E_{g}
, and thus also
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{\text{intra}}\approx\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
.

There are two reasons why this divergence will be cut off in a more realistic model of BiTeI, meaning that the
1/E_{g}
behavior breaks down in a narrow energy interval:
|E_{g}|<E_{g}^{cut}
:

(i) The first reason is that not all photo-excited quasiparticles will relax all the way down to the conduction-band bottom (where the Berry curvature diverges), but instead they will relaxe to a Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution with a characteristic thermal energy
k_{B}T_{l}
.
35
35

35

The preceding calculation of the
1/E_{g}
divergence assumed that as
|E_{g}|\rightarrow 0
,
k_{B}T_{l}
must likewise
\rightarrow 0
; indeed, the geodesic approximation relied on
k_{B}T_{l}\ll|E_{g}|
, as was explained in Sec.
II
.

(ii) The second reason is that the O(2) symmetry of our effective model of BiTeI is only approximate; in real BiTeI, the topological phase transition (between two topologically distinct semiconductors) is not intermediated by an
O(2)
-symmetric nodal-loop band touching, but by a
C_{3v}
-symmetric Weyl-semimetallic phase; the energy scale of the
C_{3v}
-symmetric trigonal warping is estimated to be about
10meV
.
Tan and Rappe 2016

Both reasons suggest the
1/E_{g}
behavior of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
to be precluded with a cutoff
E_{g}^{cut}
that is comparable to either
k_{B}T_{l}
or
10meV
, whichever is larger.

VI
Discussion and outlook

VI.1
The three-fold way

The steady shift current density in a direct-gap semiconductor has a three-fold decomposition:
\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
, corresponding respectively to current contributions by interband
exc
itation [cf. Eq. (
6
)], interband
rec
ombination [Eq. (
6
)], and
intra
band relaxation [Eq. (
6
)]. While this threefold decomposition has been studied for simplified models of pyroelectrics and piezoelectrics,
Belinicher et al. 1982
it is here that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
acquires a new dimension of understanding through the lens of wave function geometry. Geometrical notions (such as the Berry phase) transcend the traditional classification of piezoelectrics vs pyroelectrics, and provide overarching principles to guide our interpretation of the out-of-equilibrium, many-body dynamics of photo-excited matter.

One of our main results is that the excitation-induced current density
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
can be outweighed by either of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
, especially in semiconductors characterized by large intraband Berry curvature or optical vortices (topological singularities in the interband Berry phase). Model semiconductors with large Berry curvature exhibit a shift-current conductivity that is of order
mAV^{-2}
without finetuning of the incident radiation frequency; in the presence of optical vortices, the conductivity can change by
\sim mAV^{-2}
if the linear polarization vector flips by ninety degrees.
These estimates of the conductivity assumed a generic value of the joint density of states, but in principle the joint density of states can be further optimized
Cook et al. 2017
for a synergistic enhancement. To our knowledge, no measurement of the short-circuit conductivity in shift-current materials has reached the
mAV^{-2}
range.

VI.2
Wave-function approach to photovoltaic materials

Establishing the steady shift current in the broader framework of wave function geometry confers an advantage: we acquire a Rosetta stone to translate our vast body of knowledge (on topological materials) to concrete predictions of photovoltaic materials. Here are two cases in point:

(i) Intraband relaxation due to electron-phonon scattering results in an anomalous shift that is proportional to the intraband Berry curvature; cf. Eq. (
5
).
36
36

36

Electron-phonon scattering is not the only mechanism for intraband relaxation in a direct-gap semiconductor; electron-impurity scattering also results in a shift,
Belinicher et al. 1982
which may substantially contribute to
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
for dirtier samples. The impurity-mediated shift is closely analogous to the ‘side jump’ in the anomalous Hall effect of magnetic metals.
Sinitsyn et al. 2006
In their study of the ‘side jump’, Sinitsyn et al have argued that the impurity-mediated shift reduces to the anomalous shift [Eq. (
5
)] under two assumptions: (i) dominance of small-angle scattering, and (ii) the cell-periodic component of the Bloch function is spatially homogeneous. The improbability of either assumption makes for a tenuous relation between the impurity-mediated shift and the anomalous shift.
Let us juxtapose this anomalous shift with the nonlinear Hall effect predicted by Sodemann and Fu.
Sodemann and Fu 2015
What matters to the anomalous shift current is the Berry curvature of all Bloch states enclosed by the excitation surface; this contrasts with the nonlinear Hall effect, which depends (at low temperature) on the Berry curvature of the Fermi surface. However, the two effects are not completely divorced: a semiconductor with a large anomalous shift current is continuously tunable (e.g., by doping) to a metal with a large nonlinear Hall effect. This is evident from Fig.
5
(a) if one imagines the excitation surface to be a Fermi surface.

(ii) While the topological-matter community is well-versed in finding materials with large intraband Berry curvature, it is presently unclear which materials have optical vorticity. On one hand, a highthroughput ab-initio algorithm has been proposed in Ref.
Alexandradinata 2022
to search for materials with optical vorticity. On the other hand, it would also be advantageous to identify general, topological principles which guarantee the existence of optical vorticity in certain classes of materials. One such principle is the Chern-vorticity theorem in Eq. (
15
), which relates the Chern number (of a 2D cross-section of a 3D Brillouin zone, or of a 2D Brillouin zone) to the net vorticity (of the same 2D cross-section or 2D Brillouin zone). This theorem has broad implications for the vorticity in topological semimetals and topological insulators, one of which is the necessary existence of optical vorticity in BiTeI.

Our case study of the linear photogalvanic effect (LPGE) in semiconducting BiTeI [Sec.
V
] illustrates four principles:

(a) Because the
\boldsymbol{k}
-locations of optical vortices depend on the light polarization, the steady shift current sensitively depends on the light polarization. If the excitation surface is proximate to an optical vortex, the photonic shift vector is orientationally-disordered over the excitation surface, which tends to reduce the excitation shift current.

(b) The recombination shift strongly depends on the symmetry of the Hamiltonian at the wavevectors of recombination. For BiTeI, chiral symmetry reduces the recombination shift vector to about a third of the lattice period.

(c) Because band wave functions can strongly depend on energy, so can the steady shift current sensitively depend on the photon frequency. A rule of thumb is that the net shift conductivity changes sign in the transition from a photon-mediated shift current (at low frequencies corresponding to band-edge excitation) to a phonon-mediated anomalous shift current (at higher frequencies).

(d) The steady shift current is sensitive to discrete changes of the wave function topology. In particular, the sign of the steady shift current changes sign across the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological phase transition, and the magnitude of said current is extraordinarily large in the vicinity of the transition.

The experimental implications of (a-d) are summarized in Fig.
10
.
The figure also illustrates the bulk photovoltaic current in semimetallic BiTeI: a previous theory
Facio et al. 2018
has predicted a nonlinear Hall current that depends on the Berry curvature dipole
\boldsymbol{d}
of the Fermi surface and also changes sign across the topological phase transition. For light sources in the 100 THz regime (and higher), the bulk photovotaic current of semimetallic BiTeI is dominated by the circular photogalvanic effect (CPGE),
37
37

37

The CPGE is larger than the LPGE by a factor
\omega\tau\gg 1
, with
\omega
being the photon frequency and
\tau\gtrsim 100
fs being the momentum relaxation time.
Sodemann and Fu 2015
but this is not true for semiconducting BiTeI.

Figure 10:
A photovoltaic phase diagram of BiTeI. The carrier density can be tuned by varying the growth method
Tomokiyo et al. 1977
;
Lošťák et al. 1980
or by doping with Cu.
Wang et al. 2013
Pressure can be applied within a diamond anvil cell
Qi et al. 2017
or by chemical substitution.

Our
photovoltaic phase diagram
of BiTeI showcases the tight correlations between wave function geometry and the bulk photovoltaic effect over a wide range of carrier densities and on both sides of the topological phase transition. We hypothesize that similar correlations hold for other topological materials, suggesting the bulk photovoltaic effect to be an unprecedented phenomenological framework to faithfully diagnose and sensitively characterize topological materials.

VI.3
Transient vs steady photovoltaic current

Sec.
II
demonstrates that the steady, excitation-induced current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
well approximates the transient current at the onset of radiation. By substracting the transient current (measured at early times) from the steady current (measured at late times), would one obtain
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
?
38
38

38

We consider an experimental geometry where the photon-dragged current vanishes,
Danishevskii et al. 1970
;
Grinberg 1970
and where the surface photovoltaic current is separable from the bulk photovoltaic current.
Alperovich et al. 1982
Not quite, because the steady photovoltaic current includes not only the shift current but also the ballistic current.
39
39

39

The ballistic current results from a
(\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{k})
asymmetry in the quasiparticle distribution;
Belinicher and Sturman 1980
;
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
;
Alperovich et al. 1982
;
Sturman 2020
such asymmetry can be induced by electron-impurity, electron-phonon,
Dai et al. 2021
and electron-hole interactions.
Dai and Rappe 2021
. Does a large phonon-induced ballistic current correlate with a large phonon-induced
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
? We leave this open question for future investigations.

It may be argued that the transient current is insensitive to the temperature of a photoexcited semiconductor,
40
40

40

\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
depends on temperature only through the photoexcitation transition rate, which is proportional to
(1-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
f
the Fermi-Dirac distribution. Assuming the band gap greatly exceeds
k_{B}T_{0}
,
(1-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\approx 1
is insensitive to
T_{0}
.
while the steady current includes
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
which is sensitive to the temperature of the phonons. Suggestively, a substantial disparity in the temperature dependence (of the transient vs steady photocurrent) was observed for a ferroelectric charge-transfer complex, though the disparity was attributed by Nakamura et al to the formation of a Schottky barrier at the sample-electrode interface.
Nakamura et al. 2017

It would be interesting to see if this disparity persists for a different electrode whose work function is identical to that of the sample.

Conversely, it has been reported that the Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shrekbtii formula
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
for the shift current adequately describes the photoconductivity measurements of n-GaP,
Hornung et al. 1983
suggesting that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
is small for this material; this hypothesis can be tested by an ab-initio study of the intra/interband Berry phase of GaP, which we would love to see.

VI.4
The loop approach to shift currents

Our analysis of the direct-gap semiconductor relied on identifying a reduced set of quasiparticle transitions that concatenate into loops [cf. Fig.
4
] and predominantly contribute to the steady shift current [Eq. (
1
)]. App.
D
shows how to exactly reformulate the BIS formula [Eq. (
1
)] as a sum over
loop currents
:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\bigg({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\bigg)=\sum_{\text{loop}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}},}

(22)

with
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}
meaning the current contributed by a closed flow line (in energy-momentum space) of one-electron probability.

The loop formulation holistically treats excitation, relaxation and recombination as inextricably linked processes; such linkage is epitomized by the shift loop in Eqs. (
6
)-(
10
). Using loops allows to derive general properties of the steady shift current that do not necessarily apply to the transient shift current; in particular, a purported relation between the shift current and interband polarization differences
Fregoso et al. 2017
is shown in App.
D.3
to apply to the transient current but not the steady current. A related advantage of loops is calculational: approximating the steady shift current by a reduced family of predominant loops [e.g., via Eq. (
10
)] requires far less computational resources than simulating a quasiparticle distribution
f_{B}
that sets the collisional integral to zero, and then inputting
f_{B}
into the BIS formula; cf. App.
C.2
.

Our loop current formulation is applicable beyond direct-gap semiconductors, with the caveat that the predominant loops may change depending on the context. For instance, recombination in indirect-gap semiconductors is intermediated by
transitions between Bloch states and impurity-localized states; such transitions also contribute to the shift current.
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
In small-gap semiconductors or Dirac-Weyl semimetals, interband recombination may be contributed by electron-phonon scattering. It would be desirable to develop a theory of the steady shift current in Dirac-Weyl semimetals, for which the anomalous shift may potentially be large. It is hoped that photoconductivity measurements of TaAs
Osterhoudt et al. 2019
would benefit from a re-interpretation of what exactly is causing the shift.

VI.5
The potential for solar cell applications

A large short-circuit conductivity is not sufficient for solar cell applications; also required is a large open-circuit photovoltage to generate sufficient electrical power. Though shift-current materials can have open-circuit photovoltages that greatly exceed the band gap,
Brody 1973
;
Glass et al. 1974
;
Koch et al. 1975
the product of the short-circuit current and open-circuit photovoltage may be limited depending on the architecture of the shift-driven solar cell.

Let us first consider Pusch et al.’s model
Pusch et al. 2023
of a shift-driven solar cell, in which a homogeneous shift-current-carrying intrinsic semiconductor is connected via leads to an external load; henceforth, we refer to this as the
PRCE cell
. Assuming a few ideal conditions, namely that the contacts with the leads do not introduce additional resistance, and that temperature is sufficiently low (
k_{B}T\ll E_{g}
) to ignore the dark conductivity, the light-to-electrical energy conversion efficiency of a PRCE cell is calculated in App.
K
to be

\displaystyle{\text{Eff}=&\;\frac{1}{4}\frac{m_{r}\mathfrak{s}^{2}/\tau_{tr}\tau_{rec}}{\hbar\omega},}

(23)

under monochromatic illumination with frequency
\omega
. Here,
m_{r}^{-1}=m_{e}^{-1}+m_{h}^{-1}
is the reduced mass of an electron-hole pair in the parabolic-band approximation,
\tau_{tr}
is the Drude-type transport lifetime for photo-excited carriers, and
\tau_{rec}
is the recombination lifetime; cf. Sec.
II
.

\mathfrak{s}
can be interpreted as the average shift per photo-excited electron-hole pair, and is expressible as a normalized affinity shift loop [cf. Eq. (
7
)]:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}=&\;\frac{ASL_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}}{\langle f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}}.}

(24)

Our formula for the efficiency is essentially identical to Eq. (11) in Ref.
Pusch et al. 2023
, except that our
\mathfrak{s}
replaces their heuristically defined ‘average charge displacement
R
’ with a precisely calculable formula; moreover, Eqs. (
23
)-(
24
) with Eqs. (
6
)-(
7
) clarify the oft ignored roles of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
in the operation of a shift-driven solar cell.

Even if
\mathfrak{s}
is of order the lattice period
a
, it may be seen that Eff
\ll\ll 1
at solar frequencies and with typical values for
a\approx 5\mbox{\AA}
,
m_{r}\approx m_{f}
(free-electron mass),
\tau_{tr}\approx 100fs
, and
\tau_{rec}\approx 1ns
for radiative recombination. More appreciable efficiencies are expected for heavy-fermion materials dominated by faster non-radiative recombination,
Pankove 1975
and with extraordinarily large lattice periods that characterize (Moiré) superlattices. Going beyond the PRCE model, inhomogeneous doping of the shift-current-carrying semiconductor would marry advantages of both the shift mechanism and conventional barrier layer photovoltaics; whether such a synergy is incremental or transformative remains to be seen.

Appendix

The appendix contains several supplementary results for the specialized audience. Most of these results have been referenced and motivated in the main text. An organizational structure of the appendix is presented to help with navigation:

(App.
A
) This
glossary
collects many recurrent equations and symbols for easy reference. The equations include the Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman (BIS) formula for the shift current [App.
A.1
] and its threefold decomposition into contributions by excitation, recombination and intraband transitions [App.
A.4
]. The BIS formula inputs the quasiparticle, photonic and phononic occupancies and outputs a current; certain assumptions about these occupancies are recorded in App.
A.2
. All explicit calculations of the shift current in this work have been based on a two-band, direct-gap semiconducting model; the terminology that surrounds this model is collected in App.
A.3
.

(App.
B
) This appendix summarizes several salient aspects of the steady
non-equilibrium distribution of photo-excited carriers
. Included is a review of the different relaxation mechanisms for a hot photo-excited carrier in a semiconductor [App.
B.1
] and a derivation of the associated kinetic model which applies in the regime of low carrier density [App.
B.2
]. Simplified versions of this kinetic model are described if there is electron-hole symmetry [App.
B.2.1
] and if the collisional integral is constant along iso-energy surfaces [App.
B.2.2
].

(App.
C
) The
BIS formula of the shift current
is pedagogically derived in App.
C.1
, numerically calculated in App.
C.2
, and compared to other formulas of the shift current in App.
C.3
. This comparison elucidiates what is missing from the Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shkrebtii formula,
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
as well as formulas derived from dissipative Floquet methods.
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
;
Barik and Sau 2020
;
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
App.
C.1
also demonstrates that to describe the steady state perturbatively, the zeroth-order state is emphatically
not
the thermal equilibrium state (in the absence of the light source).

(App.
D
) The steady shift current is
equivalently formulated in terms of loop currents
.
The basic ingredients of the loop formulation are presented in App.
D.1
, namely the loop decomposition of the probability flow network, the shift loop, and the loop current. A theorem derived in App.
D.2
formalizes the equivalence between the BIS formula and a sum of loop currents. This theorem is applied to revise a purported relation
Fregoso et al. 2017
between the shift current and interband polarization differences [App.
D.3
], and to derive the geodesic approximation of the shift conductivity for 3D semiconductors [App.
D.4
] and quasi-2D semiconductors [App.
D.6
]. The geodesic approximation is rigorously justified as the small-angle-scattering limit of the BIS formula in App.
D.5
.

(App.
E
) The
transient shift current
is shown here to be well approximated by the excitation-induced component of the steady shift current. This appendix rigorously elaborates an argument presented in Sec.
II
.

(App.
F
) The shift conductivity is calculated for a
model Hamiltonian with optical vortices
, to corroborate claims made in Sec.
IV
about the vortex-induced linear disparity of the shift conductivity.

(App.
G
) A
theorem relating Chern numbers to optical vorticity
[cf. Eq. (
15
)] is proven here.

(App.
H
) A few facts which
support our case study on BiTeI
is presented here, including an effective Hamiltonian of a massive Dirac fermion that holds near the topological phase transition, as well as the vanishing of the shift current at the band edge, for
x
and
y
-polarized light.

(App.
I
) An
alternative derivation of the anomalous shift vector
is provided.

(App.
J
) A
misconception about the electron-phonon scattering rate
is exposed. The misconception traces back to a fallacious belief of a universally applicable gauge for the Bloch wave function.

(App.
K
) The
energy conversion efficiency
is calculated for a solar cell based on an intrinsic, shift-current-carrying semiconductor.

Appendix A
Glossary

A.1
The Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman formula

We use ‘Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman (BIS) formula’ to refer to two sets of equations for the phonon-mediated and photon-mediated shift current. The former current is given by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=&\;-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\bigg({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\bigg);\;\;\;\;B=(b\boldsymbol{k}),\;\;\;\;B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\;\;\;\;m=(\boldsymbol{q}p),}

(25)

with the phononic shift vector given by

\displaystyle{Phonon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\arg V^{m}_{B^{\prime},B}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}},}

(26)

and with the difference in absorption and emission transition rates given by

\displaystyle{Phonon:\;\;\;\;{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}=&\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}|^{2}\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega_{m})\bigg\{\big(1-f_{B^{\prime}}\big)f_{B}N_{m}-\big(1-f_{B}\big)f_{B^{\prime}}(N_{m}+1)\bigg\},}

(27)

with
E_{B^{\prime}B}=E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B}
. Actually, the above equations are more general than those presented in Ref.
Belinicher et al. 1982
, in that the above equations allow for interband phonon-mediated transitions, while the formula in the BIS paper does not. This being a minor generalization, we will anyway refer to our final formula as the BIS formula.

The BIS formula for the photon-mediated shift current combines Eq. (
25
) with

\displaystyle{Photon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};{\\
}{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}=\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\frac{(2\pi e)^{2}\omega_{m}}{{\cal V}}\;\big|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big|^{2}\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega_{m})\bigg\{\big(1-f_{B^{\prime}}\big)f_{B}N_{m}-\big(1-f_{B}\big)f_{B^{\prime}}(N_{m}+1)\bigg\},}

(28)

in Gaussian units. In Ref.
Belinicher et al. 1982
,
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}
appears with an additional multiplicative factor of
1/n^{2}
, with
n
the refractive index; this factor should not be there, according to our derivation in App.
C.1
.
41
41

41

The
1/n^{2}
factor is absent in the Sturman-Fridkin monograph
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
which followed after the BIS paper. A factor of
1/n
appears only in the conversion of photon occupancies to the radiation intensity; cf. App.
C.3
.

In the steady state, the quasiparticle distribution
f_{B}
satisfies a non-detailed balance condition that represents an invariance under simultaneous collisions with all bosons:

\displaystyle{\text{For all $B$,}\;\;\;\;0=\sum_{m}\sum_{B^{\prime}}\big({\cal A}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}+{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}-{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\big),}

(29)

with
\sum_{m}
summing over all photonic and phononic modes. The right-hand side of the above equality may be viewed as the collisional integral evaluated to second order in the electron-boson coupling, i.e., the integral has a form expected from Fermi’s golden rule. Eqs. (
25
)-(
29
) represents a closed set of equations to determine the shift current: one first determines
f_{B}
from Eq. (
29
) then inputs
f_{B}
into Eq. (
25
).

A.2
Quasiparticle, photon and phonon occupancies

The above equations show that the BIS current is a functional of the quasiparticle, photon and phonon occupancies:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{j}[f_{B},N_{m}^{phot},N_{m}^{phon}],}

(30)

with the dependence on occupancies given solely by the transition rates
{\cal A}={\cal A}[f_{B},N_{m}^{phot},N_{m}^{phon}]
and
{\cal E}={\cal E}[f_{B},N_{m}^{phot},N_{m}^{phon}]
.

Throughout this work,
f
symbolizes the occupancy of charged, fermionic quasiparticles that are long-lived in an insulator,
Kohn 1957
though strictly speaking we do not account for the renormalization of the wave function.
Sham 1966

When there is no
T
(for temperature) superscript on
f_{B}
,
f_{B}
should be understood as the non-equilibrium, steady distribution determined in a kinetic model [cf. Sec.
B.2
], hence
f_{B}
generically deviates from the thermal

\displaystyle{\text{Fermi-Dirac distribution}:\;\;\;\;f_{B}^{T}=\frac{1}{e^{(E_{B}-\mu)/k_{B}T}+1}.}

(31)

The phonons are assumed to thermalize with a lattice temperature
T_{l}
, meaning that the phonon occupancy is a Planck distribution:

\displaystyle{\text{Phonon}:\;\;\;\;N_{m}=N_{m}^{T_{l}}=\frac{1}{e^{\hbar\omega_{m}/k_{B}T_{l}}-1}.}

(32)

We will not always have the
phon
or
phot
superscript on
N_{m}
, so the meaning of
m
should be deduced from the local context. The minimal frequency for optical phonons is defined to be the optical phonon threshold:

\displaystyle{\text{Optical phonon}:\;\;\;\;min\{\omega_{m}\}=\Omega_{o};\;\;\;\;k_{B}T_{l}\ll\hbar\Omega_{o}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;N_{m}^{T_{l}}\ll 1,}

(33)

\hbar\Omega_{o}
typically comparable to
k_{B}
times room temperature. We assume in calculations of the shift conductivity that the lattice temperature is small compared to the optical phonon energy, hence the thermal occupancy of optical phonons is negligible.

The photon occupancy is
assumed to be a sum of thermal and source-generated contributions:

\displaystyle{\text{Photon}:\;\;\;\;N_{m}=N_{m}^{T_{l}}+\Delta N_{s}\delta_{m,m_{s}};\;\;\;\;\Delta N_{s}\gg 1.}

(34)

We have assumed that the source is bright (
\Delta N_{s}\gg 1
) and produces photons of a single, linearly-polarized mode:

\displaystyle{\text{Source:}\;\;\;\;\text{mode}\;=m_{s};\;\;\;\;\text{frequency}\;=\omega_{s}=c||\boldsymbol{q}_{s}||>E_{g}/\hbar;\;\;\;\;\text{polarization vector}\;=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.}

(35)

In the classical approximation to the radiation field,
\Delta N_{s}
can be be expressed in terms of the electric-wave amplitude according to:
42
42

42

This may be derived from the standard relation
Heitler 1954
;
Sakurai 1967
between the classical electromagnetic vector potential and the photon number:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{\cal E}}=-\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{A}^{s}_{em}}{\partial t};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{A}^{s}_{em}=2c\sqrt{\frac{h\,\Delta N_{s}}{\omega_{s}\,{\cal V}}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\,\cos(\boldsymbol{q}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{s}t).}

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{\cal E}}={\cal E}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}e^{i(\boldsymbol{q}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{s}t)}+c.c.;\;\;\;\;|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}=2\pi\frac{\hbar\omega\Delta N_{s}}{{\cal V}}.}

(36)

The vector-valued
shift conductivity
is defined by the nonlinear current response:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2},}

(37)

with
\boldsymbol{j}
the shift current in Eq. (
25
).

A basic property of the quasiparticle transition rates is that the absorption and emission rates cancel out if the fermions and bosons are thermalized with the same temperature:

\displaystyle{\text{Detailed balance}:\;\;\;\;\big({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\big)_{f^{T}_{B},f^{T}_{B^{\prime}},N^{T}_{m}}=0.}

(38)

This holds for both phonons and phonons, as one may verify by substituting the Fermi-Dirac and Planck distributions into Eqs. (
27
)-(
28
).

A.3
Direct-gap semiconducting model

All explicit calculations of the shift conductivity in this work are based on a model of a direct-gap intrinsic semiconductor with two bands (not counting spin):

\displaystyle{\text{Band indices}:\;\;\;\;&b=c\;\text{(conduction)};\;\;\;\;b=v\;\text{(valence)};{\\
}\text{Bloch labels:}\;\;\;\;C=(c\boldsymbol{k});\;\;\;\;V=(v\boldsymbol{k}).}

E_{C}-E_{V}
is assumed to be minimized at a single wavevector, which we call the
extremal wavevector
:

\displaystyle{\text{min}\{E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\}=E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=E_{g};\;\;\;\;E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{C}-E_{V}.}

(39)

The band gap
E_{g}
is assumed to be large compared to the lattice temperature
T_{l}
(with source turned on) and the equilibrium temperature
T_{0}
(with source turned off), hence the equilibrium Fermi-Dirac occupancies are close to being binary:

\displaystyle{k_{B}T_{0}\ll E_{g}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;f^{T_{0}}_{C}\approx 0;\;\;\;\;f^{T_{0}}_{V}\approx 1.}

(40)

Iso-energy surfaces of a band are defined to
\boldsymbol{k}
-surfaces in which the band dispersion is constant:

\displaystyle{\text{Iso-energy surface of band $b$ and energy $E$}\;\equiv\;\text{all}\;\boldsymbol{k}\;\text{satisfying}\;\;\;\;E_{B}=E.}

(41)

The source photon frequency is assumed large enough that resonant absorption can occur across the band gap, and the excitation surface is defined as the
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface where resonant optical absorption can occur:

\displaystyle{ES\equiv\;\text{excitation surface}\;\equiv\;\text{all}\;\boldsymbol{k}\;\text{satisfying}\;\;\;\;E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\hbar\omega_{s}>E_{g}.}

(42)

We define
E_{b,exc}
as the energies of
b
-band states on the excitation surface:

\displaystyle{\text{Excitation energy}:\;\;\;\;E_{c,exc}=\{E_{C}\;|\;\boldsymbol{k}\in ES\};\;\;\;\;E_{v,exc}=\{E_{V}\;|\;\boldsymbol{k}\in ES\}.}

(43)

We will encounter symmetric models where
E_{c,exc}
is degenerate for all conduction-band states on the excitation surface, meaning the excitation surface is an iso-energy surface of energy
E_{c,exc}
.

For photonic modes that mediate resonant interband transitions, their thermal occupancy is negligible:

\displaystyle{\text{Resonant photon}:N_{m}^{T_{l}}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{m})\ll\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{m})\;\;\;\;\Leftarrow\;\;\;\;k_{B}T_{l}\ll E_{g}.}

(44)

The passive and active regions of either band are defined with respect to the optical phonon threshold [Eq. (
33
)]:

\displaystyle{\text{Conduction}:\;\;\;\;&E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}>\hbar\Omega_{o}\;\text{(active)};\;\;\;\;0<E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}<\hbar\Omega_{o}\;\text{(passive)};{\\
}\text{Valence}:\;\;\;\;-(E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}})>\hbar\Omega_{o}\;\text{(active)};\;\;\;\;0<-(E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}})<\hbar\Omega_{o}\;\text{(passive)}.}

A.4
Excitation, recombination and intraband components of the shift current

As discussed in Sec.
II
, the steady shift current can be decomposed into contributions by excitation, recombination and intraband relaxation processes:

\displaystyle{\text{Threefold decomposition}:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}.}

(45)

Here, we present the explicit expressions for each of the three components in Eqs. (
46
)-(
49
).

The
excitation-induced current
is defined to be the shift current contributed by interband, vertical transitions over the excitation surface [Eq. (
42
)]:

\displaystyle{\text{Excitation-induced current:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}=&\;\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}\approx-2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}I_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}^{m_{s}};{\\
}\text{Excitation rate:}\;\;\;\;I_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}^{m_{s}}=\;\frac{(2\pi e)^{2}\omega_{s}}{{\cal V}}\;\big|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\big|^{2}f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{s})\Delta N_{s}.}

(46)

2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}=2
, with the additional subscript reminding us this two originates from the spin degree of freedom.
f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}
is defined to be
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
. Eq. (
46
) has been presented for the case where a pair of spinless bands (labelled
b=v
and
c
) are optically excited; if there are more than a pair, simply sum the right-hand side of Eq. (
46
) over all pairs.

Eq. (
46
) is derived by restricting
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
in Eq. (
25
) with the condition that
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
lies on the excitation surface, and this is the meaning of
\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}
. In principle,

\sum_{m}
in
\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}
sums over all bosonic modes with the same frequency
\omega_{m}=\omega_{s}
as the source-generated photons. This
\sum_{m}
may be restricted to photonic modes, because the band gap is presumed to exceed the optical phonon energies. Photon-mediated vertical transitions over the excitation surface can be divided into five classes, according to Eq. (
28
) and Eq. (
34
):

(i) Absorption of thermal photons with a rate
\propto N_{m}^{T_{l}}f_{v}(1-f_{c})
,

(ii) Stimulated emission of thermal photons with a rate
\propto N_{m}^{T_{l}}f_{c}(1-f_{v})
,

(iii) Absorption of source-generated photons with a rate
\propto\Delta N_{s}f_{v}(1-f_{c})
,

(iv) Stimulated emission of source-generated photons with a rate
\propto\Delta N_{s}f_{c}(1-f_{v})
, and

(v) Spontaneous emission of photons with a rate
\propto f_{c}(1-f_{v})
.

In practice, only (iii) and (iv) are significant. Here is why. Since the time scale to spontaneously emit photons (
\sim 1\,ns
) greatly exceeds the time scale for scattering with phonons (
\sim 100\,fs
), the contribution of (v) to the shift current is negligible. By our assumptions that temperature is low and that carriers are resonantly excited,
k_{B}T_{l}\ll E_{g}\leq\hbar\omega_{s}=\hbar\omega_{m}
, hence the Planck occupancy
N_{m}^{T_{l}}\ll 1
. It follows that
\Delta N_{s}\gg 1\gg N_{m}^{T_{l}}
, and we then assume (i)
\ll
(iii), and (ii)
\ll
(iv). Keeping only (iii) and (iv) leads to Eq. (
46
).

The
intraband current
is defined to be the shift current contributed by intraband transitions:

\displaystyle{\text{Intraband current}:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}=\sum_{b=v,c}\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{b};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{b}=\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{b=b^{\prime}}=-2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}|}\sum_{m}^{phon}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg({\cal A}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}-{\cal E}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg),}

(47)

with
{\cal A}-{\cal E}
given in Eq. (
27
). When bands do not overlap on the energy axis (as is true for our two-band semiconducting model), intraband transitions may be restricted to phononic modes, because the typical quasiparticle band velocity is much less than the speed of light.

It is useful to decompose the intraband current into contributions by acoustic and optical phonons:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{b}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{a,b}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{o,b}
. Assuming (a)
k_{B}T_{l}\ll\hbar\Omega_{o}
, (b) the active region is much bigger than the passive region (
|E_{b,exc}-E_{b,\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}|\gg\hbar\Omega_{o}
), and that (c) small-angle-scattering predominates, the effect of acoustic phonons is substantially outweighed by that of optical phonons:
||\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{a,b}||\ll||\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{o,b}||
. Here is why.
Assumption (c) allows us to employ the small-angle-limit of the phononic shift in Eq. (
5
); because this anomalous shift is proportional to
\delta k=||\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}||
, the net effect of transitions
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}
within the passive region are ignorable compared to transitions within the much larger active region; cf. a similar argument made in Sec.
II
. Within the active region, transitions mediated by optical phonons are predominantly that of spontaneous emission, because the thermal occupancies of optical phonons are small; cf. Eq. (
33
). Transitions mediated by spontaneous emission of optical phonons predominantly result in a larger shift than transitions mediated by acoustic phonons; this is because the time scales for individual collisions are comparable to
100fs
for both types of phonons,
Lundstrom 2010
but for optical phonons,
\delta k
has a lower bound given by
\Omega_{o}
divided by the carrier group velocity, while the only lower bound for electron-acoustic-phonon scattering is the trivial bound
\delta k>0
. Altogether, these considerations lead to the intraband shift current being dominated by:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}^{o,b}\approx&\;2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}|}\sum_{m}^{\text{optical phonons}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}{\cal E}^{sp,m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};{\\
}{\cal E}^{sp,m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\;\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}|^{2}\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega_{m})\big(1-f_{B}\big)f_{B^{\prime}}.}

(48)

The
recombination-induced current
is defined to be the shift current contributed by vertical photon-mediated transitions (
c\boldsymbol{k}\leftrightarrow v\boldsymbol{k}
) for
\boldsymbol{k}
outside the excitation surface:

\displaystyle{\text{Recombination-induced current:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}=&\;\boldsymbol{j}[f]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\notin ES}=2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{m}^{phot}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}I_{rec\boldsymbol{k}}^{m};{\\
}\text{Recombination rate:}\;\;\;\;I_{rec\boldsymbol{k}}^{m}=\;(1-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},ES})\bigg({\cal E}^{m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}-{\cal A}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg),}

(49)

with
{\cal A}-{\cal E}
given in Eq. (
28
).
We have introduced
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},ES}
as the projector to the excitation surface, and
1-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},ES}
as the complementary projector. Eq. (
49
) may be simplified on the basis of two considerations:

(a) The
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
49
) may be further restricted to a small
\boldsymbol{k}
-volume corresponding to the passive region, according to arguments presented in Sec.
II
and App.
B.1
.

(b) Because of the projection in Eq. (
49
),
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
depends on the thermal photon occupancy
N^{T_{l}}_{m}
but not the source-generated occupancy
\Delta N_{s}
; since the thermal occupancies of resonant photons are small [cf. Eq. (
44
)], one may as well retain only the transitions mediated by spontaneous emission.

Altogether, (a-b) imply

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}\approx&\;2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{m}^{phot}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{pass}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}{\cal E}^{sp,m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}},{\\
}{\cal E}^{sp,m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}=\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\frac{(2\pi e)^{2}\omega_{m}}{{\cal V}}\;\big|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\big|^{2}\delta(E_{cv}-\hbar\omega_{m})\big(1-f_{v}\big)f_{c}.}

(50)

We take
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{pass}
to mean an integral over the passive
\boldsymbol{k}
-volume of either the conduction or valence band [cf. Eq. (
A.3
)], whichever of the two volumes is smaller.

The threefold decomposition of the shift current in Eq. (
45
) imply a threefold decomposition of the shift conductivity defined in Eq. (
37
):

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}}{|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}}{|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}}{|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}}.}

(51)

Appendix B
The non-equilibrium distribution of photo-excited carriers

B.1
Relaxation mechanisms for photo-excited carriers

The steady shift current in a semiconductor cannot be calculated without understanding some basic aspects of the relaxation mechanisms and distribution of photo-excited carriers, which we review here. (We use ‘carrier’ as a shorthand for hole and electron quasiparticles.) Much of this brief review derives from a more comprehensive review by Esipov and Levinson.
Esipov and Levinson 1987

Which scattering process dominates the energy relaxation of carriers depends on (i) the radiation intensity
J
generated by a source, and (ii) the energy
E
of the carrier.

The dependence on
J
is because the rate of carrier-carrier scattering via the instantaneous Coulomb interaction increases with the density
n
of photo-excited carriers,
43
43

43

If the majority of photo-excited carriers follow a Maxwellian distribution, the rate of carrier-carrier scattering is simply proportional to
n
; cf. Eq. (2.3.6) in Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
.
and
n
is roughly proportional to
J
.

The dependence on the carrier energy
E
is because the matrix elements for scattering depend on the initial and final states. An especially strong dependence develops for
E
near the optical phonon threshold
\hbar\Omega_{o}
, which is defined as the smallest optical phonon energy. Our convention is that
E
for an electron (resp. hole) carrier is set to zero at the conduction-band mimimum (resp. valence-band maximum). For
E<\hbar\Omega_{o}
(the passive region), a carrier is forbidden by energy conservation against emitting optical phonons, and energy relaxation is substantially suppressed relative to
E>\hbar\Omega_{o}
(the active region); cf. Eq. (
A.3
) and Fig.
3
(b-c).

We define an upper bound
n_{h}
to the carrier density, such that if
n\lesssim n_{h}
(meaning much less than or comparable in magnitude), scattering by optical phonons is the
primary
/dominant mechanism of energy relaxation for photo-excited carriers
in the active region
; if
n\gg n_{h}
, it would be carrier-carrier scattering that dominates energy relaxation in the active region. For instance,
n_{h}\sim 10^{18}cm^{-3}
for GaAs.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
We assume throughout this paper that optical phonons are the primary energy relaxers in the active region. Because
the typical carrier-optical-phonon scattering time
\tau^{o}\sim 100fs
,
Lundstrom 2010
;
Na et al. 2019
which is far smaller than the interband recombination time
(\tau_{\text{rec}}\sim 1ns
),
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
;
Esipov and Levinson 1987
the majority of carriers would relax into the passive region where they await recombination.
44
44

44

Because
\hbar\Omega_{o}\sim 30meV
, and a typical band width
\sim 1eV
, it takes at most thirty emissions of optical phonons for a hot carrier to relax into the active region.
30\times 100fs
is still much less than
\tau_{\text{rec}}
.
In other words, the steady electron (hole) distribution in the passive region accounts for most of the electrons in the conduction band (resp. holes in the valence band), as illustrated in Fig.
3
(c).

It is also useful to identify the secondary/subdominant mechanism for energy relaxation in the active region; the two candidates are carrier-carrier scattering and carrier-acoustic-phonon scattering. We assume that the subdominant mechanism in the active region is also the dominant mechanism for energy relaxation in the passive region, where carrier-optical-phonon scattering ‘switches off’ discontinuously.
Let us define a second density
n_{l}\ll n_{h}
, such that in the intermediate density range:
n_{l}\ll n\lesssim n_{h}
(e.g.,
10^{13}cm^{-3}\ll n\lesssim 10^{18}cm^{-3}
for GaAs), carrier-carrier scattering is the subdominant relaxer in the active region; and in the low-density regime:
n\ll n_{l}
, carrier-acoustic-phonon scattering is the subdominant relaxer in the active region.

For at least a number of semiconductors, steady-state measurements of hot-carrier photoluminescence spectra
45
45

45

For instance, see Ref.
Shah and Leite 1969
and Ref.
Meneses et al. 1973
; more experiments are reviewed in Ref.
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
and Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
.
support the hypothesis that photo-excited electrons in the passive region largely follow a nondegenerate Maxwellian distribution with a source-dependent chemical potential
\mu_{e}
and electron temperature
T_{e}
; likewise, the majority of photo-excited holes in the passive region are Maxwellian with parameters
\mu_{h}
and
T_{h}
.
\mu_{e}
and
T_{e}
are distinct from the equilibrium chemical potential and temperature:
T_{e}
simply equals the non-equilibrium lattice temperature
T_{l}
if electron-acoustic-phonon scattering is the dominant energy relaxer in the passive region (
n\ll n_{l}
); however,
T_{e}
may exceed
T_{l}
if electron-electron scattering is the dominant energy relaxer in the passive region (
n_{l}\ll n\lesssim n_{h}
). Typically, both
k_{B}T_{e}
and
k_{B}T_{h}<\hbar\Omega_{o}
, so most of the photo-excited carriers occupy only a smaller fraction of the passive region, and recombination transitions predominantly occur between electrons (with energy
\lesssim k_{B}T_{e}
) and holes (with energy
\lesssim k_{B}T_{h}
). In large part, the theory that is presented in the main text is agnostic about fine-grained details of the carrier distribution within the passive region, meaning the theory is generally applicable whether or not a Maxwellian distribution develops in the passive region. However, if it does develop, then explicit kinetic models can be constructed that are based on the diffusive approximation for energy relaxation; cf. App.
B.2.2
and App.
E
.

B.2
The kinetic model in the low-density regime

We will introduce a kinetic model that holds in the low-density regime (
n\ll n_{l}
) and forms the basis for numerical simulations of the BIS formula in App.
C.2
.

The collisional integral for a quasiparticle in a two-band semiconductor may decomposed into vertical photon-mediated transitions and intraband phonon-mediated transitions:

\displaystyle{I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}+I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon};\;\;\;\;I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}=-I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}+I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}.}

(52)

The photon-mediated component can be further decomposed into an excitation rate [Eq. (
46
)] and recombination rate [Eq. (
49
)], depending on whether
\boldsymbol{k}
lies on the excitation surface or not:

\displaystyle{I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}=&\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},ES}I^{m_{s}}_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}-(1-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},ES})\sum_{m}^{phot}I^{m}_{rec\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(53)

The phonon-mediated component can be decomposed into incoming transitions that increase the quasiparticle occupancy and outgoing transitions that decrease the quasiparticle occupancy:

\displaystyle{I_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}=&\;\sum_{m}^{phon}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big({\cal A}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+{\cal E}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-{\cal A}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}-{\cal E}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\big),}

(54)

with
{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
defined in Eq. (
27
).
Each of
I_{\text{exc}},I_{\text{rec}},{\cal A}
and
{\cal E}
depends on the quasiparticle distribution
f_{B}
; this distribution is is defined to be steady if it sets the collisional integral to zero:

\displaystyle{\text{Steady distribution:}\;\;\;\;I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{B}]=I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{B}]=0\;\;\;\;\text{for all}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{k}.}

(55)

Let us first address the contribution to
I_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}
by carrier-optical-phonon scattering, which is assumed to be the dominant energy relaxation mechanism in the active region; cf. App.
B.1
. As justified in App.
A.4
, one may neglect the absorption and stimulated emission of optical phonons, retaining only the transition rate for spontaneous emission:
{\cal A}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-{\cal E}^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow-{\cal E}^{sp,m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
{\cal E}^{sp}
defined in Eq. (
48
). We assume that small-angle polarization scattering predominates over deformation scattering.
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
For simplicity in modelling, we focus on polarization scattering by a single branch of longitudinal optical phonons, in which case the phonon mode
m
is fully specified by a phonon wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
; for small
\boldsymbol{q}
, the phonon frequency is assumed to be approximately a constant equal to
\Omega_{o}
. The asymptotic expression for the collisional integral is then given by:
46
46

46

For general expressions, see Eq. (3.12) in Ref.
Vogl 1976
and the discussion in Sec.
III
. For the specific case of longitudinal optical phonons, Sec 1.3.E in Ref.
Mahan 1990
contains a concise derivation.

\displaystyle{\text{Optical phonon:}\;\;\;\;&I^{o}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big({\cal E}^{sp,\boldsymbol{q}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-{\cal E}^{sp,\boldsymbol{q}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\big),{\\
}{\cal E}^{sp,\boldsymbol{q}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|V^{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\delta(E_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\Omega_{o})\big(1-f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big)f_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};{\\
}|V^{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}=|V^{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}\approx\frac{\hbar}{2\pi}\frac{\zeta a}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{\left|\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}\right|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}}.}

(56)

\zeta
is a coupling parameter with dimensions of energy over time.
The inner product of cell-periodic Bloch functions is related to the quantum metric tensor
Provost and Vallee 1980
;
Resta, R. 2011
as

\displaystyle{|\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}|^{2}=&\;1-\delta k_{i}\delta k_{j}g_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{ij}+O(\delta k^{3});\;\;\;\;g_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{ij}=\real\big\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{i}u_{b}\,|\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{j}u_{b}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}-A_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}^{i}A_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}^{j};\;\;\;\;\delta\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k},}

(57)

with
\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}
the intraband Berry connection.
47
47

47

Recently, the electron-phonon coupling has been related to an orbital-projected analog of the Fubini-Study metric.
jiabin_nontrivialquantum
Below room temperature (
k_{B}T_{l}\ll\hbar\Omega_{o}
), spontaneous emission of optical phonons dominates over stimulated emission and absorption, meaning we drop all terms in Eq. (
56
) that are proportional to the Planck occupancy:
N_{\boldsymbol{q}}^{T_{l}}\ll 1
.

Next we attend to the contribution to
I_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}
by carrier-acoustic-phonon scattering, which has been assumed to be the subdominant energy relaxation mechanism in the active region; cf. App.
B.1
. Deformation scattering with acoustic phonons is typically outweighed by piezo-acoustic scattering.
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987

The precise expression of the transition rate/matrix element for piezo-acoustic scattering will not be required, and because we will eventually employ a diffusive Fokker-Planck approximation to the collisional integral. For now, it is worth knowing that the matrix element depends on the quasiparticle band index only through:
48
48

48

See Eq. (3.15) and (3.16) in Ref.
Vogl 1976
, bearing in mind a remark made in Sec.
III
about a missing factor.

\displaystyle{|V^{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k},p}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\propto|\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}|^{2},}

(58)

just as for polarization scattering with optical phonons in Eq. (
56
).

B.2.1
Electron-hole symmetric kinetic model

Because it is numerically intensive to simulate a steady distribution that satisfies
I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{B}]=I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{B}]=0
for all
\boldsymbol{k}
, we will resort to two model assumptions. The first is that band energies and electron-phonon-scattering matrix elements are

\displaystyle{\text{Electron-hole symmetric}:\;\;\;\;E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;|V^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},c\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}=|V^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k},v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2},}

(59)

with
-m
being the momentum-inverted counterpart of
m
.
This symmetry condition ensures for the phononic transition rates that

\displaystyle{{\cal A}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}[1-f_{v},1-f_{c}]={\cal A}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},f_{v}];\;\;\;\;{\cal E}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}[1-f_{v},1-f_{c}]={\cal E}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},f_{v}],}

(60)

as may be verified by inspecting Eq. (
27
); the meaning of
{\cal A}[1-f_{v},1-f_{c}]
is to replace
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow 1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
and
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow 1-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
for all terms in
{\cal A}
, and for all
\boldsymbol{k}
. The photonic transition rate satisfies:
I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}[1-f_{v},1-f_{c}]=I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}[f_{c},f_{v}]
, even without assuming electron-hole symmetry; cf. Eq. (
59
). Altogether,

\displaystyle{I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}[1-f_{v},1-f_{c}]=&\;I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phot}[f_{c},f_{v}]+\sum_{m}^{phon}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big({\cal A}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}+{\cal E}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}-{\cal A}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-{\cal E}^{-m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big)_{f_{c},f_{v}}=-I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},f_{v}].}

(61)

Thus the steady-state condition
I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}=0
is solved by an electron-hole-symmetric distribution:
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
. In particular, if
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
is found such that
I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},1-f_{c}]=0
, then it is guaranteed that
I_{v\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},1-f_{c}]=0
.

Let us check that our model for the quasiparticle Hamiltonian [
H(\boldsymbol{k})
in Eq. (
13
)] and carrier-phonon scattering [Eqs. (
54
)-(
56
)], is electron-hole-symmetric in the sense of Eq. (
59
). Suppose that the conduction- and valence-band wave functions are related by an anti-unitary operation:
|\,u_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=\hat{C}|\,u_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
, which implies
\big\langle u_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle=\big\langle u_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle
. Because the electron-phonon matrix element (for both polarization and piezo-acoustic scatterings) only depends on the band index through
|\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle|^{2}
[cf. Eq. (
58
)],
|V^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k},c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}=|V^{m}_{v\boldsymbol{k},v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}
. Applying a general property of electon-phonon matrix elements:
V^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k},b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\overline{V^{-m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}}
[cf. Eq. (
88
)], one obtains the second equation in Eq. (
59
). For the specific model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
), the anti-unitary operation is simply
\hat{C}=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K
, with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}
the second Pauli matrix and
K
implementing complex conjugation. More generally, any
H(\boldsymbol{k})
that is a sum of Pauli matrices satisfies

\displaystyle{\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}\overline{H(\boldsymbol{k})}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}=-H(\boldsymbol{k});\;\;\;\;E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(62)

The last condition further implies that the excitation surface is an iso-energy surface:

\displaystyle{0=E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{s}=2E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{s},}

(63)

for any source radiation frequency
\omega_{s}
. In other words, the set of excitation energies
E_{c,exc}
defined in Eq. (
43
) is just a single energy.

B.2.2
Iso-energy symmetric kinetic model

To recapitulate, we want to numerically simulate an electron-hole-symmetric distribution
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
such that
I_{c\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},1-f_{c}]=0
. Having reduced the problem to a single band by electron-hole symmetry, one may as well drop the band index on
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
,
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
E_{c,exc}\rightarrow E_{\text{exc}}
. We further redefine
E=0
to be the minimal energy for the conduction band.

To simplify the simulation of
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, we further assume that
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is approximately
iso-energy symmetric
, meaning that
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is approximately constant under variation of
\boldsymbol{k}
within an iso-energy surface for
E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
:

\displaystyle{f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx&\;f_{E};\;\;\;\;f_{E}=\langle\langle\,f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}{\\
}\langle\langle\,\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}=\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}E}\,\Xi_{\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}E}=\frac{\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)}{{\cal V}g_{E}},}

with
g_{E}
meaning the density of conduction-band states per unit volume (
{\cal V}
) and per spin orientation.
We refer to
\langle\langle\,\ldots\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}
as
iso-energy averaging
, and

f_{E}
as the
iso-energy-averaged distribution
. For the purpose of computing the shift current,
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx f_{E}
is justified to the extent that the collisional integral in Eq. (
52
) is iso-energy symmetric:
I_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx I_{E}=\langle\langle\,I_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}
, which constrains the model Hamiltonians that we allow ourselves to numerically simulate.
49
49

49

In general, it should be expected that the non-equilibrium distribution is iso-energy asymmetric with respect to inverting
\boldsymbol{k}
:
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq f_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
. This is possible because the continuous absorption of photons creates a non-equilibrium state with a direction for time. Consequently, the asymmetry (
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c,-\boldsymbol{k}}
) is proportional to the light intensity
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
and contributes to a ‘ballistic current’
Belinicher and Sturman 1980
but not the shift current.

By averaging the kinetic equation
I_{\boldsymbol{k}}[f_{c},1-f_{c}]=0
over an iso-energy surface, one obtains:
50
50

50

The following kinetic equation is very similar to one studied in Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1984
; however, we would rather not presume they adopted the same premises as we have adopted.

\displaystyle{E>E_{cut}:\;\;\;\;g_{E}I_{E}[f]=G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}[f]\,\delta(E-E_{\text{exc}})-\frac{g_{E}f_{E}}{\tau^{o}_{E}}+\frac{g_{E_{+}}f_{E_{+}}}{\tau^{o}_{E_{+}}}-\frac{g_{E}f_{E}}{\tau_{\text{rec}}}-\partial_{E}j_{E}^{s}=0;\;\;\;\;E_{\pm}=E\pm\hbar\Omega_{o}.}

(64)

We will explain the terms on the right-hand side in turn:

(i) Recalling the excitation energy
E_{\text{exc}}
[Eq. (
43
)] to be the energy of conduction-band states on the excitation surface,
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\,\delta(E-E_{\text{exc}})
is the rate of increase in the quasiparticle number density
g_{E}f_{E}
due to the absorption of source-generated photons.
51
51

51

G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\,\delta(E-E_{\text{exc}})
is derived by applying
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{\text{exc}})/{\cal V}
to
I^{phot}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
53
)] and retaining terms which are proportional to the source photon number
\Delta N_{s}
; cf. Eq. (
34
).
In other words,
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}[f]
is the rate at which source-generated photons are absorbed per unit volume and per spin orientation;

\displaystyle{G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}[f]=\frac{\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}[f]{\cal I}_{rad}}{\hbar\omega}}

(65)

can be expressed as a product of the single-spin absorption coefficient and the radiation intensity, divided by the source photon energy.

(ii)
-gf/\tau^{o}\big|_{E}
(resp.
+gf/\tau^{o}\big|_{E_{+}}
) in Eq. (
64
) represents an outflow (resp. inflow) of electrons due to spontaneous emission of optical phonons.
\tau^{o}_{E}
is the average time for a quasiparticle with energy
E
to spontaneously emit an optical phonon; in the passive region (
E<\hbar\Omega_{o}
), spontaneous emission is forbidden by energy conservation, hence we set
\tau^{o}_{E}=\infty
. One may relate
\tau^{o}_{E}
to the collisional integral
I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}
by

\displaystyle\frac{g_{E_{+}}f_{E_{+}}}{\tau^{o}_{E_{+}}}-\frac{g_{E}f_{E}}{\tau^{o}_{E}}=\frac{\zeta a}{{\cal V}^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{cut}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)\frac{\left|\big\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}\right|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|^{2}}\bigg\{f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-E_{+})-f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-E_{-})\bigg\}.

(66)

The right-hand side is obtained by applying
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)/{\cal V}
to the component of
I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{phon}
corresponding to spontaneous emission of optical phonons [cf. Eq. (
56
)] and dropping all terms which are nonlinear in the quasiparticle distribution:
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\ll f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
; bear in mind that nondegenerate fermion statistics (
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\ll 1
) apply to a wide range of continuous-wave laser experiments.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
Because we are employing an asymptotic expression that is valid for small-angle scattering, we have introduced a cutoff in
\sum^{cut}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
, so that
\delta k=|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|
is much less than the linear dimension of the Brillouin zone.

(iii)
-{g_{E}f_{E}}/{\tau_{\text{rec}}}
represents the quasiparticle loss rate due to interband recombination by spontaneous emission of photons. The effects of absorption and stimulated emission of thermal photons are negligible, as was explained in App.
A.4
. In numerical simulations, we just take
{\tau_{\text{rec}}}\sim 1ns
to be
a typical, energy-averaged time scale for interband recombination.
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
;
Esipov and Levinson 1987
. In principle, one could refine the model by replacing
{\tau_{\text{rec}}}\rightarrow{\tau_{E}^{\text{rec}}}
, with
\tau^{\text{rec}}
depending on
E
through the energy dependences of the dipole matrix element and
f_{E}
.
52
52

52

{g_{E}f_{E}}/{\tau_{E}^{\text{rec}}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{\text{exc}}){\cal E}^{sp,m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}/{\cal V}
, with the spontaneous emission rate defined in Eq. (
50
).

In practice, what matters to the
shift current is the order-of-magnitude difference:
\tau^{\text{rec}}_{E}\gg\gg\tau^{o}_{E}
