, which guarantees that recombination transitions predominantly occur in the passive region, independent of the precise energy dependence of
\tau^{\text{rec}}
; cf. Sec.
II
and Eq. (
50
).

(iv)
-\partial_{E}j_{E}^{s}
is the rate of change of
g_{E}f_{E}
induced by electron-acoustic-phonon scattering. A negative
j_{E}^{s}
represents a scattering-induced relaxation of the number density
g_{E}f_{E}
toward decreasing energies, so we refer to
j_{E}^{s}
as the
energy-axis current
. In principle, this current should be an integral of
f_{E}
; however, the smallness of acoustic-phonon energies relative to typical electron energies allows to employ the
diffusive Fokker-Planck approximation:
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
;
Lifshitz and Pitaevskii 1979

\displaystyle{j_{E}^{s}=-\frac{g_{E}E}{\tau^{s}_{E}}\bigg(1+k_{B}T_{l}\partial_{E}\bigg)f_{E}.}

(67)

E/\tau^{s}_{E}
is the dynamic friction coefficient,
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
which is interpretable as minus the ‘drift speed’ of a number-density-valued wavepacket on the energy axis. The form of
(1+k_{B}T_{l}\partial_{E})
encodes an Einstein relation between the dynamic friction coefficient and the diffusion coefficient.
53
53

53

For electron-acoustic-phonon scattering, the Einstein relation is derived most directly from simplified expressions in Section 4.5 of Ref.
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
, assuming that
k_{B}T_{l}\gg
the acoustic phonon energy. In a subsequent discussion in App.
E
, we will also need an analogous Einstein relation for electron-electron scattering, which has been derived in Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
.

Our previous assumption that energy relaxation is dominated by optical phonons can now be expressed as a mathematical inequality, namely that the dynamic friction coefficient is much less than the energy relaxation rate due to spontaneous emission of optical phonons:

\displaystyle{E>\hbar\Omega_{o}:\;\;\;\;\eta_{E}=\frac{E}{\hbar\Omega_{o}}\frac{\tau^{o}_{E}}{\tau^{s}_{E}}\ll 1.}

(68)

The diffusive approximation is valid on the conditions that the density of states is analytic and the collisions are quasi-elastic. The former condition rules out van Hove singularities.
Van Hove 1953
The latter condition means precisely that the change in a quasiparticle’s energy (due to a collision) is much less than the quasiparticle’s initial energy
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
. This holds for most quasiparticle energies, since acoustic-phonon energies are a very small fraction of the quasiparticle band width. We introduce a cutoff energy
E_{cut}
which is comparable to the typical acoustic phonon energy, such that the diffusive approximation holds for
E>E_{cut}
.

For energies less than the cutoff, we adopt the following kinetic equation:

\displaystyle{0<E<E_{cut}:\;\;\;\;g_{E}I_{E}[f]=\frac{g_{E_{+}}f_{E_{+}}}{\tau^{o}_{E_{+}}}-\frac{g_{E}f_{E}}{\tau_{\text{rec}}}-\frac{j_{E_{cut}}^{s}}{E_{cut}}=0.}

(69)

-j_{E_{cut}}^{s}
represents a (downward
\equiv
energy-relaxing) current of the number density
g_{E}f_{E}

across the cutoff energy [cf. Eq. (
67
)]; any density that relaxes across the cutoff is equally distributed between all conduction-band states below the cutoff.
54
54

54

The
-j_{E_{cut}}^{s}/E_{cut}
term can be viewed as a collisional term
g_{E}I^{s}_{E}[f]
due to the secondary scattering mechanism. The crudeness in our approximation lies in assuming
g_{E}I^{s}_{E}[f]
is independent of
E
, for
E
below the cutoff. This amounts to assuming that
I^{s}_{E}
is independent of
E
, because the density of states is energy-independent for a quasi-2D parabolic band.
This crude modelling of scattering below the cutoff can in principle be improved upon, but we remind the reader that the steady shift current is insensitive to fine details of the quasiparticle distribution within the passive region, owing to arguments explained in Sec.
II
. Despite the crudeness of the model, the model ensures that all phonon-mediated collisions conserve the total number of quasiparticles within the conduction band. In other words, if all the collisional terms in Eqs. (
64
)-(
69
), with the exception of terms involving
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}
and
\tau_{\text{rec}}
, are collectively denoted as
g_{E}I^{\text{intra}}_{E}
, then
\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{E}I^{\text{intra}}_{E}dE=0
.

Appendix C
Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman formula for the shift current

C.1
Derivation of the Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman formula

It has been expressed to the authors that the Belinicher-Ivchenko-Sturman theory
Belinicher et al. 1982
is difficult to penetrate. To our knowledge, no explicit derivation of the BIS formula yet exists in the literature. We will therefore derive their main formulas for pedagogy. Precisely, we mean to derive the form of the phonon-mediated (resp. photon-mediated) shift current to be
Eq. (
25
), with the phononic (resp. photonic) shift vector given in Eq. (
26
) [resp. Eq. (
28
)], and with the difference in absorption and emission transition rates given by Eq. (
27
) [resp. Eq. (
28
)].

Since the BIS formula encodes the spontaneous emission of photons, the derivation requires to quantize the radiation field. If one were to quantize the radiation field but retain a first-quantized electron description, one would derive an analog of the BIS formula that is only applicable to nondegenerate Fermi statistics, i.e., one would miss a spontaneous-emission term that is nonlinear in the distribution function [cf. Eq. (
28
) below]. To properly account for the Pauli exclusion principle in the presence of spontaneous emission, it is necessary to apply second quantization to the electron. It is fortuitous but misleading that terms which are nonlinear in the distribution function cancel out if one considers only photon absorption and stimulated emission [Eq. (
28
) with
N_{m}+1\approx N_{m}
]; thus it has been possible for theories (based on first quantization of the electron and a classical theory of radiation) to neglect the exclusion principle and yet derive correct formulas for the transient shift current, as will be elaborated in App.
C.3
.

Our derivation also manifests how a perturbation theory of the steady state differs dramatically from a perturbation theory of the transient state. Most practitioners who calculate nonlinear optical responses are calculating the transient response, and their zeroth-order state is the thermal equilibrium state in the absence of the light source. In
steady-state perturbation theory
, the zeroth-order state is emphatically
not
a thermal state; instead, App.
C.1.5
proves rigorously that if the state is steady, the zeroth-order quasiparticle distribution satisfies a non-detailed balance condition that represents an invariance under simultaneous collisions with all bosons. Moreover, we have no reason to believe that the zeroth-order state in steady perturbation theory is perturbatively connected to the thermal equilibrium state (in the absence of the light source).

The outline of the derivation is:

(i) App.
C.1.1
sets up the problem and establishes the notation. We review salient properties of the independent-electron Hamiltonian, the crystal momentum representation, the independent-boson Hamiltonian, Fock space and the electron-boson interaction. Finally, we express the shift current in terms of stationary density matrices, and derive a perturbative expression for the stationary density matrix in the Lippmann-Schwinger scattering formalism.

(ii) The Lippman-Schwinger formula for the stationary density matrix is expressed in terms of second-quantized matrix elements; these elements will be reduced to first-quantized matrix elements in App.
C.1.2
. The result of this reduction is an intermediate formula for the photonic and phononic shift current in Eq. (
109
) and Eq. (
117
), respectively. These intermediate formulas are more formal than optimal: they are expressed in terms of an infinite number of band-off-diagonal matrix elements of the position operator.

(iii) App.
C.1.3
derives an optimal expression for the photonic shift current, with help from a sum rule derived from the first-quantized commutation relation between position and canonical momentum.

(iv) App.
C.1.4
derives an optimal expression for the phononic shift current, with help from a sum rule derived from the first-quantized commutation relation between position and the phonon-induced potential-energy field.

(v) App.
C.1.5
demonstrates that the zeroth-order density matrix is not thermal; instead, the zeroth-order quasiparticle distribution satisfies a non-detailed balance condition that represents an invariance under simultaneous collisions with all bosons.

C.1.1
Preliminaries

We decompose our Hamiltonian into two independendent-particle terms and an electron-boson interaction:

\displaystyle{H=H_{0}+U,\;\;\;\;H_{0}=H_{0}^{ele}+H_{0}^{bos}.}

(70)

We will first explain the independent-particle terms:

Independent-electron Hamiltonian and the crystal momentum representation

H_{0}^{ele}
is a mean-field Hamiltonian for independent electrons in a crystalline medium:

\displaystyle{H_{0}^{ele}=\sum_{B}E_{B}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B},\;\;\;\;[{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B},c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}}]=\delta_{B,B^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;B=(b,\boldsymbol{k}),\;\;\;\;B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}

(71)

where
[x,y]=xy-yx
(the commutator) and
B
is a collective index for both the band label and crystal wavevector. We assume throughout this work that spin-orbit coupling is negligible; to simplify notation,
b
should be understood as a spinless band label, and
H
as a Hamiltonian in one spin sector; only in the final steps will the current be multiplied by two to account for the spin degeneracy of bands.

c_{B}
annihilates an electronic state with a wave function of the Bloch form:
e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r})/\sqrt{{\cal V}}
, with
u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r})=u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{R})
being periodic in Bravais-lattice translations and
{\cal V}
the volume of the medium. These cell-periodic functions are normalized as

\displaystyle{\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=\delta_{b,b^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;\big\langle X\,|\,Y\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=\int\frac{d\boldsymbol{\tau}}{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\overline{X(\boldsymbol{\tau})}{Y(\boldsymbol{\tau})},}

(72)

with
\boldsymbol{\tau}
the intracell coordinate,
\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}
a Kronecker delta function for the band labels, and
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
the real-space volume of the primitive unit cell. The orthonormality and completeness of our basis of Bloch waves reads as

\displaystyle{\big\langle B\,|\,B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{1}=\delta_{B,B^{\prime}}=\delta_{bb^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;I_{1}=\sum_{B}|\,B\big\rangle\big\langle B\,|_{1}.}

(73)

I
is the identity operator, and the superscript
1
in Eq. (
73
) reminds us that we are dealing with a first-quantized, one-particle Hilbert space.

Our notation for
\boldsymbol{k}
suggests misleadingly that
\boldsymbol{k}
is a discrete wavevector:
\sum_{B}=\sum_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\delta_{B,B^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}
. However, for the position operator to have a well-defined action on periodic Bloch states, one must take
{\cal V}
to be infinite,
Blount 1962
hence
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})/{\cal V}
should be understood as a shorthand for a Dirac delta function, and we will be applying certain identities that apply to Dirac delta functions but not Kronecker delta functions:

\displaystyle{&\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(74)

\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}
should also be understood as an integral over the Brillouin zone:
{\cal V}\int_{BZ}d^{3}k/(2\pi)^{3}
. With these caveats in mind, we present
the first-quantized position, canonical momentum and velocity operators in the crystal momentum representation:
Blount 1962

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{r}_{BB^{\prime}}=&\;\big\langle B\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\,|\,B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{1}=i\delta_{bb^{\prime}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}u_{b^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}{\\
}\boldsymbol{p}_{BB^{\prime}}=\;\big\langle B\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\,|\,B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{1}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{P}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{P}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=m_{f}\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}},{\\
}\boldsymbol{v}_{BB^{\prime}}=\;\big\langle B\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,|\,B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{1}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}.}

(75)

We have assumed in the absence of spin-orbit coupling that
\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}/m_{f}
, with
m_{f}
the free-electron mass. It is also worth defining the band-off-diagonal position operator as

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\text{off}}=&\;\sum_{BB^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{r}^{\text{off}}_{BB^{\prime}}|\,B\big\rangle\big\langle B^{\prime}\,|_{1};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{r}^{\text{off}}_{BB^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}(1-\delta_{bb^{\prime}}),}

(76)

which is related to the band-off-diagonal elements of the velocity operator:
Blount 1962

\displaystyle{\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{\text{off}}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}}{E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(77)

Independent-boson Hamiltonian

H_{0}^{bos}
is the independent-boson Hamiltonian absent the zero-point energy:

\displaystyle{H_{0}^{bos}=\sum_{m}\hbar\omega_{m}a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m}{a}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{m^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;{a}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{m}a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m^{\prime}}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m^{\prime}}{a}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{m}=\delta_{m,m^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;m=(\boldsymbol{q},p),}

(78)

where the index
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
runs over both photonic and phononic modes.

We follow E. Fermi’s prescription
Fermi 1932
in quantizing the transverse/solenoidal component of the electromagnetic vector potential in the Coulomb gauge.
Heitler 1954
;
Sakurai 1967
For photons,
\boldsymbol{q}
is a wavevector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with a cutoff:
\hbar cq<E_{cut}
; the cutoff energy may be taken as the largest energy difference between the Bloch bands which are excited by the light source.
55
55

55

This cutoff is imposed for self-consistency: our use of the dipole approximation requires that
q
is much less than the linear dimension of the BZ.

p\in\{1,2\}
specifies one of the two possible transverse polarizations for a given
\boldsymbol{q}
; we adopt a linearly polarized basis, meaning the polarization vector is real-valued:
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\equiv\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}^{(p)}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}^{(p)}\in\mathbb{R}
. The photon frequency is polarization-independent:
\omega_{m}=cq
with
q=||\boldsymbol{q}||
.

For phonons,
\boldsymbol{q}
is a wavevector in the BZ, and
p=3,4,\ldots,3N_{nuc}+2
a label for a nondegenerate phonon band, with
N_{nuc}
being the number of nuclei per primitive unit cell.
\omega_{\boldsymbol{q}p}=\omega_{-\boldsymbol{q}p}
is the renormalized phonon dispersion.
Sham and Ziman 1963
;
Keating 1968

Altogether,
\sum_{m}=\sum_{p}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}
with
p
running over
3N_{nuc}+2
values, and
\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}={\cal V}\int d^{3}q/(2\pi)^{3}
with the integration domain depending on
p
, and

\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}
should be understood as
\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}^{\prime}}\delta_{p,p^{\prime}}
.

Fock space

Eigenstates of the independent-particle Hamiltonian are labelled by electronic occupancies
n_{B}\in\{0,1\}
and bosonic occupancies
N_{m}\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
:

\displaystyle{(H_{0}-E_{\mu})|\,\mu\,\rangle=0,\;\;\;\;\mu=\bigg(\{n^{\mu}_{B}\}_{B},\{N_{m}^{\mu}\}_{m}\bigg),\;\;\;\;E_{\mu}=\sum_{B}E_{B}n^{\mu}_{B}+\sum_{m}\hbar\omega_{m}N^{\mu}_{m}.}

(79)

Throughout this appendix, Greek symbols (like
\mu
) are used as a collective index for all electronic and bosonic occupancies.
\{n_{B}\}_{B}
means a set of occupancies for all Bloch states, but we will often use the shorthand:
\{n_{B}\}_{B}\rightarrow\{n\}.
Likewise for
\{N_{m}\}_{m}\rightarrow\{N\}.
We will refer to
|\,\mu\,\rangle
as an
independent-particle state
. The set of independent-particle states forms an orthonormal basis (
\big\langle\mu\,|\,\nu\big\rangle=\delta_{\mu,\nu}
) for the combined-electron-boson Fock space. The resolution of identity is given by

\displaystyle{I=\sum_{\mu}|\,\mu\big\rangle\big\langle\mu\,|=\sum_{\{n\}}\sum_{\{N\}}|\,\{n\}\{N\}\big\rangle\big\langle\{n\}\{N\}\,|,\;\;\;\;\sum_{\{n\}}=\prod_{B}\sum_{n_{B}=0}^{1},\;\;\;\;\sum_{\{N\}}=\prod_{m}\sum_{N_{m}=0}^{\infty}.}

(80)

Any operator
O^{e}
with an
e
superscript should be understood as acting only in the electronic Fock space, which is spanned by
independent-electron states
denoted as
|\,\{n\}\,\rangle_{e}
. (The existence or absence of subscripts distinguishes kets in different Hilbert spaces.) We will focus on bilinear electronic operators

\displaystyle{O^{e}=\sum_{B,B^{\prime}}O_{B,B^{\prime}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;O^{e}_{\mu\nu}=\big\langle\{n^{\mu}\}\,|\,O^{e}\,|\,\{n^{\nu}\}\big\rangle_{e},}

(81)

with matrix elements denoted as
O^{e}_{\mu\nu}
; the commutator of two bilinear operators is expressible as:

\displaystyle{[G^{e},O^{e}]=\sum_{BB^{\prime}}[G,O]_{BB^{\prime}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;G^{e}=\sum_{B,B^{\prime}}G_{B,B^{\prime}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}}.}

(82)

Electron-boson interaction

We decompose
U
into a tensor product of operators acting in the electronic and photonic Fock spaces:

\displaystyle{U=\sum_{m}U_{m}^{e}({a}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m});\;\;\;\;U^{e}_{m}=\sum_{B,B^{\prime}}U^{m}_{B,B^{\prime}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}}=(U^{e}_{-m})^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}},}

(83)

with
-m=(-\boldsymbol{q},p)
the momentum-reversed partner of
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
.
U^{e}_{m}=(U^{e}_{-m})^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}
ensures that
U
is self-adjoint. For
m
that is photonic (resp. phononic),
U^{m}_{B,B^{\prime}}
is defined as the
electron-photon
(resp.
electron-phonon
)
matrix element
:

\displaystyle U^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{b\boldsymbol{k},b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\breve{W}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\breve{W}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{he^{2}}{\omega_{\boldsymbol{q}p}{\cal V}}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}^{(p)}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;&(\text{electron-photon})\\
V^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{b\boldsymbol{k},b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}}\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}={\cal V}^{-1}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}^{RL}\widetilde{PE}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}}\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}.\;\;\;\;&(\text{electron-phonon})\end{cases}

(84)

We will describe each matrix element in turn.

The electron-photon matrix element is derived from the first-order term in the non-relativistic minimal coupling:
|e|\boldsymbol{A}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}/c
, with
\boldsymbol{v}
the second-quantized electron velocity operator and
\boldsymbol{A}_{\perp}
the quantized electromagnetic vector potential satisfying
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{\perp}=0
.
Heitler 1954

The photonic expression in Eq. (
84
) is valid in the dipole approximation. Within this approximation,
U^{e}_{m}=U^{e}_{-m}=(U^{e}_{m})^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}
is self-adjoint. Minimal coupling also results in an electron-photon interaction proportional to
e^{2}
, but such a coupling does not contribute to the shift current because it cannot induce interband transitions within the dipole approximation [cf. Eq. (
90
) below].

We adopt a simplified electron-phonon matrix element
\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
which is derived in the adiabatic approximation (where phonons are frozen from the electron’s perspective) and by applying the Hartree approximation to electron-electron interactions.
Vogl 1976
;
Sham and Ziman 1963

In the expression for
\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
,
\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}^{RL}
sums over all reciprocal-lattice vectors,
\boldsymbol{q}
is a wavevector in the Brillouin zone
BZ
and
\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{Q}
a wavevector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
\widetilde{PE}^{m}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-i\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}PE^{m}_{\boldsymbol{r}}d\boldsymbol{r}
is a Fourier transform of the one-electron potential energy
PE^{m}_{\boldsymbol{r}}
induced by annihilating a phonon of mode
m
.
PE_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{m}
is self-consistently
Sham and Ziman 1963
screened in a crystalline medium, and is linearly related to the bare potential energy
PE_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{m;0}
:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{PE}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}}=&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}^{RL}\varepsilon^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}\widetilde{PE}^{\boldsymbol{q}p;0}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}=\overline{\widetilde{PE}^{-\boldsymbol{q}p}_{-\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{G}}},}

(85)

with
\varepsilon^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime}}=\overline{\varepsilon^{-1}_{-\boldsymbol{Q},-\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime}}}
the static, inverse dielectric function in the Hartree approximation.
56
56

56

An explicit expression can be found in Eq. (12.16) of Ref.
Sham and Ziman 1963
.
The bare potential energy is expressible in terms of
\tilde{v}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}=4\pi e^{2}/||\boldsymbol{Q}||^{2}
, the Fourier transform of the Coulomb interaction:

\displaystyle{\widetilde{PE}^{\boldsymbol{q}p;0}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}}=&\;i\tilde{v}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}}\sum^{nuclei}_{j}\bigg(\tfrac{\hbar N_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}Z_{j}^{2}}{2\omega_{\boldsymbol{q}p}M_{j}}\bigg)^{\scriptscriptstyle{1/2}}(\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G})\cdot\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}^{j}e^{-i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{j}}=\overline{\widetilde{PE}^{-\boldsymbol{q}p;0}_{-\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{G}}},}

(86)

with the caveat that
\tilde{v}_{\boldsymbol{0}}=0
to account for the electrical neutrality of the entire medium.
Bardeen and Pines 1955

N_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}={\cal V}/{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
is the number of primitive unit cells;
j
labels the nuclei in one primitive unit cell; a nucleus labelled
j
has a charge
Z_{j}|e|
, mass
M_{j}
, and real-spatial coordinate
\boldsymbol{r}_{j}
;
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}^{j}=\overline{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{-m}^{j}}
is the polarization vector of the
j
’th nucleus.
57
57

57

The above expressions are obtained from equations (2.9) to (2.11) in Ref.
Vogl 1976
.

It is worth defining a first-quantized operator whose matrix elements (with respect to Bloch waves) are identical to the electron-phonon matrix element [Eq. (
84
)]:

\displaystyle{\hat{V}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}={\cal V}^{-1}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}^{RL}{\widetilde{PE}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G}}}{e^{i(\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{G})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}};\;\;\;\;\big\langle B\,|\,\hat{V}^{m}\,|\,B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{1}=V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}.}

(87)

Because
PE^{m}_{\boldsymbol{r}}
is the one-body potential induced by a complex-valued wave (rather than a standing wave), the potential is not real-valued but satisfies
PE^{m}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=\overline{PE^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{r}}}
; moreover,
\hat{V}^{m}
is not self-adjoint:

\displaystyle{(\hat{V}^{m})^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}=\hat{V}^{-m}=\hat{T}\hat{V}^{m}\hat{T}^{-1};\;\;\;\;\overline{V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}}=V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B},}

(88)

with
\hat{T}
being the first-quantized, time-reversal operator.

Shift current in terms of density matrices

We adopt the Schrödinger representation in which
a_{m},a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m},U
and
H
are all time-independent, i.e.,
a_{m}
is not accompanied with the multiplicative factor
e^{-i\omega_{m}t}
. This allows to solve for the stationary density matrix

\displaystyle{\partial_{t}\rho=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\rho]=0,\;\;\;\;\rho=\rho^{(0)}+\rho^{(1)}+\rho^{(2)}+\ldots}

(89)

in time-independent perturbation theory, with
\rho^{(n)}
proportional to the
n
’th power of the perturbation
U
.

Because
\rho
is stationary,
-|e|\text{Tr}[\boldsymbol{v}\rho]
represents a direct current.
-|e|\text{Tr}[\boldsymbol{v}\rho^{(0)}]
represents the direct current in the absence of the light source, and vanishes by time-reversal symmetry.
We will see in Sec.
C.1.2
that
\rho^{(1)}
does not contribute to the direct current, but
\rho^{(2)}
does. The shift current is the second-order direct current contributed by band-off-diagonal elements of the velocity matrix
\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big];\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}=\sum_{b,b^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{v}^{\text{off}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{v}^{\text{off}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}(1-\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}).}

(90)

Band-diagonal elements contribute to the ‘ballistic current’,
Belinicher and Sturman 1980
which we do not touch upon in this work.

Stationary density matrix from the Lippmann-Schwinger formalism

We will derive
\rho^{(n)}
based on the Lippmann-Schwinger scattering formalism,
Lippmann and Schwinger 1950
;
Weinberg 2005
which we briefly review.

For any independent-particle state
|\,\mu\,\rangle
with energy
E_{\mu}
, one can construct an ‘in’ state
|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle
that is an eigenstate of the full Hamiltonian with the same energy:

\displaystyle{(H-E_{\mu})|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle=0;\;\;\;\;|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle=|\,\mu\,\rangle+G^{+}_{E_{\mu}}U|\,\mu\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;G^{+}_{E}=\frac{1}{E-H+i0^{+}},}

(91)

with
G^{+}
the retarded Green’s function and
0^{+}
a positive infinitesimal. An ‘in’ state has the same normalization as its independent-particle counterpart.
Weinberg 2005
Since the set of independent-particle states forms an orthonormal basis, so then does the set of all ‘in’ states:
\big\langle\mu_{+}\,|\,\nu_{+}\big\rangle=\delta_{\mu,\nu}
.

Let us motivate the imaginary infinitesimal by a wave packet interpretation proposed in Ref.
Weinberg 2005
. The above correspondence between
|\,\mu\,\rangle
and
|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle
allows to parametrize
|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle
by the one-particle wavevectors
(\boldsymbol{k}_{1},\boldsymbol{k}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{2},\ldots
) of electrons and bosons that make up
|\,\mu\,\rangle
. Thus it is possible to form a wave packet by smoothly linearly combining
|\,\mu_{+}\,\rangle
with slightly different values for the one-particle wavevectors. The
i0^{+}
guarantees that such a wave packet behaves essentially as a superposition of independent particles in the far past:
t\rightarrow-\infty
.
58
58

58

One can construct ‘out’ states by flipping the sign of
i0^{+}
, such that the wave packet becomes essentially non-interacting in the far future. This wave packet interpretation is elaborated in Chapter 3 of Ref.
Weinberg 2005
. In other derivations of the conductivity,
Holder et al. 2020
;
Gao et al. 2021

i0^{+}
appears as a result of an adiabatic turn-on process in accordance with Kubo tradition,
Kubo 1957
yet no such adiabatic process exists in the typical experiment, e.g., with lasers.
The use of ‘in’ states thus simulates a scattering process in which localized wavepackets of electrons and bosons are initially separated (in real space) but subsequently approach each other, and in so doing evolves into an entangled, polaritonic/polaronic state with a nontrivial current.

Let us construct a density matrix by summing over outer products of ‘in’ states weighted by probability coefficients
F_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle{\rho=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}|\,\mu_{+}\big\rangle\big\langle\mu_{+}\,|;\;\;\;\;1=&\;\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}.}

(92)

Because the ‘in’ state is an eigenstate of
H
,
\rho
satisfies the stationary condition in Eq. (
89
).
By iteratively expanding the Green’s function in a perturbative series

\displaystyle{G^{+}=G^{+}_{0}+G^{+}_{0}UG^{+}_{0}+G^{+}_{0}UG^{+}_{0}UG^{+}_{0}+\ldots;\;\;\;\;G^{+}_{0;E}=\frac{1}{E-H_{0}+i0^{+}},}

(93)

one obtains a perturbative series for the density matrix:

\displaystyle{\rho=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}|\,\mu_{+}\big\rangle\big\langle\mu_{+}\,|=\rho^{(0)}+\rho^{(1)}+\rho^{(2)}+\ldots;\;\;\;\;\rho^{(0)}=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}|\,\mu\big\rangle\big\langle\mu\,|.}

(94)

Because the zeroth-order component
\rho^{(0)}
is stationary with respect to the non-interacting Hamiltonian
H_{0}
, one may as well
take
F_{\mu}
to be a product of one-particle probabilities
p_{n_{B}}
and
P_{N_{m}}
:

\displaystyle{F_{\mu}=p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}P_{\{N^{\mu}\}},\;\;\;\;p_{\{n\}}=\prod_{B}p_{n_{B}},\;\;\;\;P_{\{N\}}=\prod_{m}P_{N_{m}}.}

(95)

The sense in which
p
and
P
are one-particle probabilities is that

\displaystyle{1=&\;\sum_{n_{B}=0}^{1}p_{n_{B}}=\sum_{N_{m}=0}^{\infty}P_{N_{m}};\;\;\;\;\left\langle n_{B}\right\rangle=\sum_{n_{B}=0}^{1}p_{n_{B}}n_{B}=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}n_{B}^{\mu};\;\;\;\;\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle=\sum_{N_{m}=0}^{\infty}P_{N_{m}}N_{m}=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}N_{m}^{\mu},}

(96)

with
\left\langle n_{B}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle
being the average number of electrons and bosons with the one-particle labels
B
and
m
, respectively. In a generic, non-equilibrium state,
p_{n_{B}}
does not have the Fermi-Dirac form, and instead satisfies a non-detailed balance condition that represents an invariance under simultaneous collisions with all bosons, as detailed in App.
C.1.5
.

It is convenient to introduce the shorthand

\displaystyle{F_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu}-F_{\nu},\;\;\;\;E_{\mu\nu}=E_{\mu}-E_{\nu},}

(97)

and express
\rho^{(1)}
and
\rho^{(2)}
in terms of their matrix elements in the independent-particle basis:

\displaystyle{\rho^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}=&\;\big\langle\mu\,|\,\rho^{(1)}\,|\,\nu\big\rangle=\frac{F_{\mu\nu}U_{\mu\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}{\\
}\rho^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}=\;\sum_{\lambda}U_{\mu\lambda}U_{\lambda\nu}\bigg[\frac{F_{\lambda}}{(E_{\lambda\mu}+i0^{+})(E_{\lambda\nu}-i0^{+})}+\frac{F_{\nu}}{(E_{\nu\mu}+i0^{+})(E_{\nu\lambda}+i0^{+})}+\frac{F_{\mu}}{(E_{\mu\lambda}-i0^{+})(E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+})}\bigg]{\\
}=\;\sum_{\lambda}\frac{U_{\mu\lambda}U_{\lambda\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}\bigg[\frac{F_{\mu\lambda}}{E_{\mu\lambda}-i0^{+}}+\frac{F_{\nu\lambda}}{E_{\lambda\nu}-i0^{+}}\bigg].}

C.1.2
From second-quantized matrix elements to first-quantized matrix elements

We need only concern ourselves with matrix elements
\rho^{(n)}_{\mu\nu}
with
|\,\mu\,\rangle
and
|\,\nu\,\rangle
having identical occupations numbers for all bosonic modes.
After all, for any operator
O=O^{e}\otimes(identity)
that acts trivially in the bosonic Fock space,

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}[O\rho]=\sum_{\mu\nu}O_{\nu\mu}\rho_{\mu\nu}\delta_{\{N^{\mu}\},\{N^{\nu}\}}.}

(98)

In particular, Eq. (
98
) holds for
O
being the electronic velocity operator
\boldsymbol{v}
. An immediate implication is that
\rho^{(1)}
does not contribute to the direct current:
\text{Tr}[\boldsymbol{v}\rho^{(1)}]=0
, because
\rho^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\propto U_{\mu\nu}
[cf. Eq. (
C.1.1
)] and
U
necessarily changes the boson number; cf. Eq. (
83
).

Let us apply Eq. (
98
) to the shift current [Eq. (
90
)] with
\rho^{(2)}=\sum_{\lambda}U_{\mu\lambda}U_{\lambda\nu}\ldots
given in Eq. (
C.1.1
). If
U_{\lambda\nu}
represents the creation (resp. annihilation) of a boson of mode
m
, then
U_{\mu\lambda}
must represent the annihilation (resp. creation) of a boson of the same mode. Thus,
\rho^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}\delta_{\{N^{\mu}\},\{N^{\nu}\}}=

\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda,m}\frac{\big\langle\mu\,|\,U_{m}^{e}a_{m}\,|\,\lambda\big\rangle\,\big\langle\lambda\,|\,U^{e}_{-m}a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m}\,|\,\nu\big\rangle+\big\langle\mu\,|\,U^{e}_{-m}a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m}\,|\,\lambda\big\rangle\,\big\langle\lambda\,|\,U^{e}_{m}{a}_{m}\,|\,\nu\big\rangle}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}\bigg[\frac{F_{\mu\lambda}}{E_{\mu\lambda}-i0^{+}}+\frac{F_{\nu\lambda}}{E_{\lambda\nu}-i0^{+}}\bigg]\delta_{\{N^{\mu}\},\{N^{\nu}\}}.}

(99)

In particular, Eq. (
98
) implies there are no
‘cross terms’ proportional to
\langle\ldots U^{e}_{m}\ldots\rangle\langle\ldots U^{e}_{m^{\prime}}\ldots\rangle
with
m
photonic and
m^{\prime}
phononic.

Eq. (
99
) manifests
two classes of intermediate states
|\,\lambda\,\rangle
– one with
\{N^{\lambda}\}
differing from
\{N^{\mu}\}
only in that
N^{\lambda}_{m}=N^{\mu}_{m}+1
, and another with
\{N^{\lambda}\}
differing from
\{N^{\mu}\}
only in that
N^{\lambda}_{m}=N^{\mu}_{m}-1
. We distinguish the two classes by the notation
\{N^{\lambda}\}=\{\ldots,N^{\mu}_{m}\pm 1,\ldots\}
, which allows to express
\rho^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}\delta_{\{N^{\mu}\},\{N^{\nu}\}}=

\displaystyle{\sum_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\sum_{m}\bigg\{\frac{(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{-m})_{\lambda\nu}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}(N_{m}^{\mu}+1)\bigg[\frac{p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}P_{\{N^{\mu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}P_{\{\ldots,N_{m}^{\mu}+1,\ldots\}}}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\frac{p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}P_{\{N^{\mu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}P_{\{\ldots,N_{m}^{\mu}+1,\ldots\}}}{E^{e}_{\lambda\nu}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg]{\\
}+\frac{(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{{m}})_{\lambda\nu}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}N_{m}^{\mu}\bigg[\frac{p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}P_{\{N^{\mu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}P_{\{\ldots,N_{m}^{\mu}-1,\ldots\}}}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\frac{p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}P_{\{N^{\mu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}P_{\{\ldots,N_{m}^{\mu}-1,\ldots\}}}{E^{e}_{\lambda\nu}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg]\bigg\}\delta_{\{N^{\mu}\},\{N^{\nu}\}}.}

The above expression utilizes the definition of
O^{e}
in Eq. (
81
) and a new definition for the electronic component of the total energy:

\displaystyle{E^{e}_{\mu}=\sum_{B}n^{\mu}_{B}E_{B};\;\;\;\;E^{e}_{\mu\nu}=E^{e}_{\mu}-E^{e}_{\nu}.}

(100)

The factors of
N_{m}^{\mu}
and
N_{m}^{\mu}+1
in Eq. (
C.1.2
) are obtained from the standard matrix elements for bosonic creation and annihilation.

In evaluating
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big]
, we first perform a partial trace by summing over the bosonic occupancies. In this manner, one converts expressions involving
N^{\mu}_{m}
to expressions involving average occupancies:

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big]=&\;\sum_{\{n^{\mu},n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}\sum_{m}\frac{(\boldsymbol{v}^{e}_{\text{off}})_{\nu\mu}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}\bigg\{(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{-m})_{\lambda\nu}\bigg[\frac{p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\frac{p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\lambda\nu}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg]{\\
}\;\;\;\;+(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{m})_{\lambda\nu}\bigg[\frac{p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\frac{p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\lambda\nu}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg]\bigg\}.}

Let us apply the relation between band-off-diagonal elements of the velocity operator and band-off-diagonal elements of the position operator [Eq. (
77
)], which translates to the following identity in second quantization:

\displaystyle{\frac{(\boldsymbol{v}^{e}_{\text{off}})_{\nu\mu}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}bb^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\langle\{n^{\nu}\}\,|\,c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,\{n^{\mu}\}\big\rangle_{e}\equiv-\frac{i}{\hbar}(\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}})_{\nu\mu}.}

(101)

In dropping the
i0^{+}
, we have assumed that
E^{e}_{\mu\nu}=E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
(for some
b\neq b^{\prime}
) is nonzero for the bands and wavevectors of interest; it is worth recalling that
b
does not include the spin label, hence one should not expect an energy degeneracy owing to spin. By plugging Eq. (
101
) into Eq. (
C.1.2
) and recognizing that two of the four terms are complex conjugates of the other two,
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big]=

\displaystyle{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\sum_{\{n^{\mu},n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}\sum_{m}(\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}})_{\nu\mu}\bigg[(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{-m})_{\lambda\nu}\frac{p_{\{n^{\mu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\lambda}(U^{e}_{m})_{\lambda\nu}\frac{p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\left\langle N_{m}+1\right\rangle}{E^{e}_{\lambda\nu}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg]+c.c..}

(102)

Let us interchange variables
\{n^{\mu}\}\leftrightarrow\{n^{\nu}\}
for the first term and
\{n^{\lambda}\}\leftrightarrow\{n^{\nu}\}
for the second, and then apply the resolution of identity within the electronic Fock space:
\sum_{\{n^{\mu}\}}|\,\{n^{\mu}\}\big\rangle\big\langle\{n^{\mu}\}\,|=I^{e}
.

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big]=&\;\frac{i}{\hbar}\sum_{\{n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}\sum_{m}\frac{(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\lambda}\big[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{-m}\big]_{\lambda\nu}}{E^{e}_{\nu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}\bigg(\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle\big(p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\big)+p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}\bigg)+c.c.,}

(103)

with
[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{m}]_{\lambda\nu}
meaning a matrix element of the commutator of two electronic operators, as defined in Eqs. (
81
)-(
82
). By splitting
\sum_{m}=\sum_{m}^{photon}+\sum_{m}^{phonon}
in Eq. (
103
), one decomposes
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho^{(2)}\big]=\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phot}^{(2)}\big]+\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phon}^{(2)}\big]
, which we
separately tackle.

Evaluating
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phot}^{(2)}\big]

Recalling the definitions of
U^{e}_{m}
,
\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}}
in Eq. (
84
), Eq. (
77
) and Eq. (
101
), and that
U^{e}_{m}=U^{e}_{-m}
within the dipole approximation,

\displaystyle{(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\lambda}[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{-m}]_{\lambda\nu}=\tfrac{he^{2}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}bb^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg]_{aa^{\prime}}\big\langle\{n^{\nu}\}\,|\,c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,\{n^{\lambda}\}\big\rangle_{e}\big\langle\{n^{\lambda}\}\,|\,c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{a\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,\{n^{\nu}\}\big\rangle_{e},}

(104)

with
a,a^{\prime},b
and
b^{\prime}
being band labels, and
[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}]
being a commutator of two matrices in the band indices.

The product
\left\langle c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle_{e}\left\langle c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{a\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{e}
is given by

\displaystyle{\text{(i)}\;\delta_{AA^{\prime}}\delta_{BB_{2}}n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{\nu}n^{\nu}_{a\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}}+\text{(ii)}\;(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{AB_{2}}\delta_{A^{\prime}B}\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B},}

(105)

with
B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
,
B_{2}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k})
,
A=(a\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
, and
A^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
, and
\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B
labels an electronic Fock basis state that differs from
\{n^{\lambda}\}
only in having one-particle state
B_{2}
be unoccupied and
B
be
occupied.
Eq. (
105
) implies two additive contributions to
(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\lambda}[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{-m}]_{\lambda\nu}=
(i’)+(ii’), namely

\displaystyle{&\text{(i')}\;=\tfrac{he^{2}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}}\bigg\{\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{\nu}\bigg\}\bigg\{\sum_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg]_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}n^{\nu}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg\}\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}};{\\
}\text{(ii')}\;=\tfrac{he^{2}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg]_{b^{\prime}b}(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B},}

where we replace the dummy index
a
by
b^{\prime}
in (i’). Plugging (i’)+(ii’) into Eq. (
103
) leads to two additive contributions to
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phot}^{(2)}\big]=
(i”)+(ii”).

It should be seen that (i”) is at least fourth order in the electron charge and therefore does not contribute to the second-order
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phot}^{(2)}\big]
. This follows from

\displaystyle{\sum_{\{n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}\frac{\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle\big(p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}-p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}\big)+p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}}{E^{e}_{\mu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{\nu}n^{\nu}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}}=\sum_{\{n\}}\frac{p_{\{n\}}}{-\hbar\omega_{m}}n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}n_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=-\frac{\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle\left\langle n_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\hbar\omega_{m}},}

(106)

and

\displaystyle{\text{(i'')}\;\propto e^{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle=e^{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle n_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle}{2}\propto e^{4}.}

(107)

Due to time-reversal symmetry,
\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}
is odd under
\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{k}
. The same symmetry would constrain
\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle
to be an even function, if the average were taken in a state of thermal equilibrium. However, optical excitation creates a non-equilibrium state that breaks time-reversal symmetry, which is reflected in a nonzero
(\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle n_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle)
that is proportional to the source intensity, i.e., to
e^{2}
.
59
59

59

The ‘ballistic current’ is essentially
-|e|/2{\cal V}\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}(\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle n_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle).
Belinicher and Sturman 1980

What remains of
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phot}^{(2)}\big]
is (ii”). To evaluate (ii”), we point out that the energy denominator in Eq. (
103
) reduces to

\displaystyle{\frac{(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B}}{E^{e}_{\nu\lambda}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}=\frac{(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B}}{E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}.}

(108)

We need two more identities which follow from
p_{\{n\}}
being a probability function for independent particles [cf. Eq. (
95
)]:

\displaystyle{\sum_{\{n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}p_{\{n^{\nu}\}}(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B}=&\;\sum_{n^{\lambda}_{B},n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}}(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}\sum_{n^{\nu}_{B}}p_{n^{\nu}_{B}}{n^{\nu}_{B}}\sum_{n^{\nu}_{B_{2}}}p_{n^{\nu}_{B_{2}}}{(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})}=\left\langle n_{B}\right\rangle\left\langle 1-n_{B_{2}}\right\rangle{\\
}\sum_{\{n^{\nu},n^{\lambda}\}}p_{\{n^{\lambda}\}}(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{2}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{2}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{2}+B}=\;\left\langle n_{B_{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle 1-n_{B}\right\rangle.}

Altogether, the photonic shift current is expressible as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{phot}=&\;-\frac{2\pi i|e|^{3}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}^{2}}\sum_{m}^{\text{photon}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}-{f_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}\big(1-{f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}\big)\bigg\}\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg]_{bb^{\prime}}}{E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}+c.c.;\;\;\;\;f_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}={f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}-{f_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}.}

(109)

In this last step, we interchanged
b\leftrightarrow b^{\prime}
and simplified our notation as
\left\langle N_{m}\right\rangle\rightarrow N_{m}
and
\left\langle n_{B}\right\rangle\rightarrow f_{B}
, to be consistent with the rest of the paper.

To go from Eq. (
109
) to the final expression for the photonic shift current [Eq. (
25
), Eq. (
28
) and Eq. (
28
)] involves a sum rule derived from the first-quantized commutation relation:
[\hat{r}^{n},\hat{p}^{n^{\prime}}]=i\hbar\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}
, with
n
and
n^{\prime}
denote the components of three-vectors. We follow this through in App.
C.1.3
.

Evaluating
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phon}^{(2)}\big]

Recalling the definitions of
U^{e}_{m}
,
\breve{V}^{m}
,
\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}}
in Eq. (
84
), Eq. (
77
) and Eq. (
101
),

\displaystyle{(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\lambda}[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{-m}]_{\lambda\nu}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{aa^{\prime}bb^{\prime}}\breve{V}^{m}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}}\bigg)_{aa^{\prime}}\big\langle\{n^{\nu}\}\,|\,c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\,|\,\{n^{\lambda}\}\big\rangle_{e}\big\langle\{n^{\lambda}\}\,|\,c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{a\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}}\,|\,\{n^{\nu}\}\big\rangle_{e},}

(110)

with
m=(\boldsymbol{q},p)
,
a,a^{\prime},b
and
b^{\prime}
being band labels, and
\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
being a product of two matrices indexed by band labels.

Imitating Eq. (
105
) and Eq. (
C.1.2
), we find
two additive contributions to
(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\lambda}[\boldsymbol{A}^{e}_{\text{off}},U^{e}_{-m}]_{\lambda\nu}=
(i’)+(ii’), the first of which is nontrivial only if the phonon wavevector vanishes:

\displaystyle{&\text{(i')}\;=\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{0}}\bigg\{\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\breve{V}^{m}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}^{\nu}\bigg\}\bigg\{\sum_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg]_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}}n^{\nu}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg\}\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}};{\\
}\text{(ii')}\;=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}}\breve{V}^{m}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}-\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg)_{b^{\prime}b}(1-n_{B}^{\lambda})n^{\lambda}_{B_{3}}n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n^{\nu}_{B_{3}})\delta_{\{n^{\nu}\},\{n^{\lambda}\}-B_{3}+B},}

with
B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
and
B_{3}=(b^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})
. The contribution to (i’) is only by zero-wavevector optical phonons, since zero-wavevector acoustic phonons do not admit quantization.
60
60

60

One way to see this is that in the quantization of the displacement field, the prefactor in front of
a_{m}
is inversely proportional to
\sqrt{\omega_{m}}
.
Gantmakher and Levinson 1987
Plugging (i’)+(ii’) into Eq. (
103
) leads to two additive contributions to
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phon}^{(2)}\big]=
(i”)+(ii”).

It should be seen that (i”) is at least fourth order in the electron-boson coupling and therefore does not contribute to the second-order
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phon}^{(2)}\big]
. To appreciate this, apply Eq. (
106
) once again, noting that the
1/\omega_{m}
factor in Eq. (
106
) is well-defined for optical phonons as
\boldsymbol{q}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{0}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\text{(i'')}\;\propto\bigg(\sum_{\ldots}\breve{V}^{m}_{\ldots}\ldots\bigg)\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg]_{b,b}\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle=\bigg(\sum_{\ldots}\breve{V}^{m}_{\ldots}\ldots\bigg)\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big]_{b,b}\frac{\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle n_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle}{2}.}

(111)

Because
(\left\langle n_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle n_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle)
is proportional to
e^{2}
, altogether (i”) is quadratic in both the electron-photon and electron-phonon couplings.

To arrive at the last line in Eq. (
111
), we had applied that
\big[\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{0}p}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big]_{b,b}
is odd under
\boldsymbol{k}
-inversion, owing to time-reversal symmetry. Indeed, the anti-unitary nature of time reversal:

\displaystyle{\hat{T}|\,u_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=e^{i\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}};\;\;\;\;\big\langle u_{B}\,|\,\hat{T}u_{B^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=\big\langle u_{B^{\prime}}\,|\,\hat{T}^{-1}u_{B}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}},}

(112)

results in a transposition of the band labels for matrix elements:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{bb^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{k}}=e^{i(\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}})}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{0}p}_{b^{\prime}b,-\boldsymbol{k}}=e^{i(\phi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}})}\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{0}p}_{bb^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\;\;\;\;\big(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{-\boldsymbol{k}}\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{0}p}_{-\boldsymbol{k}}\big)_{b,b}=\big(\breve{V}^{\boldsymbol{0}p}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big)_{b,b}.}

(113)

To elaborate on the middle equality, we utilize our general expression for the self-consistently-screened electron-phonon matrix element [Eq. (
84
)] and massage the matrix element as:

\displaystyle{\big\langle u_{b^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,u_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle=&\;\big\langle u_{b^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,\hat{T}^{-1}e^{-i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\hat{T}\,|\,u_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle=\overline{\big\langle\hat{T}u_{b^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\hat{T}u_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle}=e^{i(\phi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}})}\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{G}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle,}

(114)

omitting the
cell
superscript in the above equation.

What remains of
\text{Tr}\big[\boldsymbol{v}_{\text{off}}\rho_{phon}^{(2)}\big]
is (ii”). To evaluate (ii”), we follow steps closely analogous to Eqs. (
108
)-(
C.1.2
), replacing the Bloch label
B_{2}\rightarrow B_{3}
. This leads to the following expression for the phononic shift current:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{phon}=&\;-\frac{i|e|}{\hbar{\cal V}}\sum^{\text{phonon}}_{m}\sum_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{(f_{B_{3}}-f_{B})}-{f_{B}}\big(1-{f_{B_{3}}}\big)\bigg\}\frac{\bigg(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}-\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg)_{b^{\prime}b}\breve{V}^{m}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}{E_{B_{3}}-E_{B}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}+c.c.,}

(115)

with
m=(\boldsymbol{q},p),B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
and
B_{3}=(b^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})
. Utilizing our definition of the band-off-diagonal position operator [Eq. (
76
)] and the first-quantized electron-phonon operator [Eq. (
87
)],

\displaystyle{[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\text{off}},\hat{V}^{-m}]_{B^{\prime}B}=\big\langle B^{\prime}\,|\,[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\text{off}},\hat{V}^{-m}]\,|\,B\big\rangle_{1};\;\;\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\text{off}},\hat{V}^{-m}]_{B^{\prime}B}V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}=\bigg(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}-\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg)_{b^{\prime}b}\breve{V}^{m}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}},}

(116)

with
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
. This identity can be inserted into Eq. (
115
) to obtain an equivalent expression for the phononic shift current:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{phon}=&\;-\frac{i|e|}{\hbar{\cal V}}\sum^{\text{phonon}}_{m}\sum_{BB^{\prime}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{(f_{B^{\prime}}-f_{B})}-{f_{B}}\big(1-{f_{B^{\prime}}}\big)\bigg\}\frac{[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\text{off}},\hat{V}^{-m}]_{B^{\prime}B}V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}}{E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}+c.c..}

(117)

To go from Eq. (
117
) to the final expression for the phononic shift current [Eq. (
25
), Eq. (
26
) and Eq. (
27
)] involves a sum rule derived from
[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}},\hat{V}^{-m}]=0
. The zero is because
\hat{V}^{-m}
is defined in terms of the position operator but not the momentum operator [Eq. (
87
)]. We follow this through in App.
C.1.4
.

C.1.3
Sum rule for the photonic shift current

The first-quantized commutation relation

\displaystyle{i\hbar\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{BB^{\prime}}=&\;\big\langle B^{\prime}\,|\,[\hat{r}^{n},\hat{p}^{n^{\prime}}]\,|\,B\big\rangle_{1}=\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}\big(r^{n}_{BB^{\prime\prime}}p^{n^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime}}-p^{n^{\prime}}_{BB^{\prime\prime}}r^{n}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime}}\big).}

(118)

will be used to prove:

\displaystyle{\big[A^{\text{off}n},P^{n^{\prime}}\big]_{bb^{\prime}}=i\hbar\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}+\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{n}+(A^{n}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-A^{n}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]P^{n^{\prime}}_{bb^{\prime}},}

(119)

with all
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent quantities evaluated at the same
\boldsymbol{k}
. By inserting Eqs. (
75
)-(
75
) into the right-hand side of Eq. (
118
) and carrying out
\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}
,

\displaystyle{i\hbar\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{BB^{\prime}}=&\;iP^{n^{\prime}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{n}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-iP^{n^{\prime}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{n}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}[A^{n},P^{n^{\prime}}]_{bb^{\prime}}.}

(120)

By applying the second Dirac-delta identity [Eq. (
74
)] and separating diagonal and off diagonal components of
A^{n}
one derives Eq. (
119
).

Let us plug Eq. (
119
) into our expression for the shift current [Eq. (
109
)]. It should be remarked that the
i\hbar\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}
term in Eq. (
119
) does not contribute to the current because the band-diagonal velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}
is an odd function of
\boldsymbol{k}
and the rest of the integrand may be taken as even.
61
61

61

An argument can be constructed that is analogous to the one used in Eq. (
107
).
What remains is

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{phot}=&\;{\text{Im}\;}\sum_{m}\frac{4\pi|e|^{3}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}^{2}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}-{f_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}\big(1-{f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}\big)\bigg\}\frac{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}{E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}},}

(121)

with all
\boldsymbol{k}
subscripts omitted for simplicity. By applying the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem:
1/(x+i0^{+})=CPV[1/x]-i\pi\delta(x)
, with
CPV
meaning Cauchy’s principal value, one can decompose
\boldsymbol{j}=(a)+(b)
, with

\displaystyle{(a)\propto&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}-{f_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}\big(1-{f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}\big)\bigg\}CPV\frac{{\text{Im}\;}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}{E_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}+\hbar\omega_{m}},{\\
}(b)=\;-\sum_{m}\frac{4\pi^{2}|e|^{3}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}^{2}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}-{f_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}\big(1-{f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}\big)\bigg\}\delta({E_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{m})}\real\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(a) vanishes by time-reversal symmetry, which imposes that

\displaystyle{{\text{Im}\;}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=-|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|}

(122)

is an odd function of
\boldsymbol{k}
. To appreciate this, apply that
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}
is real, the velocity operator inverts sign under time reversal, and the time-reversal symmetry of cell-periodic wave functions [Eq. (
112
)]:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{k}}=-e^{i\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-i\phi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}}.}

(123)

(b) is related to the photonic shift vector [Eq. (
28
)] by the following identity:

\displaystyle{\real\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\big[-i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})\big]\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(124)

Plugging the above equation and
Eq. (
77
) into Eq. (
C.1.3
), one finally derives Eq. (
25
) with Eq. (
28
) and Eq. (
28
).

C.1.4
Sum rule for the phononic shift current

Substituting Eqs. (
75
)-(
75
) into the right-hand side of

\displaystyle{0=&\;\big\langle B^{\prime}\,|\,[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}},\hat{V}^{-m}]\,|\,B\big\rangle_{1}=\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}\big(\boldsymbol{r}_{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}V^{-m}_{B^{\prime\prime}B}-V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{B^{\prime\prime}B}\big),}

(125)

applying the standard identity
f(x,x^{\prime})\partial_{x}\delta(x-x^{\prime})=\delta(x-x^{\prime})\partial_{x^{\prime}}f(x,x^{\prime})
, and separating the band-diagonal and band-off-diagonal matrix elements of the position operator, one obtains:

\displaystyle{0=&\;\big(\;i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\;\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\bigg(\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}-\breve{V}^{-m}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{A}^{\text{off}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg)_{b^{\prime}b}.}

(126)

Plugging this into our expression for the phononic shift current [Eq. (
117
)],

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{phon}=&\;-{\text{Im}\;}\frac{2|e|}{\hbar{\cal V}}\sum_{m}^{phonon}\sum_{BB^{\prime}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{B^{\prime}B}}-{f_{B}}\big(1-{f_{B^{\prime}}}\big)\bigg\}\frac{V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}\big(i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}}{E_{B^{\prime}B}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}},}

(127)

with
B=(b\boldsymbol{k}),B^{\prime}=(b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}),f_{B^{\prime}B}=f_{B^{\prime}}-f_{B}
and
E_{B^{\prime}B}=E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B}
. By applying the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem, one can decompose
\boldsymbol{j}_{phon}=(a)+(b)
, with

\displaystyle{(a)\propto&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{q}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{B^{\prime}B}}-{f_{B}}\big(1-{f_{B^{\prime}}}\big)\bigg\}CPV\frac{{\text{Im}\;}V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}\big(i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}}{E_{B^{\prime}B}+\hbar\omega_{m}},{\\
}(b)=\;\frac{2\pi|e|}{\hbar{\cal V}}\sum_{m}^{phonon}\sum_{BB^{\prime}}\bigg\{{N_{m}}{f_{B^{\prime}B}}-{f_{B}}\big(1-{f_{B^{\prime}}}\big)\bigg\}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}}-\hbar\omega_{m})\real V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}\big(i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}.}

(128)

To simplify the above expressions,
it is worth recalling
\overline{V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}}=V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}
from Eq. (
88
).

(a) vanishes by time-reversal symmetry, which imposes that

\displaystyle{{\text{Im}\;}V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}\big(i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}=|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}})|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|}

(129)

is odd under simultaneusly inverting
(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{q})\rightarrow(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{q})
, and the rest of the integrand in Eq. (
128
) is even. (Certainly all energies are even functions, and we have argued for
f_{B}\approx f_{-B}
; we suppose further that
N_{m}\approx N_{-m}
, i.e., that any time-reversal-breaking of the phonon occupations is proportional to the light intensity, and does not affect the second-order shift current.) To prove oddness of Eq. (
129
), it suffices to show that
|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|
is even, i.e.,
|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|=|V^{-m}_{-B,-B^{\prime}}|
with the minus signs denoting a reversal in wavevectors. Recalling how time reversal acts on
\hat{V}^{m}
[Eq. (
88
)] and on Bloch waves [Eq. (
112
)],

\displaystyle{V^{m}_{-B,-B^{\prime}}=e^{i(\phi_{B}-\phi_{B^{\prime}})}V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;|V^{-m}_{-B,-B^{\prime}}|^{2}=V^{-m}_{-B,-B^{\prime}}V^{{m}}_{-B^{\prime},-B}=V^{-m}_{{B}^{\prime}{B}}V^{{m}}_{{B}{B}^{\prime}}=|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}.}

(130)

Plugging

\displaystyle{\real V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}\big(i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+i\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\big)V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}=-|V^{{m}}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}\bigg\{-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}})\arg V^{{m}}_{BB^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\}}

(131)

into Eq. (
128
) and interchanging
B\leftrightarrow B^{\prime}
, one finally derives Eq. (
25
), Eq. (
26
) and Eq. (
27
).

It is worth justifying our interpretation of Eq. (
27
) as a difference between absorption and emission rates:

(i) Suppose a Bloch state transits from
B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
while absorbing a phonon of mode
m
; this is implemented by the electron-phonon interaction
U^{e}_{m}(a_{m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m})
[cf. Eq. (
83
)], or more specifically by
V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B}a_{m}
[cf. Eq. (
84
)]. Thus one expects the associated shift vector for this process to be
-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}+\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
[cf. Eq. (
26
)].
By the golden rule, one expects a transition probability that is proportional to
|V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}|^{2}
and given by the first term in Eq. (
27
), namely
{\cal A}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}^{m}
. The associated contribution to the current is then
-(|e|/{\cal V})\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\;{\cal A}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}^{m}
, which is the first term in Eq. (
25
).

(ii) Suppose a Bloch state transits from
B^{\prime}\rightarrow B
while emitting a phonon of mode
m
; this is implemented by the electron-phonon interaction
U^{e}_{-m}(a_{-m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{{m}})
[cf. Eq. (
83
)], or more specifically by
V^{-m}_{BB^{\prime}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}{c}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{B^{\prime}}a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m}
[cf. Eq. (
84
)]. Thus one expects the associated shift vector for this process to be
-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg V^{-m}_{BB^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}
[cf. Eq. (
26
)]. By the golden rule, one expects a transition probability that is proportional to
|V^{-m}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}=|V^{m}_{B^{\prime}B}|^{2}
[cf. Eq. (
88
)] and given by (negative of) the second term in Eq. (
27
), namely
{\cal E}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}^{m}
. Why the minus sign in Eq. (
27
); equivalently, why the minus sign in Eq. (
25
)? The reason is that the current contributed by this transition is

\displaystyle{-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}{\cal E}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}^{m}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}(-\boldsymbol{S}^{{m}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}){\cal E}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}^{m},}

(132)

which is the second term in Eq. (
25
). Note that
\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}=-S^{{m}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
follows from
\overline{V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}}=V^{-m}_{B^{\prime}B}
[cf. Eq. (
88
)].

C.1.5
The zeroth-order quasiparticle distribution is not thermal

Let us define the non-perturbative quasiparticle distribution as

\displaystyle{f^{stat}_{B}=&\;\text{Tr}[n_{B}^{e}\rho];\;\;\;\;n_{B}^{e}=c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{B}c_{B}.}

(133)

In the Schrödinger representation (indicated by
\begin{subarray}{c}S\\
=\end{subarray}
below), density matrices can be time-dependent but operators (such as
n_{B}^{e}
) are time-independent:

\displaystyle{\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{S}\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\text{Tr}[n_{B}^{e}\partial_{t}\rho].}

(134)

Because
\rho
is stationary, the non-perturbative quasiparticle distribution is steady:

\displaystyle{0=&\;\partial_{t}\rho=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[H,\rho]\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;0=\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B}.}

(135)

In the Heisenberg representation, density matrices are generally time-independent, but operators (like
n_{B}^{e}
) satisfy Heisenberg’s equation of motion:

\displaystyle{\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B}=\text{Tr}[(\partial_{t}n_{B}^{e})_{H}\rho_{H}]=\frac{i}{\hbar}\text{Tr}\{[U_{H},(n_{B}^{e})_{H}]\rho_{H}\},}

(136)

with
O_{H}
denoting an operator
O
in the Heisenberg representation;
62
62

62

This may be verified by substituting
\rho=e^{-iHt/\hbar}\rho_{H}e^{iHt/\hbar}
and
n_{B}^{e}=e^{iHt/\hbar}(n_{B}^{e})_{H}e^{-iHt/\hbar}
into Eq. (
134
).
here, it should be recalled that
H=H_{H}=(H_{0})_{H}+U_{H}
and
[H_{0},n_{B}^{e}]=0\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;[(H_{0})_{H},(n_{B}^{e})_{H}]=0
.
Since traces are independent of the representation:

\displaystyle{\text{Tr}\{[U_{H},(n_{B}^{e})_{H}]\rho_{H}\}=\text{Tr}\{[U,n_{B}^{e}]\rho\},}

(137)

and we may insert the perturbative expansion for
\rho
in Eq. (
92
). A term in this perturbative expansion that is even in powers of
U
has a vanishing contribution to
\text{Tr}\{[U,n_{B}^{e}]\rho\}
, because one traces over an odd multiple of the bosonic creation/annilation operator. In particular,
\text{Tr}\{[U,n_{B}^{e}]\rho^{(0)}\}=0
because
\rho^{(0)}=\sum_{\mu}F_{\mu}|\,\mu\big\rangle\big\langle\mu\,|
[cf. Eq. (
92
)] and
\big\langle\mu\,|\,a_{m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m}\,|\,\mu\big\rangle=(a_{m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m})_{\mu\mu}=0
. Let us therefore evaluate
\text{Tr}\{[U,n_{B}^{e}]\rho^{(1)}\}
, using our expression for
\rho^{(1)}
in Eq. (
C.1.1
):

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{m\mu\nu}\big\{[U^{e}_{m},n_{B}^{e}](a_{m}+a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m})\big\}_{\nu\mu}\frac{F_{\mu\nu}U_{\mu\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}+O(U^{4}).}

(138)

Each photon/phonon that is created must be subsequently annihilated, and vice versa:

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{m\mu\nu}[U^{e}_{m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{F_{\mu\nu}U_{\mu\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}\big\{(a_{m})_{\nu\mu}(a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{m})_{\mu\nu}+(a^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{-m})_{\nu\mu}(a_{-m})_{\mu\nu}\big\}.}

(139)

Switching
m\rightarrow-m
in the second term, and applying the standard matrix elements for bosonic operators,

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{m\mu\nu}[U^{e}_{m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{F_{\mu\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}N_{m}^{\mu}\delta_{N^{\mu},N^{\nu}+m}+\sum_{m\mu\nu}[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{F_{\mu\nu}}{E_{\mu\nu}-i0^{+}}(N_{m}^{\mu}+1)\delta_{N^{\mu}+m,N^{\nu}}.}

(140)

\delta_{N^{\mu},N^{\nu}+m}
is a Kronecker delta function enforcing
N_{m^{\prime}}^{\mu}=N_{m^{\prime}}^{\nu}
for all
m^{\prime}
, except for
N^{\nu}_{m}+1=N^{\mu}_{m}
; for
\delta_{N^{\mu}+m,N^{\nu}}
, it is
N^{\mu}_{m}+1=N^{\nu}_{m}
that is the exception. We use this delta function to kill the summation over
N^{\nu}
:

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}N^{\mu}}[U^{e}_{m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{p_{n^{\mu}}P_{N^{\mu}}-p_{n^{\nu}}P_{\ldots N_{m}^{\mu}-1\ldots}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}N_{m}^{\mu}{\\
}+\;\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}N^{\mu}}[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{p_{n^{\mu}}P_{N^{\mu}}-p_{n^{\nu}}P_{\ldots N_{m}^{\mu}+1\ldots}}{E_{\mu\nu}^{e}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}(N_{m}^{\mu}+1).}

E^{e}
is the electronic component of
E
. Carrying out the sum over
N^{\mu}
,

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}}[U^{e}_{m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{p_{n^{\mu}}\langle N_{m}\rangle-p_{n^{\nu}}\langle N_{m}+1\rangle}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}}[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}\frac{p_{n^{\mu}}\langle N_{m}+1\rangle-p_{n^{\nu}}\langle N_{m}\rangle}{E_{\mu\nu}^{e}-\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}.}

(141)

Interchanging summation variables
n^{\mu}\leftrightarrow n^{\nu}
for the second term,

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}}\bigg(-\frac{[n_{B}^{e},U^{e}_{m}]_{\nu\mu}(U_{-m}^{e})_{\mu\nu}}{E^{e}_{\mu\nu}+\hbar\omega_{m}-i0^{+}}+\frac{[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\mu\nu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\mu}}{E_{\mu\nu}^{e}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}\bigg)\bigg(p_{n^{\mu}}\langle N_{m}\rangle-p_{n^{\nu}}\langle N_{m}+1\rangle\bigg).}

(142)

By applying that
n_{B}^{e}
is self-adjoint and
U^{e}_{m}=(U^{e}_{-m})^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}
[cf. Eq. (
83
)], one recognizes one fraction to be the complex conjugate of the other:

\displaystyle{-i\hbar\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;\sum_{mn^{\mu}n^{\nu}}\bigg(2i{\text{Im}\;}\frac{[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\mu\nu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\mu}}{E_{\mu\nu}^{e}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}\bigg)\bigg(p_{n^{\mu}}\langle N_{m}\rangle-p_{n^{\nu}}\langle N_{m}+1\rangle\bigg).}

(143)

At this point we split the photonic and phononic contributions:

\displaystyle{\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;(\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat})^{phot}+(\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat})^{phon}}

(144)

by splitting the sum over the bosonic modes:
\sum_{m}=\sum_{m}^{phot}+\sum_{m}^{phon}.
Focusing first on the photonic contribution,
we evaluate the numerator in Eq. (
143
) with help from Eq. (
82
), Eq. (
84
) and
\epsilon_{m}=\epsilon_{-m}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle{[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]=&\;\sum_{b^{\prime}}\sharp\epsilon_{-m}\cdot\big(v^{off}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}c^{\scriptstyle{\dagger}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}c_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-(b\leftrightarrow b^{\prime})\big);\;\;\;\;\sharp=\sqrt{\tfrac{he^{2}}{\omega_{m}{\cal V}}},}

(145)

\displaystyle{[U^{e}_{-m},n_{B}^{e}]_{\mu\nu}(U_{m}^{e})_{\nu\mu}=&\;\sum_{b^{\prime}}|\sharp\epsilon_{m}\cdot v^{off}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\bigg\{(1-n_{B^{\prime}}^{\nu})n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n_{B}^{\mu})n_{B^{\prime}}^{\mu}\delta_{n^{\mu},n^{\nu}-B+B^{\prime}}-(b\leftrightarrow b^{\prime})\bigg\}.}

(146)

Since the numerator in Eq. (
143
) is manifestly real, it suffices to evaluate the imaginary part of the denominator:

\displaystyle{\delta_{n^{\mu},n^{\nu}-B+B^{\prime}}{\text{Im}\;}\frac{1}{E_{\mu\nu}^{e}+\hbar\omega_{m}+i0^{+}}=-\pi\delta_{n^{\mu},n^{\nu}-B+B^{\prime}}\delta(E_{\mu\nu}^{e}+\hbar\omega_{m})=-\pi\delta_{n^{\mu},n^{\nu}-B+B^{\prime}}\delta(\varepsilon_{B^{\prime}B}+\hbar\omega_{m}),}

(147)

with
\varepsilon_{B}
being a one-electron energy.
Summing over electron occupancies,

\displaystyle{\sum_{n^{\mu}n^{\nu}}p^{n^{\mu}}(1-n_{B^{\prime}}^{\nu})n^{\nu}_{B}(1-n_{B}^{\mu})n_{B^{\prime}}^{\mu}\delta_{n^{\mu},n^{\nu}-B+B^{\prime}}=(1-f_{B})f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;f_{B}=\langle n_{B}\rangle.}

(148)

Combining it all, we arrive at a steady-state condition on the quasiparticle occupancies:

\displaystyle{0=\partial_{t}f_{B}^{stat}=&\;(\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B})^{phot}_{gain}-(\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B})^{phot}_{loss}+(\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B})^{phon}+O(U^{4}){\\
}(\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B})^{phot}_{gain}=\;\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\sum_{mb^{\prime}}|\sharp\epsilon_{m}\cdot v^{off}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}(1-f_{B})f_{B^{\prime}}\bigg\{\langle N_{m}\rangle\delta(\varepsilon_{BB^{\prime}}-\hbar\omega_{m})+\langle N_{m}+1\rangle\delta(\varepsilon_{BB^{\prime}}+\hbar\omega_{m})\bigg\}{\\
}(\partial_{t}f^{stat}_{B})^{phot}_{loss}=\;\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\sum_{mb^{\prime}}|\sharp\epsilon_{m}\cdot v^{off}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}(1-f_{B^{\prime}})f_{B}\bigg\{\langle N_{m}\rangle\delta(\varepsilon_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega_{m})+\langle N_{m}+1\rangle\delta(\varepsilon_{B^{\prime}B}+\hbar\omega_{m})\bigg\}.}

It may be seen that the gain and loss rates are of the form expected from Dirac’s time-dependent perturbation theory, i.e., Fermi’s golden rule.
The phononic contribution may be evaluated analogously and also has the form expected from Dirac’s time-dependent perturbation theory.

In conclusion, for the non-perturbative quasipartice distribution
f_{B}^{stat}
to be steady (up to
U^{4}
corrections), the zeroth-order quasiparticle distribution
f_{B}
is the steady solution of
I_{coll}[f_{B}]=0
, where
I_{coll}
is the collisional integral (evaluated by Fermi’s golden rule) in the presence of the light source. In particular,
f_{B}
is
not
the thermal quasiparticle distribution in the absence of the light source, contrary to the way in which most authors approach perturbation theory in nonlinear optical response.

C.2
Numerical implementation of the BIS shift-current formula

This appendix explains how to simulate an iso-energy-averaged quasiparticle distribution
f_{E}
that is a steady solution to the kinetic equation derived in App.
B.2
, and how
f_{E}
is subsequently inputted to the BIS formula [Eq.(
25
)] to determine the shift conductivity and its threefold decomposition. The conductivity will be determined for the model Hamiltonian [Eq. (
13
) with
\tilde{Q}=1
and
\tilde{P}=4
] that is characterized by large time-reversal-symmetric Berry curvature; in particular, we would like the reader to be able to reproduce the conductivity plot in Fig.
5
(e).

In App.
B
, we have motivated the momentum-resolved collisional integral in Eqs. (
52
)-(
56
