), and derived the corresponding iso-energy-averaged collisional integral in Eqs. (
64
)-(
69
), having assumed that the quasiparticle distribution is iso-energy symmetric:
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx f_{E}
; cf. Eq. (
B.2.2
). This assumption is justified to the extent that the collisional integral is iso-energy symmetric, meaning that Eqs. (
52
)-(
56
) is well-approximated by Eqs. (
64
)-(
69
). Whether this is a good approximation depends on the parameters chosen in our model Hamiltonian [Eq. (
13
)] as well as the source radiation frequency
\omega_{s}
. We have checked that the e-isotropy condition approximately holds with our chosen parameters (
\tilde{Q}=1
and
\tilde{P}=4
) in the frequency range
\hbar\omega/E_{o}\in[0.8,1.5]
.
63
63

63

The dipole matrix element becomes iso-energy asymmetric at higher frequencies, as explained in Sec.
IV
.
The iso-energy symmetric assumption was made to save computational simulation time, but one may do without this assumption if one is numerically sophisticated.

There remains some work in fixing the parameters in both sets of collisional integrals, chief among them being the electron-optical-phonon coupling constant
\zeta
in Eq. (
56
), as well as the time scale
\tau^{o}_{E}
for spontaneous emission of optical phonons in Eq. (
64
). The two parameters are related through Eq. (
66
), which can be simplified as:

\displaystyle\zeta\;\frac{a}{\mathcal{V}^{2}g_{E}}\sum^{cut}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\frac{\left|\big\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}\right|^{2}}{|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|^{2}}\delta(E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E)\delta(E_{c\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\hbar\Omega_{o})=\frac{1}{\tau^{o}_{E}}.

(149)

The summation is restricted by the condition
\delta k=|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|\leq\mathscr{G}/10
, with
\mathscr{G}=2\pi/a
being the reciprocal lattice period. A typical scale for
\tau^{o}_{E}
is
0.1ps
,
Lundstrom 2010
;
Na et al. 2019

hence we set
\tau^{o}_{E^{*}}=0.1ps
for a reference energy
E^{*}=0.41375E_{0}
in the active region; this fixes
\zeta=6.329\frac{E_{0}}{0.1ps}
and causes
\tau_{E}^{o}
to vary from value 33.3 fs to value 165.2fs in the active region, as illustrated in Fig.
11
(a). To be clear, all plotted energies are defined to equal zero in the middle of the gap, in contrast to the carrier energies defined with respect to the band extrema. The other parameters in the kinetic model are fixed to be:
\tau_{\text{rec}}=1ns
(a typical interband recombination time
Sturman and Fridkin 1992
);
\tau_{E}^{s}=1ns
for all
E
(a typical energy relaxation time due to spontaneous emission of acoustic phonons
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
);
64
64

64

\tau_{E}^{s}\gg\tau_{E}^{o}
and the optical phonon scattering explicitly dominates in the active region.
and
a=5\mbox{\AA}
(a typical lattice period). All calculation in this appendix are presented for a linearly polarized source:
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
.

Figure 11:
(a) shows the plot of
\tau_{E}^{o}
versus
E
in the active region. (b) shows the plot of
||g_{E}I[f^{(n)}]||
versus the evolution step
n
. (c) compares
f_{c}(1-f_{v})
and
f_{v}(1-f_{c})
. (d) compares
\langle|A_{cv}^{x}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}
and
\langle|A_{cv}^{y}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}
in the passive region, i.e.,
\hbar\omega\in[E_{g},E_{g}+2\hbar\Omega_{o}]
.

Our first step is to simulate
f_{E}
which sets the iso-energy-averaged collisional integral [Eqs. (
64
)-(
69
)] to zero. We begin by discretizing the energy:
\ldots,E_{j},E_{j+1},E_{j+2},\ldots
such that adjacent energy levels are separated by
\Delta E=E_{j+1}-E_{j}
. Conduction-band Bloch states are binned according to the following rule: if
E_{j}-\Delta E/2\leq E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}<E_{j}+\Delta E/2
, then the Bloch state
(c\boldsymbol{k})
belongs in the
j
’th bin. By choosing
\hbar\omega=n_{\omega}\Delta E
and
\hbar\Omega_{o}=n_{\Omega}\Delta E
to be integer multiples of
\Delta E
, one can translate Dirac delta functions to Kronecker delta functions:
\delta(E_{c\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-\hbar\Omega_{o})\rightarrow\delta_{E_{c\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},\hbar\Omega_{o}}/\Delta E
and
\delta(E_{cv\mathbf{k}}-\hbar\omega)\rightarrow\delta_{E_{cv\mathbf{k}},\hbar\omega}/\Delta E
.
For instance,
\delta_{E_{c\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},\hbar\Omega_{o}}=1
if and only if
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow E_{j}
and
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j-n_{\Omega}}
for some bin index
j
.
Then the photon-absorption term is discretized as

G_{\uparrow}[f]\,\delta(E-E_{c,exc})\rightarrow g_{E}\sum_{m_{s}}I^{m_{s}}_{{\text{exc}},E};\;\;\;\;I^{m}_{{\text{exc}},E}=2\pi^{2}\alpha_{fs}c\frac{\Delta N_{m}}{\mathcal{V}}\,2E\,\left(1-2f_{E}\right)\langle|\epsilon_{m}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv\mathbf{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega_{m}}\frac{\delta_{2E,\hbar\omega_{m}}}{\Delta E},

(150)

with
\alpha_{fs}=|e|^{2}/(\hbar c)
being the fine-structure constant, and
I^{m}_{\text{exc},E}
being the iso-energy average of
I_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}^{\omega\epsilon}
; cf. Eq. (
46
). In practice, we have chosen
\Delta E=E_{0}/1600
and
\hbar\Omega_{o}=20\Delta E
. To avoid certain artifacts of our energy discretization scheme, we introduced a small frequency bandwidth (
4\Delta E/\hbar
) for the source-generated photons; this means that the source produces an equal number of photons in each of four modes (
m_{s}=1,2,3,4
), with differing frequencies
\omega_{s}-2\Delta E/\hbar
,
\omega_{s}-\Delta E/\hbar
,
\omega_{s}
,
\omega_{s}+\Delta E/\hbar
but identical polarization
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
.

We initialize the distribution as a Boltzmann-Maxwell distribution:
f^{(0)}_{E}=f_{E,BM}^{T}=\exp(-(E-\mu_{e})/k_{B}T)
,
which is the steady distribution favored by the Fokker-Planck term:
(1+k_{B}T\partial_{E})f^{T}_{E}=0
.
65
65

65

hot-carrier photoluminescence spectra support the hypothesis that most photoexcited carriers are distributed à la Maxwell-Boltzmann; cf. App.
B.1
.

\mu_{e}
is generically not the chemical potential in thermal equilibrium; instead, it is determined by balancing recombination and excitation rates for the conduction band as a whole:
\sum_{E}g_{E}f_{E}^{T}\Delta E/\tau_{\text{\text{rec}}}=G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}
, with
\sum_{E}\Xi(E)
our shorthand for
\sum_{j}\Xi(E_{j})
.

Beginning from our ansatz Maxwellian distribution, we evolve the system over a discrete time interval
\delta t
to obtain a new distribution:

f_{E}^{(n+1)}=f_{E}^{(n)}+I_{E}[f^{(n)}]\delta t,

(151)

for
n=0,1,2,....
, with the collisional integral
I_{E}[f]
defined in Eqs. (
64
)-(
69
). This is a numerical procedure to obtain a steady state, and in no way reflects the actual time evolution of quasiparticle distribution in an experiment.
We stop this iterative process when the norm

||g_{E}I[f^{(n)}]||=\sqrt{\sum_{E}(g_{E}I[f^{(n)}(E)])^{2}},

(152)

decays below a certain threshold, i.e.,
0.05\%
of
\sum_{E,m_{s}}g_{E}I^{m_{s}}_{\text{exc}}
.
Supposing the threshold is crossed when
n=n_{0}
, then we say
f^{(n_{0})}
is a
numerically steady
solution of the kinetic equation.

For illustration, Fig.
3
(c) represents a numerically steady distribution calculated using the above scheme, with
\Delta N_{m_{s}}/\mathcal{V}=(1ns)^{-1}\frac{\Delta E\mathscr{G}^{2}}{440\pi^{2}c\alpha_{fs}E_{0}}\approx 10^{10}cm^{-3}
for each of the four source modes,
E_{\text{exc}}=5\hbar\Omega_{o}
,
n_{0}=5000
steps, and a time step
\delta t=1fs
. Fig.
11
(b) illustrates a decay of
||g_{E}\,I[f_{n}||
below our threshold of
(5\times 10^{-4})\sum_{E}g_{E}I_{\text{exc}}\approx 1.08\times 10^{-7}
.

To calculate the shift current, we input the numerically steady
f^{(n_{0})}_{E}
to the threefold-decomposed current formulas in Eq. (
46
), Eq. (
48
) and Eq. (
50
). The discrete analogs of these formulas are:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{j}_{\text{exc}}=&\;-2_{\uparrow\downarrow}\frac{|e|}{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{m_{s}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}S^{\epsilon_{s}}_{c\mathbf{k}\leftarrow v\mathbf{k}}I_{\text{exc},E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}^{m},{\\
}\mathbf{j}_{\text{intra}}=\;2_{\uparrow\downarrow}2_{cv}\frac{|e|}{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{cut}\frac{\zeta a}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}|^{2}}f_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}(1-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})\frac{\delta_{E_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},\hbar\Omega_{o}}}{\Delta E}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c,(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})/2}\times(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}),{\\
}\mathbf{j}_{\text{\text{rec}}}=\;2_{\uparrow\downarrow}\frac{|e|}{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\text{pass}}\mathbf{S}^{\hat{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}\frac{f_{c\mathbf{k}}}{\tau_{\text{\text{rec}}}}.}

(153)

We will explain each equation in turn:

(i-excitation)
I_{\text{exc}}^{m}
was defined in Eq. (
150
) and
\sum_{m_{s}}
sums over the aforementioned source modes.

(ii-intra) Eq. (
153
) is derived by substituting the electron-phonon matrix element [Eq. (
56
)] and the anomalous shift vector [Eq. (
5
)] into Eq. (
48
), and then summing over both conduction and valence bands. In this sum, each band contributes equally due to the presumed electron-hole symmetry [
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
; cf. App.
B.2.1
], hence the factor of
2_{cv}=2
in Eq. (
153
). To see why, note
for any two-band model that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
, hence
\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{c;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{v;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
and
f_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}(1-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{c;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}=(1-f_{v\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}(-\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{v;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}})
. Recognizing from Eq. (
5
) that
\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{v;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{v;\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
, we find that Eq. (
48
) is identical for valence and conduction bands.

(iii-recombination)
\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\text{pass}}
in Eq. (
153
) integrates over the passive
\boldsymbol{k}
-volume, based on a previous argument [cf. Sec.
II
] that the majority of photo-excited carriers are steadily distributed within the passive region; this argument is corroborated by our numerical simulation in Fig.
3
(c), bearing in mind that
g_{E}
is constant in our quasi-2D model. Because a dipole selection rule fixes
A^{y}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for
k_{x}=0
, and
A^{y}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
cannot vary substantially in the small passive region [assuming the band gap is not anomalously small], it may be deduced that

|A^{y}_{cv,\mathbf{k}}|^{2}\ll|A^{x}_{cv,\mathbf{k}}|^{2}
everywhere in the passive region; cf. Fig.
11
(d). Therefore, one may as well approximate all recombination transitions as being mediated by
x
-polarized photons, with the spontaneous emission rate
\mathcal{E}^{sp,\hat{x}}_{v\mathbf{k}\leftarrow c\mathbf{k}}=f_{c\mathbf{k}}/\tau_{\text{\text{rec}}}
. The corresponding photonic shift vector
\mathbf{S}^{\hat{x}}_{v\mathbf{k}\leftarrow c\mathbf{k}}
is also approximated as
\mathbf{S}^{\hat{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
, because the variation of the photonic shift vector within the passive region is small.

The threefold-decomposed conductivities are obtained by diving each of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}},\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
by
|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}|^{2}
; cf. Eq. (
51
). It is advantageous to express the squared electric amplitude
|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}|^{2}
in terms of the discrete
I^{m}_{\text{exc}}
[Eq. (
150
)]:

|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}|^{2}=\left(\sum_{E,m_{s}}g_{E}\Delta EI^{m_{s}}_{\text{exc},E}\right)/[2\pi c\alpha_{fs}(1-2f_{E})\langle|\epsilon_{m_{s}}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv\mathbf{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}JDOS_{\uparrow}],

(154)

in accordance with
\sum_{m_{s}}\Delta N_{m_{s}}\hbar\omega_{s}/\mathcal{V}=|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}|^{2}/(2\pi)
; c.f. Eq. (
36
). For the conductivity plot in Fig.
5
(e), we had chosen
\sum_{m_{s}}\Delta N_{m_{s}}/\mathcal{V}\approx 10^{10}cm^{-3}
.
For comparison, in a typical argon-ion-laser experiment with a radiation intensity of
40Wcm^{-2}
,
Koch et al. 1976
the number density of source photons is approximately
1/3\times 10^{10}\,cm^{-3}
.

C.3
Comparison with the Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shkrebtii formula and dissipative Floquet methods

The Kraut-Baltz-Sipe-Shkrebtii formula (KBSS) for the shift current is
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{KBSS}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{KBSS}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2},\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{KBSS}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=-2\pi\frac{|e|^{3}}{\hbar}\sum_{bb^{\prime}}\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}f^{T}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega),}

(155)

with
E_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
and
f^{T}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=f^{T}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-f^{T}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
.
One can convert Eq. (
155
) to a proportionality relation with the radiation intensity (within the dielectric medium) by
{\cal I}_{rad}=(c/2\pi)n_{\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}
, assuming the medium is non-magnetic with a frequency-dependent refractive index
n_{\omega}
that is spatially uniform and isotropic.
66
66

66

The time-averaged Poynting vector (within the dielectric medium) has the form
{\cal I}_{rad}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
, with
{\cal I}_{rad}=(c/2\pi)n_{\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}
having dimensions of energy per unit area per unit time, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
being the unit directional vector of the electromagnetic wave propagation. We adopt the same, real-valued definition of the refractive index as in Ref.
Landau et al. 1960
. In an absorptive medium,
{\cal I}_{rad}
should be multiplied by a coordinate-dependent, exponential damping factor;
Landau et al. 1960
however this factor is negligible if the attenuation length greatly exceeds the thickness of the medium.

The KBSS formula has been derived in a variety of models and methods,
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981
;
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
;
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
;
Barik and Sau 2020
;
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
;
Parker et al. 2019
;
Ahn et al. 2021
;
Holder et al. 2020
;
Watanabe and Yanase 2021
which may have created an impression that the KBSS formula is universally truthful. The actual reason for the universality is a largely unjustifiable and often implicit assumption shared by all these models, namely that the electronic quasiparticle distribution retains its equilibrium value under continuous-wave irradiation. It is an experimental fact that this assumption does not hold, as is most vividly demonstrated by hot-carrier photoluminescence spectroscopy.
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
;
Esipov and Levinson 1987

This formula was originally derived by Kraut and Baltz
Kraut and von
Baltz 1979
;
von Baltz and Kraut 1981

and subsequently rederived by Sipe and Shkrebtii
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
using more-or-less standard perturbation theory. In the Kraut-Baltz derivation, relaxation was accounted for in a crude relaxation time approximation, with the relaxation time eventually taken to be arbitrarily small compared to the Rabi oscillation period at resonance; in other words, relaxation to equilibrium is assumed to be such a strong effect (relative to the optical excitation) that the electronic quasiparticle distribution never deviates from the equilibrium value. (Similar perturbative derivations
Watanabe and Yanase 2021
;
Ahn et al. 2021
;
Holder et al. 2020
have proposed without rigorous justification to view the imaginary infinitesimals in the energy denominator as an inverse relaxation time.) In the Sipe-Shkrebtii derivation
Sipe and Shkrebtii 2000
(and similar diagrammatic methods
Parker et al. 2019
), relaxation was omitted entirely; because their method is based on perturbing an equilibrium state in the lowest orders for the electric field, it is not surprising that their final formula is expressed in terms of the equilibrium quasiparticle distribution. The KBSS formula has been alternatively derived from dissipative Floquet methods
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
;
Barik and Sau 2020
;
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
in the regime of strong dissipation: relaxation rate
\gg
Rabi frequency. This is another model where relaxation to equilibrium is assumed to be overwhelmingly strong.

The rest of this appendix will be used to demonstrate that the BIS formula
also
reduces to the KBSS formula if the electronic quasiparticle distribution is thermal. On one hand, this planned demonstration can be viewed as a consistency check of the BIS formula. On the other hand, the BIS-to-KBSS reduction crystallizes what is missing from the KBSS formula: namely, the missed photocurrent can be precisely attributed to the deviation of the steady quasiparticle distribution from its equilibrium value, given a realistic model of relaxation in which relaxation also causes shifts.

Without further ado, the KBSS formula in Eq. (
155
) is related to the BIS formula in Eq. (
25
) by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{KBSS}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T}_{B},N^{T;phot}_{m}+\Delta N_{s}\delta_{m,m_{s}},N^{T;phon}_{m}].}

(156)

That the BIS formula is a functional of the quasiparticle, photon and phonon occupancies has been explained in Sec.
A.2
. The KBSS formula is thus the BIS formula with a very specific input for occupancies:
f^{T}_{B}
is a Fermi-Dirac distribution [Eq. (
31
) ],
N_{m}^{T;phon}
is a Planck distribution [Eq. (
32
)] with the same temperature, and the photon occupancy is a sum of thermal and non-thermal contributions; the non-thermal photons are generated by a mono-modal source with mode index
m_{s}
.

All bosonic modes with a thermal occupancy cannot contribute to the shift current, owing to detailed balance; cf. Eq. (
38
). For the source mode
m_{s}
, the net transition rate [Eq. (
28
)] can be decomposed as

\displaystyle{\big({\cal A}^{m_{s}}_{C\leftarrow V}-{\cal E}^{m_{s}}_{V\leftarrow C}\big)_{f^{T}_{B},N^{T}_{m_{s}}+\Delta N_{s}}=&\;\big({\cal A}^{m_{s}}_{C\leftarrow V}-{\cal E}^{m_{s}}_{V\leftarrow C}\big)_{f^{T}_{B},N^{T}_{m_{s}}}+\frac{(2\pi e)^{2}\omega_{s}}{{\cal V}}\;\big|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\big|^{2}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega_{s})f^{T}_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta N_{s},}

(157)

with
C=(c\boldsymbol{k})
and
V=(v\boldsymbol{k})
.
The first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
157
) vanishes by detailed balance [Eq. (
38
)], hence the right-hand side of Eq. (
156
) reduces to Eq. (
155
).

We will say a few words about what is missed from the KBSS formula, how the BIS formula does better, and why dissipative Floquet models (in their present formulation) do not.
As explained in Sec.
II
and elaborated in App.
E
, the KBSS current is approximately the transient photocurrent, or equivalently the excitation component of the steady photocurrent:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{KBSS}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}\approx\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}.}

(158)

As defined through the BIS formula, the shift conductivity
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}
[Eq. (
37
)] has a threefold decomposition explained in Eq. (
11
); apparently, the KBSS formula misses out on current contributions by intraband relaxation and interband recombination.

Because the KBSS formula is derived by dissipative Floquet methods in the strongly dissipative regime,
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
;
Barik and Sau 2020
;
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
it is evident that these methods also miss out on the effects of intraband relaxation and interband recombination. The present formulation of Floquet methods are inadequate for the following reasons: (a) The premise of time-periodic Hamiltonians relies on a classical approximation of the radiation field, and precludes the quantum effect of radiative recombination by spontaneous emission. (b) In Ref.
Morimoto and Nagaosa 2016a
and Ref.
Matsyshyn et al. 2021
, the use of experimentally-unrealizable ‘fermionic baths’ as a relaxation mechanism precludes the phonon-induced shift [Eq. (
5
)] responsible for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}
. (c) In Ref.
Barik and Sau 2020
, Barik and Sau considered electron-phonon scattering as a relaxation mechanism; however, they also missed the phonon-induced shift [ Eq. (
26
)] due to an unjustifiable assumption that the electron-phonon matrix element is momentum-independent.

Appendix D
Loop formulation of the steady shift current

We present an equivalent formulation of the steady shift current, namely that the BIS formula in Eq. (
25
) is equivalent to a sum of loop currents:

\displaystyle{\text{Loop current theorem:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{B,B^{\prime},m}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\bigg({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\bigg)=\sum_{\text{loop}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}},}

(159)

with
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}
being the current contributed by a closed flow line (in energy-momentum space) of one-electron probability, as illustrated in Fig.
12
(b-c). The precise definition of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}
is given in Eq. (
172
) after some preliminary preparations.

Figure 12:
(a) Caricature of a probability flow network. Panels (b) and (c) illustrate two distinct loop decompositions of the network in panel (a). Panel (d) illustrates a geodesic approximation of the network in (a). Part of the approximation amounts to neglecting the radiative recombination transition between the two Bloch states labelled
B_{2}
and
B_{3}
, as justified in Sec.
III
.

As a first step to reformulating the shift current in terms of loop currents, App.
D.1
shows how to interpret the flow of one-electron probabilities in energy-momentum space as an oriented graph with nodes corresponding to Bloch states, as illustrated in Fig.
12
(a); it will be shown that this graph can be decomposed into loops, and for each loop one can associate a net shift vector [Eq. (
171
)] and a current [Eq. (
172
)]. We will then prove the loop current theorem in App.
D.2
, and subsequently discuss two applications:

(i) The loop-current formula manifests that the intraband-Berry connection terms:
(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})
in the shift vector [Eqs. (
2
)-(
3
)] always cancel out, when all transitions in the steady state are accounted for. From this follows a revision of a purported relation between the shift current and interband polarizaton differences,
Fregoso et al. 2017
as discussed in App.
D.3
.

(ii) The loop formulation naturally leads to equitable approximations of the shift current, which treat excitation, relaxation and recombination on equal footing. The approximation lies in identifying a reduced family of loops which contribute most substantially to the shift current. Once a reduced family of loops is identified, calculating the shift current via Eq. (
159
) requires far less computational resources than a direct calculation of the BIS formula [cf. App.
C.2
]. This work focuses on the geodesic loops [Fig.
12
(d)] which predominate the shift current in direct-gap semiconductors. For 3D semiconductors, the geodesic approximation to the shift conductivity [Eq. (
10
)] is derived from the loop-current formula [Eq. (
159
)] in App.
D.4
, and as a small-angle-scattering limit of the BIS formula [Eq. (
25
)] in App.
D.5
.
Finally, the geodesic approximation is extended to quasi-2D semiconductors in App.
D.6
.

D.1
The shift loop and the loop current

Let us define a
link
as a pair of Bloch labels. A link is said to be ordered if the band energy of the first label is larger than the band energy of the second:

\displaystyle{\text{ordered link}\;\equiv\;(B^{\prime}{>}B);\;\;\;\;E_{B^{\prime}B}=E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B}>0.}

(160)

A
general link
written as
(B^{\prime},B)
admits any possible ordering of
E_{B^{\prime}}
and
E_{B}
. For instance, given the three Bloch labels in Fig.
12
(a), one may write
(B_{1}>B_{2})
and
(B_{3},B_{2})
but not
(B_{3}>B_{2})
.

For every ordered link, we define the
ordered transition rate
as the sum of one-electron transition rates over all possible bosonic modes indexed by
m
:

\displaystyle{\text{ordered transition rate}\;=R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=&\;\sum_{m}\big({\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\big),}

(161)

and the
ordered shift vector
as a weighted average of the shift vector [cf. Eqs. (
26
)-(
28
)] over all bosonic modes:

\displaystyle{\text{ordered shift vector}\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=&\;\sum_{m}\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\frac{{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}}{R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}}.}

(162)

As a reminder,
{\cal A}
is the absorption rate and
{\cal E}
the emission rate defined in Eqs. (
27
)-(
28
). Because
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\propto\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega_{m})
with a bosonic energy
\hbar\omega_{m}
that is strictly positive,
67
67

67

As remarked earlier in Sec.
C.1.2
, quantized phonons/photons are not well-defined for zero
\omega_{m}
.
we wrote
(B^{\prime}{>}B)
in Eq. (
162
) rather than
(B>B^{\prime})
. Let us discuss two classes of ordered shift vectors:

Ex-1: Phononic ordered shift vector

If
B^{\prime}
and
B
differ in electronic wavevectors, then, within the dipole approximation for the electron-photon coupling, one can restrict
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) to phononic modes.

(a) If the difference in wavevectors (
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
) is small and the transition is intraband (
b=b^{\prime}
),
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
in Eq. (
162
) is well-approximated by the anomalous shift
\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{b;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
5
)], which does not depend on the phonon branch
p
. It should also be recalled from Eq. (
27
) that
{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}\propto\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}}
. Altogether, these imply that Eq. (
162
) reduces to
\boldsymbol{S}_{(b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}>b\boldsymbol{k})}=\boldsymbol{S}^{ano}_{b;\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
.

(b) If the difference in wavevectors (
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
) is not necessarily small, but the phonon energy
\hbar\omega_{m}=E_{B^{\prime}B}
is nondegenerate, then
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) is restricted to one phonon branch (say
m
), and
\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
as defined in Eq. (
26
).

Ex-2: Photonic ordered shift vector

If
B^{\prime}
and
B
are identical in electron wavevectors (
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
), with
E_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
exceeding the optical phonon energies, than one may restrict
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) to photonic modes.

(a) If
\boldsymbol{k}
does not lie on the excitation surface,
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) is restricted (by energy conservation) to photonic modes whose occupations are thermal, i.e.,
N_{m}=N_{m}^{T_{l}}
has the Planck form and does not depend on the orientation
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
of the photon wavevector. In fact, the only quantities in Eq. (
162
) that depend on
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
is the photonic shift vector [Eq. (
28
)] and the square of the interband Berry connection [Eq. (
28
)]. Thus Eq. (
162
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}_{(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}>b\boldsymbol{k})}=\frac{\int d\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}\sum_{p=1}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}}{\int d\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}\sum_{p=1}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{q}p}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}},}

(163)

where we integrate over
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
(parametrized by solid angle
\lambda_{\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}}
) and sum over both transverse polarizations.

(b) If
\boldsymbol{k}
lies on the excitation surface,
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) sums over all photonic modes with the same frequency
\omega_{s}
as the source-generated photons.
For a bright source, an argument in App.
A.4
conveys that
\sum_{m}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
) may as well be restricted to the single source mode
m_{s}
, so that Eq. (
162
) simplifies to
\boldsymbol{S}_{(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}>b\boldsymbol{k})}=\boldsymbol{S}^{m_{s}}_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow b\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
28
)].

It would also be useful to discuss the net transition rate for
B^{\prime}\leftarrow B
, with
E_{B^{\prime}}
not necessarily greater than
E_{B}
. For this purpose, we define the:

\displaystyle{\text{oriented transition rate}\;R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=\text{sgn}[E_{B^{\prime}B}]R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=-R_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}},}

(164)

such that
R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}>0
represents a net probability flow from
B
to
B^{\prime}
, independent of the ordering of band energies.

We may draw a cartoon to visualize the flow of probability in energy-momentum space. In Fig.
12
(a), we represent every link by an arrow; the thickness of the arrow shaft is proportional to
|R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}|
; the arrowhead points from
B^{\prime}\leftarrow B
if
R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}>0
, and vice versa. Our cartoon is thus an oriented graph/network, with each node/vertex corresponding to a Bloch state, and with each link/edge oriented according to direction of the probability flow. We will use node
{=}{B}
interchangeably.

By comparing the BIS formula [Eq. (
25
)] with the definitions of
R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}
in Eqs. (
161
)-(
162
), one deduces that the shift current is essentially the sum of
R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}
over all ordered links in the probability-flow network:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}=&\;-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}.}

(165)

In the steady state, the time-independence of the occupancy of each Bloch state implies that for each node (say,
B
) in the graph, incoming transition rates must exactly balance outgoing transition rates:
\sum_{B^{\prime}}R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=0
. The probability-flow network can therefore be viewed as a discrete analog of a divergence-free/solenoidal vector field.
This discrete solenoidal condition allows to decompose the probability-flow network into loops, as illustrated in Fig.
12
(b).
68
68

68

Analogously, a divergence-free vector field can be approximated by a superposition of elementary solenoids, which includes the case of finite-length loops.
Smirnov 1994
Each loop represents the closed flow line of an electron’s probability in energy-momentum space, with the perspective that forward-moving holes are backward-moving electrons.

More precisely, here are three defining properties of a loop:

(a) The first property of a loop is that it is a closed concatenation of general links:

\displaystyle{\text{loop with}\;N\;\text{links}\;=&\;(B_{N},B_{N-1})(B_{N-1},B_{N-2})\ldots(B_{2},B_{1})(B_{1},B_{N}).}

(166)

If
(B^{\prime},B)
is one of the
N
links appearing above, then we say the link is contained in the loop:
(B^{\prime},B)\in loop
; if
E_{B^{\prime}B}>0
[resp.
<0
], we would further say that
(B^{\prime}>B)\in loop
[resp.
(B>B^{\prime})\in loop
].

(b) To each loop, we associate a positive-valued
loop rate

|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|
which is the magnitude of the probability flow rate along the loop.

(c) Each loop has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued orientation (
Or_{\text{loop}}
) which determines the direction of probability flow:

\displaystyle{Or_{\text{loop}}=&\;+1:\;\;\;\;B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow B_{N}\rightarrow B_{1}{\\
}Or_{\text{loop}}=\;-1:\;\;\;\;B_{1}\leftarrow B_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow B_{N}\leftarrow B_{1}.}

(167)

It follows from (a-c) that one can assign an
oriented loop rate
to each link in the loop:

\displaystyle{\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B_{n+1}\leftarrow B_{n}}=-\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B_{n}\leftarrow B_{n+1}}=Or_{\text{loop}}|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|,\;\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;\;B_{N+1}\equiv B_{1}.}

(168)

The sense in which the probability-flow network is decomposed to loops is that for each link in the network,

\displaystyle{R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=\sum_{loop\ni(B^{\prime},B)}\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B},}

(169)

where the summation is over all loops that contain the link
(B^{\prime},B)
;
R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
is given by the Fermi’s golden rule [c.f. Eqs. (
27
) and (
28
)] and depends on the carrier distribution. Equivalently, for every ordered link in the network,

\displaystyle{R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=\sum_{loop\ni(B^{\prime}{>}B)}\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}.}

(170)

Consider the cartoon of Fig.
12
(b) for illustration:
(B_{3},B_{2})
is contained in two loops colored red and blue, hence
R_{B_{3}\leftarrow B_{2}}
is given by a sum of two
\delta R
’s. The loop decomposition is not unique, meaning that a different set of loops may satisfy Eq. (
170
) for the same network, as illustrated in Fig.
12
(c).

For each loop, the shift loop is defined by summing the ordered shift vector over all ordered links in the loop, weighted by a sign that encodes the direction of probability flow in that loop:

\displaystyle{\text{Shift loop}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}=\sum_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)\in loop}\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}.}

(171)

Because the summation is over ordered links,
E_{B^{\prime}B}>0
, and
\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]=+1
(resp.
-1
) if the loop-decomposed probability flow is toward increasing band energies (resp. decreasing band energies).

To motivate this sign factor, consider an example which elaborates on case (b) of
Ex-1
[cf. box under Eq. (
162
)]. For the conduction-band link
(B_{1}>B_{2})
illustrated in Fig.
12
(c), the probability flow is toward decreasing band energies, which reflects the predominance of phonon emission over absorption. Then the link’s contribution to
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
is simply

\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B_{1}\leftarrow B_{2}}]\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B_{1}{>}B_{2})}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{q}p}_{B_{1}\leftarrow B_{2}}
, which equals
\boldsymbol{S}^{-\boldsymbol{q},p}_{B_{2}\leftarrow B_{1}}
by the inversion symmetry of the phonon shift vector [Eq. (
26
)]. As explained at the end of App.
C.1.4
,
\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{B_{2}\leftarrow B_{1}}
is precisely the shift vector associated to emitting a phonon of mode
m
.

With
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
and
|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|
in hand, we can now define the
loop current

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|,}

(172)

that enters our loop current theorem in Eq. (
159
). It may be seen that the loop current depends implicitly on the carrier population through
|\delta R_{loop}|
, as per Eq. (
169
) and with identifying
R_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
as the golden-rule transition rates in Eqs. (
27
) and (
28
).

D.2
Derivation of loop current theorem

Beginning from the right-hand side of Eq. (
159
), we input the definitions of the loop current in Eq. (
172
) and the shift loop in Eq. (
171
),

\displaystyle{\sum_{\text{loop}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}=&\;-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\text{loop}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\text{loop}}\sum_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)\in loop}\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]|\delta R_{\text{loop}}|\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}.}

(173)

Utilizing the definition of the oriented loop rate in Eq. (
168
),

\displaystyle{\sum_{\text{loop}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{loop}}=&\;-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\text{loop}}\;\sum_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)\in loop}\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}\;\sum_{loop\ni(B^{\prime}{>}B)}\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}.}

(174)

In the last step, we have applied that summing over all ordered links in a given loop and subsequently summing over all loops is equivalent to summing over all loops which contain a given ordered link and subsequently summing over all ordered links. Carrying out the restricted summation over loops on the right-hand side of Eq. (
174
) and utilizing the rate decomposition condition in Eq. (
170
), we obtain the BIS formula [Eq. (
25
)], which completes the proof.

D.3
Gauge invariance of the reduced shift loop

The theorem allows to simply derive general properties of the steady shift current. We focus on one such property, namely that the
(\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}})
terms in both phononic and photonic shift vectors [Eqs. (
26
)-(
28
)] cancel out, when all transitions are accounted for. This cancellation was pointed out by BIS without an explicit demonstration,
Belinicher et al. 1982
but is a simple consequence of the loop current theorem.

Recall that the shift vector (in either the photonic or phononic case) may be decomposed into a term that depends on the bosonic mode and terms that do not:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}=\delta S^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}+\boldsymbol{A}_{B^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{A}_{B^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{A}_{B}=\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(175)

The mode-dependent term is the negative gradient of an argument of a certain transition matrix element [Eqs. (
26
)-(
28
)]; we will refer to
\delta S^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}
as the
reduced shift vector
. It follows that the ordered shift vector [Eq. (
162
)] decomposes similarly as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=&\;\boldsymbol{A}_{B^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B}+\sum_{m}\delta\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}\frac{{\cal A}^{m}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}-{\cal E}^{m}_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}}{R_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}}.}

(176)

One may verify that the intraband connection terms cancel out in the shift loop
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
, for any loop. Indeed, in the case of the first orientation in Eq. (
167
), the shift loop decomposes as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}=\delta\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}+(\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{2}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{1}})+(\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{3}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{2}})+\ldots+(\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{N}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{N-1}})+(\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{1}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{B_{N}})=\delta\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}.}

(177)

\delta\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
, the
reduced shift loop
, is defined by replacing all shift vectors by reduced shift vectors [cf. Eq. (
175
)] in Eq. (
171
). Thus it follows that each loop current, being proportional to
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
, is invariant if the intraband connection terms are dropped. Finally, the steady shift current, being a sum of loop currents, also satisfies the same invariance property.

Eq. (
177
) implies that the reduced shift loop is a well-defined, gauge-invariant quantity, despite the fact that the reduced shift vector [Eq. (
175
)] of a single transition is not gauge-invariant. By ‘gauge-invariance’, we mean being invariant under redefining one-electron Bloch wave functions by a Bloch-label-dependent phase
\phi_{B}
that is differentiable with respect to
\boldsymbol{k}
:
|\,u_{B}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}\rightarrow e^{i\phi_{B}}|\,u_{B}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
.

The cancellation in Eq. (
177
) calls into question a claim made by Fregoso-Morimoto-Moore,
Fregoso et al. 2017
namely that large polarization differences
[-|e|\int_{BZ}(\boldsymbol{A}_{cc\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{vv\boldsymbol{k}})d^{3}k/(2\pi)^{3}]
between the conduction and valence bands imply a large shift current, in the absence of optical vortices. At best, the Fregoso-Morimoto-Moore claim holds for the frequency-integrated transient shift conductivity [App.
E
], but not the steady shift conductivity.

D.4
Geodesic approximation of loop currents for 3D semiconductors

The steady shift current is well approximated well by keeping the most relevant loops in Eq. (
159
).
This appendix focuses on the reduced family of
geodesic loops
, which predominate the shift current in an intrinsic, direct-gap semiconductor with a single minimum for
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
, namely
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=E_{g}
, and conditioned on (i) carrier-optical-phonon scattering being the dominant mechanism for energy relaxation in the active region, (ii) small optical phonon energies (relative to
E_{g}
and the largest energy of a photoexcited carrier), (iii) low temperature
k_{B}T_{l}\ll E_{g},\hbar\Omega_{o}
(
\Omega_{o}
the optical phonon threshold frequency). The goal of this section is to derive the geodesic approximation to the shift conductivity [Eq. (
10
)] from the loop-current formula [Eq. (
159
)].

To motivate the geodesic loop, let us first consider a pair of Bloch states with Bloch labels
V=(v,\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
and
C=(c,\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
;
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
lies on the optical surface,
v
denotes the valence band, and
c
the conduction band. The oriented transition rate
R_{C\leftarrow V}
[cf. Eq. (
164
)] is assumed to be dominated by the absorption of non-thermal, source-created photons. The probability-flow subgraph that includes the link
(C,V)
is caricatured in Fig.
13
. We will not repeat the arguments [detailed in Sec.
II
and Sec.
III
] that explain why such a subgraph is predominant; our goal here is to explain how such a subgraph can be approximated by a geodesic loop.

Figure 13:
Loop decomposition of a probability flow subgraph. A similar subgraph was considered in Fig.
4
(c).

Granted some poetic license, one may view the subgraph as a cyclic probability river that rises in elevation, then splits into tributaries which eventually merge into a waterfall. The splitting reflects the multiple possible intraband relaxation pathways in the conduction band; the merging reflects the existence of a band-energy extremum that causes relaxation pathways to converge toward the extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
. The cyclic river may be decomposed into
N
cyclic streams, such that for each stream, the flow rate is constant along the stream. (
N=4
for our caricature in Fig.
13
.) This constant flow rate is identified with
|\delta R_{loop_{n}}|
, with
\{loop_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
being labels for the
N
streams. All streams merge at
(C,V)
, such that the sum of the stream flow rates [Eq. (
168
)] equals the river flow rate:

\displaystyle{\sum_{n=1}^{N}|\delta R_{loop_{n}}|=R_{C\leftarrow V};\;\;\;\;C=(c\boldsymbol{k}_{exc});\;\;\;\;V=(v\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}).}

(178)

We have chosen a stream decomposition such that all the streams flow with the same orientation as the river, and this is always possible to choose. In principle, one may choose a stream decomposition in which some of the streams flow against the river along
(C,V)
; then for those counter-flowing streams, one would replace
|\delta R_{loop_{n}}|\rightarrow-|\delta R_{loop_{n}}|
in Eq. (
178
).

We proceed without further use of metaphors. The contribution of the above subgraph to the shift current
69
69

69

Bear in mind that ‘current’ has nothing to do with the metaphorical river current.
is a sum of
N
loop currents:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{subgraph[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop_{n}}|\delta R_{loop_{n}}|.}

(179)

As argued in Sec.
III
, the predominant intraband-relaxation pathways do not deviate far from geodesic paths connecting
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
; we remind the reader that the geodesic path is orthogonal to all iso-energy contours. Let us define the
geodesic loop
as combining an excitation transition at
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
, geodesic-path relaxation to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
through the conduction band, recombination at
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
, and geodesic-path relaxation back to
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
through the valence band, as caricatured in Fig.
12
(d). We denote the geodesic, oriented
\boldsymbol{k}
-paths by
p_{c}
and
p_{v}
respectively, and the geodesic loop by
loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]
; the associated shift loop is denoted
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}
, with
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}
generally defined in Eq. (
171
).

It is of interest to show how
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}
simplifies to an expression for the shift loop [Eqs. (
6
)-(
6
)] that we have used in the main text:

(i) For the recombination transition associated to
(B^{\prime}{>}B)=(c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}>v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext})
[left-most link in Fig.
13
], one applies Eq. (
163
) to show that
\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
, as defined in Eq. (
6
).
\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]
being
-1
accounts for the reversed orientation in a recombination transition, but this minus sign can be absorbed by
-\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{v\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}
.

(ii) For the excitation transition associated to
(B^{\prime}{>}B)=(c\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}>v\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
,
\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}=\boldsymbol{S}^{m_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}}
assuming that the source is mono-modal and bright [cf. argument in App.
A.4
].

(iii) For an intraband transition associated to
(B^{\prime}{>}B)=(c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}>c\boldsymbol{k})
, it is assumed small-angle scattering predominates (
||\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}||\ll
Brillouin-zone dimension), such that
\text{sgn}[\delta R^{\text{loop}}_{B^{\prime}\leftarrow B}]\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(B^{\prime}{>}B)}
reduces to the asymptotic expression
-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k}
[cf. case (a) in
Ex-1
of App.
D.1
]. By approximating a discrete sum over intraband links as a line integral, one obtains the first line integral in Eq. (
6
). The second line integral is obtained in an analogous manner.

Because
loop_{n}
does not deviate far from
loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]
, we approximate
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop_{n}}\approx\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}
for all loops that make up the subgraph; this is the geodesic approximation. The approximation is justified to the extent that small-angle scattering predominates over large-angle scattering, as elaborated in App.
D.5
. Applying the geodesic approximation to Eqs. (
178
)-(
179
),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{subgraph[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}\approx-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}R_{C\leftarrow V}.}

(180)

The shift current is a sum of loop currents over loops that constitute the full probability-flow network, and not just the subgraph containing
(C,V)
. [For the one-dimensional caricature in Fig.
12
(d), the full network is composed of two subgraphs.] In other words, one should sum
\boldsymbol{j}_{subgraph[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]}
over all
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
on the excitation surface:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}\approx-2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}R_{(c\boldsymbol{k})\leftarrow(v\boldsymbol{k})},}

(181)

with the understanding that
R_{(c\boldsymbol{k})\leftarrow(v\boldsymbol{k})}\propto\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)
[Eq. (
28
)] constrains
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}
to the excitation surface; we have also included a factor of
2
to account for spin. Assuming a bright, mono-modal light source, we may follow the argument in App.
A.4
to derive that
R_{(c\boldsymbol{k})\leftarrow(v\boldsymbol{k})}\approx I_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\omega}
with
I_{\text{exc}}
defined in
Eq. (
46
). Converting the source mode occupancy
\Delta N_{s}
to an electric-field amplitude
{\cal E}_{\omega}
through Eq. (
36
), one obtains:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}\approx-2\pi\frac{|e|^{3}}{\hbar}\langle f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}\rangle_{\omega}(2JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}})|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2},}

(182)

with
f_{vc}=f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
a difference in the steady-state quasiparticle distribution functions, and
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\omega}
and
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}
defined in Eq. (
8
). When expressed in terms of a nonlinear conductivity:
\boldsymbol{j}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}
, Eq. (
182
) is equivalent to Eq. (
10
).

D.5
Geodesic approximation as a small-angle-scattering limit of BIS formula

Because the geodesic approximation of the shift conductivity [Eq. (
10
)] has been used in all model calculations, it is of interest to clarify the regime of validity of the approximation. Here, we will demonstrate that Eq. (
10
) derives as a
small-angle-scattering limit
of the BIS formula Eq. (
25
):

\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
s\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{BIS}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{geo};
with
s

a parameter that controls the angle of scattering. The choice of
s
is not unique. One possible choice is to increase the power in the square of the electron-phonon matrix element:
|V^{m}_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},b\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\propto 1/||\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}||^{s}
[Eq. (
84
)], bearing in mind that this is a theoretical exercise to elucidate the essence of the geodesic approximation; the physical value of
s
is two, for polarization scattering with optical phonons; cf. Sec.
III
.

Implementing the threefold decomposition of both
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{geo}
[Eq. (
11
)] and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{BIS}
[App.
A.4
], one can straightforwardly verify that the excitation components match exactly, while the recombination components match to a good approximation, bearing in mind that recombination transitions predominantly occur at
\boldsymbol{k}
near the extremal wavevector.
70
70

70

As described in Sec.
II
and elaborated here,
\sigma_{BIS}^{\text{rec}}
reduces to
\sigma_{geo}^{\text{rec}}
if the photonic shift vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
[in Eq. (
50
), with
\boldsymbol{k}
in the passive
\boldsymbol{k}
-volume] is approximated to be
\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
. This approximation leads to a relative error of order
\hbar\Omega_{o}/E_{g}
, assuming that the band gap
E_{g}
is the energy scale for significant variation of the shift vector. If the photo-excited carriers within the passive region follow a Maxwellian distribution, with electron temperature
k_{B}T_{e}<\hbar\Omega_{o}
and hole temperature
k_{B}T_{h}<\hbar\Omega_{o}
[cf. App.
B.1
], then the relative error is reducible to
\sigma_{BIS}^{\text{rec}}=\sigma_{geo}^{\text{rec}}+O_{r}(k_{B}T/E_{g})
, with
T
being the smaller of
\{T_{e},T_{h}\}
.
This appendix will demonstrate for the intraband components that
\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
s\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{BIS}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{geo}
.

Assuming only two bands are optically excited, the intraband conductivity decomposes into contributions by individual bands:
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{v}
, and we will prove for the conduction band that

\displaystyle{\text{BIS-geodesic reduction:}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
s\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,BIS}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,geo}+O_{r}\big(\surd{{\tfrac{\cramped{\hbar\Omega_{o}}}{\cramped{E_{\text{exc}}}}}},\surd\tfrac{\hbar\Omega_{o}}{E_{g}}\big),}

(183)

with
\Omega_{o}
the optical phonon threshold [Eq. (
33
)] and
E_{\text{exc}}
the excitation energy measured from the conduction-band minimum [Eq. (
43
)]; the meaning of
O_{r}
is relative error, i.e.,
a+O_{r}(b,c)
means
O_{r}(b,c)
has a magnitude less than or comparable to
maximum\{|ba|,|ca|\}
, assuming
b
and
c
to be dimensionless.
The BIS-geodesic reduction for the valence band [Eq. (
183
) with
c\rightarrow v
] also holds true, but is a straightforward extension requiring no further substantiation.

To clarify,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,geo}
is given by Eqs. (
6
)-(
10
) with the shift loop reduced to the line integral over the geodesic path
p_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
connecting
\boldsymbol{k}
to
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,geo}=&\;-2\pi\frac{|e|^{3}}{\hbar}\left\langle f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot A_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\int_{p_{c\boldsymbol{k}}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c}\times d\boldsymbol{k}\right\rangle_{\omega}2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}},}

(184)

while
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,BIS}
is taken from Eqs. (
48
)-(
51
):

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{c,BIS}=-2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{S}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}{\cal E}^{sp}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}.}

(185)

We have omitted the phonon mode
m=(\boldsymbol{q}p)
superscript on the phononic shift
\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{S}^{-m}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
and spontaneous emission rate
{\cal E}^{sp,m}
, with the understanding that
p
is fixed to a single branch of optical phonons and
\boldsymbol{q}=\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
is fully determined by momentum conservation; cf. Eq. (
27
). Henceforth, we will simplify notation by omitting the
c
subscript on all quantities, except in instances where such omission may lead to confusion.

In addition to certain assumptions that justify the predominance of geodesic loops [summarized in the beginning of App.
D.4
], we will make additional model assumptions which simplifies the demonstration of the BIS-geodesic reduction [Eq. (
183
)], though we do not believe
these additional assumptions are ultimately necessary for the reduction:

(i) The optical phonon frequency is roughly a constant equal to
\hbar\Omega_{o}
for the small phonon wavevectors we consider.

(ii) Both conduction and valence bands have isotropic dispersions, i.e.,
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
and
E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
depend on
\boldsymbol{k}
through
||\boldsymbol{k}||
, as may be expected near band extrema with cubic symmetry.

(iii) In the active region, electron-optical-phonon scattering overwhelmingly dominates over electron-acoustic-phonon scattering as the primary mechanism for energy relaxation. One way to formalize this is to take
\eta_{E}
defined in Eq. (
68
) to zero.

Some implications of (i-iii) will hereby be elucidated, in preparation to prove the BIS-geodesic reduction [Eq. (
183
)].

Excitation rate

(i-ii) imply that the excitation energy
E_{\text{exc}}
[cf. Eq. (
43
)] of conduction-band states is degenerate, i.e., the excitation rate [Eq. (
46
)] is nonzero only if
E_{\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{\text{exc}}
:

\displaystyle{I_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}=\tilde{I}_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{\text{exc}}};\;\;\;\;\tilde{I}_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{2\pi|e|^{2}}{\hbar}f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\epsilon\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{c}|}{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{cv}|}{\cal V}g_{E_{\text{exc}}}|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}|^{2}.}

(186)

We collect here a few useful properties of
surface projectors
:

\displaystyle{\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E}=\frac{\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)}{{\cal V}g_{E}};\;\;\;\;\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},E}=\sum_{E}\delta_{\mathbf{k},E}=1;\;\;\;\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E};\;\;\;\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j^{\prime}}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}},}

(187)

which encode their completeness (with
\sum_{E}
meaning
\int{\cal V}g_{E}dE
), idempotence and orthogonality.
71
71

71

If the reader is bothered by
(\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E})^{2}
being a product of two Dirac delta functions, one may regularize the surface projector as:

\displaystyle\delta^{{\cal V}}_{\boldsymbol{k},E}=

\displaystyle\;\begin{cases}1,&|E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E|<1/2{\cal V}g_{E}\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(188)

multiply two regularized projectors and then subsequently take
{\cal V}\rightarrow\infty
.

Integrating a surface-projected test function is equivalent to averaging the test function over a two-sphere parametrized by the solid angle
\lambda
:

\langle\langle\,\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E}\;\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})\big|_{\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{E},\lambda)};\;\;\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\int_{0}^{cutoff}\frac{{\cal V}k^{2}dk}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d\lambda,

(189)

with
k_{E}
being the inverse of the isotropic band dispersion
E_{k}
.
\langle\langle\,\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})\,\rangle\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E}
is referred to as the
iso-energy average
of
\Xi(\boldsymbol{k})
.

Quasiparticle distribution

(i-iii) imply that the non-equilibrium quasiparticle distribution within the active region is singularly peaked at periodic intervals:
Esipov and Levinson 1984
;
Esipov and Levinson 1987

\displaystyle{f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}}\tilde{f}_{\boldsymbol{k},j}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j}};\;\;\;\;E_{j}=E_{\text{exc}}-j\hbar\Omega_{o}.}

(190)

f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
thus has a ladder-like structure,
with the top rung of the ladder corresponding to the excitation energy (
E_{0}=E_{\text{exc}}
), and the lowest rung
E_{j_{max}}
lying just above the passive region. The singular nature of
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
originates from the source being monochromatic and the predominant phonons being dispersionless. One may verify that the regular function
f_{E}
in Eq. (
222
) becomes proportional to a Dirac delta function as
\eta_{E}\rightarrow 0
.

Spontaneous emission rate

A related implication of (i-iii) is that the spontaneous emission rate is a sum of terms that connect adjacent rungs of the ladder:

\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j+1}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}.

(191)

We have extracted
1/||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}
and the singular delta functions such that
\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is regular as
\boldsymbol{k}
approaches
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
. To derive the surface projector
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}
in Eq. (
191
), apply that
\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
[Eq. (
48
)] is proportional to the singular distribution
f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
[Eq. (
190
)]; the second surface projector
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{j+1}}
in Eq. (
191
) originates from energy conservation:
E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=\hbar\Omega_{o}
[Eq. (
48
)]. The ladder structure in Eq. (
191
) implies that the operator
\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}
acts trivially on the emission rate:

\displaystyle{\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},}

(192)

owing to the idempotence of surface projectors; cf. Eq. (
187
).

Kinetic equation

The kinetic equation for the steady quasiparticle distribution [Eqs. (
52
)-(
56
)] simplifies to

I_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}=0,

(193)

for
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
in the active region [Eq. (
A.3
)];
I_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is given in Eq. (
186
) and
\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
in Eq. (
191
). We have dropped the recombination component [Eq. (
53
)] of the kinetic equation because the loss rate due to spontaneous emission of optical phonons greatly outweighs the loss rate due to interband recombination; cf. the discussion under Eq. (
221
).

The last preparation for the BIS-geodesic reduction [Eq. (
183
)] will be to relate the excitation and spontaneous emission rates as:

\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},(k_{0},\lambda)}=\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle^{j+1}_{j};\;\;\;\;\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{j}^{j+1}\equiv\int\frac{d\lambda^{\prime}}{4\pi}\frac{1}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}=(k_{j+1},\lambda^{\prime});\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}=(k_{j},\lambda)}.

(194)

k_{j}
is short for
k_{E_{j}}
, meaning it is the radius of the spherical iso-energy surface with energy
E_{j}
. The term on the right-hand side of
\tilde{I}_{\text{exc}}
can be interpreted as the rate at which a quasiparticle on the
j
’th iso-energy surface drops to the
(j+1)
’th surface by spontaneously emitting an optical phonon.

Eq. (
194
) is ultimately a consequence of the conservation of probability flow in energy-momentum space. Proving Eq. (
194
) takes three steps: (A) we first relate the excitation rate to the rate of phonon-mediated transitions between the
0
’th/excitation surface to the
1
’th iso-energy surface. (B) We then relate the rate of phonon-mediated transitions between the
(j-1)
’th and
j
’th surfaces to the rate of phonon-mediated transitions between the
j
’th and
(j+1)
’th surfaces. (C) Combining our relations from (A) and (B) and taking the small-angle-scattering limit gives us Eq. (
194
).

(Step (A)) Projecting the kinetic equation [Eq. (
193
)] onto the excitation surface tells us

0=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{0}}\left(I_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\right)=I_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{0}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},

(195)

with the last term dropping out because there are no quasiparticles with energies exceeding
E_{\text{exc}}
that can drop to the excitation surface by emitting a phonon; cf. Eq. (
191
). Let us substitute the ladder formula for the emission rate [Eq. (
191
)] into Eq. (
195
) and apply the orthogonality of surface projectors [Eq. (
187
)] to reduce
\sum_{j}
to the
j=0
term. We then convert
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}E_{1}}
to a solid-angular integral via Eq. (
189
) to obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{0}}\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{0}}\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\lambda)},

(196)

(Step (B)) If we project the kinetic equation [Eq. (
193
)] to the
j
’th iso-energy surface with
j\neq 0
and
\neq j_{max}
, then it is the excitation term that drops out:

\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\right)=0.

(197)

Like how we derived the right-hand side of Eq. (
196
), Eq. (
197
) can be massaged to the form:

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\lambda^{\prime\prime}}{4\pi}\;\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}}{||\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}||^{s}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}=(k_{j-1},\lambda^{\prime\prime})}=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}=(k_{j+1},\lambda)}.

(198)

for any
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
on the
j
’th iso-energy surface. With
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
as a reference point, Eq. (
198
) encodes that the incoming probability flow from the
(j-1)
’th surface matches the outgoing probability flow to the
(j+1)
’th surface.

(Step (C))
Both Eq. (
196
) and Eq. (
198
) involve solid-angular integrals which simplify in the small-angle-scattering limit: fixing
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}=(k_{j},\lambda^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}=(k_{j+1},\lambda)}=\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda^{\prime})\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{j}^{j+1},

(199)

with
\langle 1/q^{s}\rangle
defined in Eq. (
194
).
The crucial step taken here is to replace
\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
in the integral by its value when
||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{-s}
is maximized, or equivalently when
||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||
is minimized. This replacement is justified asymptotically as
s\rightarrow\infty
, and may be seen as an application of Laplace’s method.
Olver 1974
To manifest the usual form of the integral seen in Laplace’s method, we momentarily adopt spherical-angular coordinates
\lambda=(\cos\theta,\phi)
such that
\boldsymbol{k}=(k\sin\theta\cos\phi,k\sin\theta\sin\phi,k\cos\theta)
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}=(0,0,k^{\prime})
; then for any smooth function
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f(k,\lambda)
,

\displaystyle{L_{s}=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\frac{f(k,\lambda)}{||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||^{s}}=\int^{1}_{-1}e^{sR(x)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{f(k,x,\phi)}{4\pi}d\phi\bigg]dx;\;\;\;\;R(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\text{ln}\;[k^{2}+{k^{\prime}}^{2}-2kk^{\prime}x],}

(200)

with
x=\cos\theta
.
R(x)
has a unique global maximum at
x=1
, which is an end point of the interval of integration. Applying a standard formula from asymptotic analysis,
Olver 1974

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
s\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}\;L_{s}=\frac{f(k,1,\phi)}{2}\frac{e^{sR(1)}}{sR^{\prime}(1)}+O_{r}(s^{-1})=f_{0,0,k}\frac{1}{2skk^{\prime}|k-k^{\prime}|^{s-2}}+O_{r}(s^{-1}),}

(201)

with
R^{\prime}=dR/dx
.
In our application,
f_{0,0,k}
corresponds to
\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda^{\prime})\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda^{\prime})}
in Eq. (
199
).

Substituting Eq. (
199
) into Eq. (
198
), we relate the transition rates between two adjacent pairs of iso-energy surfaces as:

\frac{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j-1},\lambda)}}{\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}}=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle^{j+1}_{j}}{\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle^{j-1}_{j}}.

(202)

Combining Eq. (
196
) and Eq. (
202
), we relate the excitation rate to the transition rate between a pair of iso-energy surfaces:

\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},(k_{0},\lambda)}=\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{0}^{1}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle^{i+1}_{i}}{\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle^{i-1}_{i}}.

(203)

The solid-angular integral in Eq. (
194
) is evaluated to be

\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{j}^{j+1}=\frac{1}{2(s-2)k_{j}k_{j+1}}\bigg[\frac{1}{|k_{j}-k_{j+1}|^{s-2}}-\frac{1}{(k_{j}+k_{j+1})^{s-2}}\bigg],

(204)

which manifests that
\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{j}^{j+1}
is symmetric under interchanging
j
and
j+1
, hence
Eq. (
203
) simplifies to Eq. (
194
), as desired.

Proof of BIS-geodesic reduction [Eq. (
183
)]

Let us begin the proof by demonstrating that the ratio between Eq. (
184
) and Eq. (
185
)
reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{geo}^{\text{intra}}}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{BIS}^{\text{intra}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\big\langle\big\langle\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},\mathbf{k}}\int_{p_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\mathbf{\Omega}\times d\mathbf{k}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E_{0}}}{\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\big\langle\big\langle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}E_{j}}},

(205)

Beginning with the geodesic expression in Eq. (
184
), we insert the integral expression of
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\omega}
from Eq. (
8
) and decompose the excitation rate
{I}_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}
according to Eq. (
186
):

\displaystyle{-\frac{{\cal V}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}}{2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}|e|}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{geo}^{\text{intra}}=&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},E_{\text{exc}}}\tilde{I}_{exc\boldsymbol{k}}\int_{p_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times d\boldsymbol{k}.}

(206)

The right-hand side of the above equation is simply the numerator of Eq. (
205
), per our definition of iso-energy averaging in Eq. (
189
).
Working now on the BIS formula [Eq. (
185
)], we insert the trivial operator
\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},E_{j}}
[Eq. (
192
)] and apply again the definition of iso-averaging in Eq. (
189
):

\displaystyle-\frac{{\cal V}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}}{2_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}|e|}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{BIS}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\big\langle\big\langle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}E_{j}}.

(207)

Taking the ratio of Eq. (
206
) and Eq. (
207
) gives Eq. (
205
), as desired.

Focusing on a summand of fixed
j
and taking the small-angle-scattering limit,

\displaystyle S{\cal E}_{j}=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\big\langle\big\langle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}E_{j}}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}.

(208)

We then apply Laplace’s method [Eqs. (
200
)-(
201
)] and replace
\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
by its value when
||\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}||
is minimized:

\displaystyle{S{\cal E}_{j}&=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\boldsymbol{S}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}\;\tilde{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}^{sp}_{(k_{j+1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j},\lambda)}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle 1/q^{s}\right\rangle_{j}^{j+1}=\int\frac{d\lambda}{4\pi}\;\mathbf{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}^{j}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{j}\;\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},(k_{0},\lambda)},}

(209)

with
\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}^{j}=(\boldsymbol{k}^{j}+\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime j})/2
,
\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{j}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime j}-\boldsymbol{k}^{j}
,
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime j}=(k_{j+1},\lambda)
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{j}=(k_{j},\lambda)
.
In the last step, we substituted the spontaneous emission rate with the excitation rate in accordance with Eq. (
194
), and replaced the phonon-mediated shift vector with its asymptotic small-angle limit [Eq. (
5
)]. The sum of
S{\cal E}_{j}
over
j
may be regarded as a Riemann sum which approximates a line integral over the geodesic path:

\displaystyle{\sum_{j}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}^{j}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{j}=\int_{p_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\times d\boldsymbol{k}+O_{r}\big(\surd{\tfrac{\hbar\Omega_{o}}{E_{\text{exc}}}},\surd\tfrac{\hbar\Omega_{o}}{E_{g}}\big).}

(210)

Indeed, it may be seen that the discrete transitions between iso-energy surfaces:

\displaystyle{(k_{j_{max}},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{j_{max}-1},\lambda)\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow(k_{1},\lambda)\leftarrow(k_{0},\lambda)=\boldsymbol{k}}

(211)

concatenate into a straight path
p^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
of fixed solid-angular orientation; the geodesic path
p_{\boldsymbol{k}}
similarly connects
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}
in a straight path. One caveat is that
(k_{j_{max}},\lambda)
is not
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
and lies just outside the passive
\boldsymbol{k}
-volume [Eq. (
A.3
)], thus
|p^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
is shorter than
|p_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
by about
k_{\hbar\Omega_{o}}
. In the parabolic-band approximation,
k_{\hbar\Omega_{o}}/k_{E_{\text{exc}}}=(\hbar\Omega_{0}/E_{\text{exc}})^{1/2}
, which is the reason for the relative error in Eq. (
210
). This estimate presumes the band gap
E_{g}
is comparable to
E_{\text{exc}}
. For semiconductors with anomalously small band gaps, the Berry curvature may be concentrated in an energy interval comparable to
E_{g}
, hence the relative error is modified to

(\hbar\Omega_{0}/E_{g})^{1/2}
.
Altogether,

\displaystyle{\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{j_{max}-1}\big\langle\big\langle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{S}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}^{sp}_{\mathbf{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}E_{j}}=\big\langle\big\langle\tilde{I}_{\text{exc},\mathbf{k}}\int_{p_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\mathbf{\Omega}\times d\mathbf{k}\big\rangle\big\rangle_{\boldsymbol{k}E_{0}}+O_{r}\big(\surd{{\tfrac{\cramped{\hbar\Omega_{o}}}{\cramped{E_{\text{exc}}}}}},\surd\tfrac{\hbar\Omega_{o}}{E_{g}}\big),}

(212)

which combines with Eq. (
205
) to give the BIS-geodesic reduction [Eq. (
183
)].

For finite
s=2
which is appropriate to polarization scattering, one should expect the ratio
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{geo}/\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{intra}}_{BIS}
to deviate from unity. In practice, we find this deviation to be small: for the model calculation in Sec.
III
, the ratio turns out to be
1.08
for a source photon energy of
\hbar\Omega=0.8E_{0}
; cf. Fig.
5
(e).

D.6
Geodesic approximation for quasi-2D semiconductors

Having formulated the geodesic approximation for 3D direct-gap semiconductors, we would like to extend the notion to quasi-2D direct-gap semiconductors, as exemplified by the model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
).

By quasi-2D, we mean that the electronic band energies
E_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
and cell-periodic wave functions
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
are approximately independent of one wavevector coordinate, say,
k_{z}
. The former condition implies that the band gap is minimized not at a single
\boldsymbol{k}
-point but along a
\boldsymbol{k}
-line. In our model [Eq. (
13
)], this
\boldsymbol{k}
-line is parametrized by
\boldsymbol{k}=(0,0,k_{z})
, as illustrated by the purple line in Fig.
14
.
The latter condition on the wave function implies that the intraband Berry curvature vector is collinear with the z unit directional vector:
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}=\Omega^{z}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\vec{z}
, and that the shift current vanishes in the z direction. Indeed, a nonzero z-component of the photonic/phononic shift vector requires that
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
nontrivially depend on
k_{z}
, as deducible from Eqs. (
26
)-(
28
) and Eq. (
84
).

Figure 14:
Quasi-two-dimensional conduction band plotted over
(E,k_{x},k_{z})
, with
E
parametrizing an implicit vertical axis.

Let us then consider the shift current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\perp}
orthogonal to
\vec{z}
. We would like to demonstrate that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\perp}
is well approximated by Eq. (
182
), with
loop[\boldsymbol{k}]
reinterpreted as a
planar geodesic loop
: a geodesic loop confined to the
k_{x}-k_{y}
plane that contains
\boldsymbol{k}
. Precisely, we mean that all nodes in
loop[\boldsymbol{k}]
have identical values for
k_{z}
, and
\boldsymbol{k}
is connected by a geodesic path to the extremal wavevector that lies closest to
\boldsymbol{k}
, as illustrated by the green trajectory in Fig.
14
. A consequence of
loop[\boldsymbol{k}]
being planar is that the affinity shift loop in Eq. (
182
) simplifies to a planar integral:

\displaystyle{\langle f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}\rangle_{\omega}=\int\frac{dk_{x}dk_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}a_{z}}\frac{\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)}{JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}}f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}_{loop[\boldsymbol{k}]}.}

(213)

We have introduced a lattice constant
a_{z}
such that
\int dk_{z}=2\pi/a_{z}
. The z component of
\boldsymbol{k}
in the integrand can be arbitrarily chosen, and the integrand only depends on band energies and wave functions within the arbitrarily chosen
k_{x}-k_{y}
plane. This justifies our use of the planar model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
), which explicitly depends on
k_{x}
and
k_{y}
but not
k_{z}
.

To recapitulate, being quasi-two-dimensional allows to simplify the loop analysis to planar loops, as if the problem were strictly two-dimensional. Such a simplification is not a priori obvious, since a hot photo-excited electron with initial wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
(on the excitation surface) may relax to any point along the conduction-band minimum, including points which differ from
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
in the z component [cf. pink trajectory in Fig.
14
]. If
subgraph[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]
, the probability-flow subgraph that includes the link
(c\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}>v\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
[cf. Fig.
13
], is decomposed into loops, one expects to find loops which are extended in the
k_{z}
direction.

Let us denote the pink-colored trajectory by
p_{c}
;
the lower-energy boundary point of
p_{c}
corresponds to the wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{rec}
of recombination; it is assumed the electron traces a path
p_{v}
from Bloch label
(v,\boldsymbol{k}_{exc})
to
(v,\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})
, which is not illustrated in Fig.
14
. Altogether,
p_{c},p_{v}
