and the vertical links at
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{rec}
combine to form
loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})
; its associated shift loop
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})}
is defined through Eq. (
171
). We define
P\circ loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})
as the projection of
loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})
onto the
k_{x}-k_{y}
plane containing
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
. If
k_{\text{exc}}^{z}=0
, then this projection amounts to setting
k_{z}=0
for all nodes along the loop, such that the pink trajectory collapses to the green trajectory in Fig.
14
.

We would prove that the shift loop is invariant under such a projection:
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})}=\boldsymbol{S}_{P\circ loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})}
. Points (i-iii) in App.
D.4
can be used to show that
\boldsymbol{S}_{loop(\boldsymbol{k}_{exc},\boldsymbol{k}_{rec})}
has the same form as the right-hand side of Eq. (
6
), with
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
defined as in Eq. (
6
) but with
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{k}_{rec}
. Because the photonic shift vector [Eq. (
28
)] and the interband Berry connection is purely a function of
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
which is
k_{z}
-independent, the two photonic terms in Eq. (
6
) are invariant under changing the z component of
\boldsymbol{k}_{rec}
. What remains is to demonstrate a similar invariance for the anomalous component of the shift loop, which is given by a sum of the two line integrals in Eq. (
6
).
p_{c}
differs from
P\circ p_{c}
only in that the
\boldsymbol{k}
-path of integration is extended in the
k_{z}
direction [Fig.
14
]. Since
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\vec{z}
is
k_{z}
-independent, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c,P\circ\boldsymbol{k}}\times P\circ d\boldsymbol{k}
, meaning
\int_{p_{c}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}
is invariant under projecting
p_{c}\rightarrow P\circ p_{c}
. The same argument and conclusion holds for
v\rightarrow c
. This completes our proof of invariance for the shift loop.

It is a straightforward generalization to demonstrate that the invariance property:
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{loop}}=\boldsymbol{S}_{P\circ loop}
holds for any loop, not just the simple loop we considered above. Thus for the purpose of evaluating the loop current contribution by
subgraph[\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}]
[Eq. (
179
)], one may as well project the entire subgraph to the
k_{x}-k_{y}
plane containing
\boldsymbol{k}_{exc}
.

At this point, one may apply essentially the same arguments that led to approximating Eq. (
179
) by Eq. (
182
), with the only modification being that all loops are now planar, and in particular,
loop(\boldsymbol{k})
in Eqs. (
180
)-(
181
) is a planar geodesic loop. This completes the proof of Eq. (
213
).

Appendix E
The transient current approximates the excitation-induced current

App.
B
establishes concepts and notations which are prerequisite to understanding this section.

We focus on the photo-excited carrier density regime:
n\lesssim n_{h}
, where energy relaxation in the active region is dominated by optical phonons. Assuming that the excitation energy [Eq. (
43
)] of photo-excited carriers lies in the active region, we would demonstrate that the transient current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}
is well approximated by the excitation-induced component
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
[cf. Eq. (
46
)] of the steady current.

Before the tackling the transient and non-equilibrium currents, let us take a step back to consider an equilibrated mix of electrons, photons and phonons in the absence of the light source. The quasiparticle occupancy then follows the Fermi-Dirac distribution:
f^{T_{0}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
31
) ], while the occupancy of photons and phonons follow the Planck distribution:

N_{m}^{T_{0}}
[Eq. (
32
)] with the same equilibrium temperature. The shift current, viewed as a functional of the quasiparticle, photon and phonon occupancies [Eq. (
30
)], vanishes:

\displaystyle{\text{Equilibrium:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B},N^{T_{0};phot}_{m},N^{T_{0};phon}_{m}]=0,}

(214)

owing to detailed balance; cf. Eq. (
38
).

At the onset of turning on a light source (with frequency
\omega_{s}
, mode
m_{s}
, polarization
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
), the quasiparticles and phonons retain their equilibrium distributions, but the photon occupancy is modified to
N^{T_{0};phot}_{m}+\Delta N_{s}\delta_{m,m_{s}}
. We define
the transient current as the current at the onset of radiation:

\displaystyle{\text{Onset:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B},N^{T_{0};phot}_{m}+\Delta N_{s}\delta_{m,m_{s}},N^{T_{0};phon}_{m}].}

(215)

All bosonic modes with a thermal occupancy cannot contribute to the shift current, owing to detailed balance; cf. Eq. (
38
). For the source mode
m_{s}
, the net transition rate [Eq. (
28
)] can be decomposed just as in Eq. (
157
), with
T
replaced by
T_{0}
. Because the first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
157
) vanishes by detailed balance [Eq. (
38
)], the transient current is simply proportional to the source-generated photon occupancy:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{C\leftarrow V}\frac{(2\pi e)^{2}\omega_{s}}{{\cal V}}\;\big|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\big|^{2}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)f^{T_{0}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta N_{s}.}

(216)

The formula here assumes a two-band semiconducting model [App.
A.3
], but more generally one would just sum over contributions from all resonant interband transitions.
It should be borne in mind that
f^{T_{0}}_{C}
is exponentially suppressed with exponent
E_{g}/k_{B}T_{e}\gg 1
for an intrinsic semiconductor:

\displaystyle{f^{T_{0}}_{C}\ll 1,\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;1-f^{T_{0}}_{V}\ll 1.}

(217)

Eq. (
216
) manifests that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}\neq 0
must originate solely from the disruption of detailed balance between pairs of Bloch states that are resonantly coupled by the light source, i.e., pairs labelled
(c\boldsymbol{k})
and
(v\boldsymbol{k})
, with
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface
ES
; cf. Eq. (
42
). It follows that in evaluating
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}},\ldots]
, one may as well restrict the wavevector summations
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
in Eq. (
25
) with the condition
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES
:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}_{B},N^{T_{0};phot}_{m}+\Delta N_{s}\delta_{m,m_{s}},N^{T_{0};phon}_{m}]_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}.}

(218)

As derived in App.
A.4
, the excitation-induced component of the steady shift current [cf. Eq. (
46
)] differs from Eq. (
216
) only in that
f_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{T_{0}}
is replaced by the non-equilibrium
f_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{C}-f_{V}
. If one accepts that the non-equilibrium quasiparticle distribution over the excitation surface satisfies:

\displaystyle{\forall\boldsymbol{k}\in ES:\;\;\;\;f_{C}\ll 1,\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;1-f_{V}\ll 1,}

(219)

then
f_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\approx f^{T_{0}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
(on the excitation surface), and therefore the excitation-induced current approximates the transient current:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{tran}}=\boldsymbol{j}[f^{T_{0}}]|_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}\approx\boldsymbol{j}[f]|_{\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in ES}=\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}},}

(220)

in accordance with Eqs. (
216
)-(
218
).

For
n\ll n_{h}
, we believe the inequalities in Eq. (
219
) hold generally, due to an argument presented in the main text and reproduced here: the smallness of
f_{C}
and
(1-f_{V})
originates from the slowness in optical excitations compared to the fastness of energy relaxation by carrier-carrier and carrier-phonon scatterings.

We will flesh out this argument by deriving an explicit expression of
f_{C}
for the kinetic model set up in App.
B.2
. This model
encodes certain assumptions which caricature reality, as detailed in App.
B.2.1
and App.
B.2.2
. Thus our explicit expression for
f_{C}
should be understood as an order-of-magnitude estimate for more realistic distributions; this is fine because the advertised inequality [Eq. (
219
)] is a statement about orders of magnitude.

With this caveat in mind, let us reproduce from Eq. (
64
) the kinetic equation for the iso-energy-averaged quasiparticle distribution
f_{E}
in the conduction band:

\displaystyle{E>\hbar\Omega_{o}:\;\;\;\;G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\delta(E-E_{c,exc})-\frac{g_{E}f_{E}}{\tau^{o}_{E}}+\frac{g_{E_{+}}f_{E_{+}}}{\tau^{o}_{E_{+}}}+\partial_{E}\bigg[\frac{g_{E}E}{\tau^{s}_{E}}\bigg(1+k_{B}T_{e}\partial_{E}\bigg)f_{E}\bigg]=0;\;\;\;\;E_{+}=E+\hbar\Omega_{o}.}

(221)

We assume the reader has read the discussion leading to Eq. (
64
), and we will not repeat the definitions and descriptions of each term in the kinetic equation. However, we will mention two slight differences between the above equation and Eq. (
64
):

(i) We have dropped the interband recombination term
[-g_{E}f_{E}/\tau_{\text{rec}}]
that was present in Eq. (
64
). This is alright for
E>\hbar\Omega_{o}
(the active region), because electron-optical-phonon scattering results in a substantially larger loss rate:
-{g_{E}f_{E}}/{\tau^{o}_{E}}
, given that
\tau_{\text{rec}}\sim 1\,ns
and
\tau^{o}_{E}\sim 100fs
.
Lundstrom 2010
;
Na et al. 2019
;
Sturman and Fridkin 1992

(ii) The diffusive Fokker-Planck term in Eq. (
221
) carries a more general meaning than the corresponding term in Eq. (
64
):

(ii-a) For
n\ll n_{l}
, the diffusive term encodes electron-acoustic-phonon scattering, and

\tau^{s}\equiv\tau_{A}
is the energy relaxation time due to spontaneous emission of acoustic phonons; a typical value is
\tau_{A}\sim 1\,ns
.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
;
Zakharchenya et al. 1982

(ii-b) For
n_{h}\gg n\gg n_{l}
, the diffusive term encodes electron-electron scattering, and
\tau^{s}=\tau_{ee}
is the time taken for a hot ‘test electron’ (with initial energy
\gg k_{B}T_{e}
) to cool down to an energy comparable to
k_{B}T_{e}
.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
By assumption for this density regime, electron-electron scattering is more efficient in relaxing an electron’s energy than electron-acoustic-phonon scattering, meaning
\tau^{ee}\ll\tau^{A}\sim 1ns
. It is also possible for electron-electron collisions to establish an electron temperature
T_{e}
that exceeds the lattice temperature
T_{l}
.
Esipov and Levinson 1987

The solution to the differential equation [Eq. (
221
)] has been derived in Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1984
and Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
. Here, we extract a few salient facts from these references that help to prove Eq. (
219
): In the absence of the secondary scattering process (
\tau^{s}_{E}\rightarrow\infty
), the distribution is a sum of Dirac-delta functions centered at
E_{k}:=E_{c;exc}-k\hbar\Omega_{o}
. The effects of the secondary scatterers is that each peak shifts as:

E_{k}\rightarrow E_{k}-\eta_{E_{k}}(k+1)\hbar\Omega_{o}
,
as well as broadens to a regular function. Assuming
\hbar\Omega_{o}/k_{B}{T_{e}}\gtrsim 1
and
k\sim 1
, the width of each peak remains small compared to
\hbar\Omega_{o}
. The highest peak
(k=0)
has the functional form:

\displaystyle{f_{E}=&\;\frac{G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\tau^{o}}{g}\frac{\varrho-1}{4k_{B}T_{e}\sqrt{\varrho}}\exp\bigg[-x-\sqrt{\varrho}|x|\bigg]\bigg|_{x=(E-E_{c;exc})/2k_{B}T_{e}};\;\;\;\;\varrho=1+4\frac{k_{B}T_{e}}{\hbar\Omega_{o}}\eta^{-1},}

(222)

with
g,\tau^{o}
and
\eta
evaluated at
E_{c;exc}
.
72
72

72

The solution presented in Ref.
Esipov and Levinson 1987
is missing a factor of
1/k_{B}T_{e}
, which we presume is a minor typographical oversight.
In particular,

\displaystyle{f_{E_{c;exc}}=&\;\frac{G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\tau^{o}}{g\hbar\Omega_{o}\eta\sqrt{\varrho}}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\hbar\Omega_{o}/k_{B}T_{e}\ll 1\\
\approx\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{2}\frac{G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}}{g}\sqrt{\frac{\tau^{s}\tau^{o}}{E_{c;exc}k_{B}T_{e}}}.}

(223)

Let us estimate
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}/g
under realistic experimental conditions. Recalling
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}{\cal I}_{rad}/\hbar\omega
from Eq. (
65
), and assuming typical values for the lattice period
a\sim 5\mbox{\AA}
, density of states
g\sim eV/a^{3}
, absorption coefficient
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\sim 10^{3}cm^{-1}
,
DiDomenico and Wemple 1968
and continuous-wave laser intensity
{\cal I}_{rad}\sim 40Wcm^{-2}
,
Koch et al. 1975
one finds a modest value for
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}/g\sim 10eV/s
.

f_{E_{c;exc}}
is the product of
G_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}/g
with a quantity which has dimensions of time over energy. This quantity encodes the microscopic energy relaxation processes, which occur at much shorter time scales than
1s
: as a reminder,
\tau^{o}\sim 100fs
and
\tau^{s}\lesssim 1\,ns
.
Lundstrom 2010
;
Na et al. 2019
;
Esipov and Levinson 1987
;
Zakharchenya et al. 1982
Thus,
f_{E_{c;exc}}\ll 1
even at the low temperature of
T_{e}\sim 1\,K
. Given that
f_{C}\ll 1
for
\boldsymbol{k}\in ES
,
1-f_{V}=f_{C}\ll 1
immediately follows from the electron-hole symmetry of our model; cf. App.
B.2.1
. This completes our demonstration of Eq. (
219
).

Appendix F
Model calculations with optical vortices

This appendix details the model calculations that support certain claims stated in Sec.
IV
, which we reproduce here for easy reference:

(I)
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{x},\omega}
is dominated by the recombination-induced current;

(II)
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\vec{y},\omega}
is dominated by the excitation-induced and intraband currents;

(III) The signs of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}
differ over a broad range of frequencies.

(IV) The linear disparity in the conductivity is large:
|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}|\sim mAV^{-2}
.

(V) The current response to unpolarized light is given by:
|\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}|/2\sim 0.1mAV^{-2}
.

Some aspects of the following demonstration will be a more quantitative elaboration of qualitative arguments made in Sec.
IV
.

The form of our model Hamiltonian is identical to the one studied in the context of the anomalous shift; cf. Eq. (
13
). Having studied the case of
\tilde{Q}=1
, we now tune
\tilde{Q}
from positive to negative values. The conduction and valence bands touch (at
\boldsymbol{k}=0
) when
\tilde{Q}=0
and subsequently untouch for negative
\tilde{Q}
. This untouching is accompanied by the nucleation of two time-reversal-related
\vec{x}
-vortex lines at
(\tilde{k}_{x},\tilde{k}_{y})\approx(\pm\sqrt{-\tilde{Q}/(1-\tilde{Q}/2)},0)
, as illustrated in Fig.
15
(a); there are no
\vec{y}
-vortices in this model [Fig.
15
(b)]. Henceforth, we fix
\tilde{Q}=-1
.

Figure 15:
Characterization of the model Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
) with
\tilde{Q}=-1
. Panels (a,c,e) are characterizations for a light source with linear polarization vector
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
, and (b,d,f) for
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{y}
. The pink dots in (a,c,e) represent the
\boldsymbol{k}
-locations of
\vec{x}
-vortices. Panels (a) and (b) depict the photonic shift vector field
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
respectively. For panels (c) and (d), the red ellipse represents the excitation surface for a photon frequency
\hbar\omega=4.5E_{0}
; arrows on the ellipse represent the vectors
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface; the central arrow represents the recombination component of the affinity shift loop:
ASL^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},4.5E_{0}}
; cf. Eq. (
224
). For panels (e) and (f), the size of the red dots indicates the magnitude of
|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}
for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface; the colored background represents the Berry curvature scalar field
\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
in units of
a^{2}=({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}})^{2/3}
, with a color legend on the right.

Firstly, let us consider the case of an
\vec{x}
-polarized light source and make the case that the excitation-induced current is outweighed by the recombination-induced current:
||\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}[\vec{x}]||\ll||\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}[\vec{x}]||
, owing to the vortex-induced orientational disorder of the photonic shift vector field. This inequality simplifies to
||\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\text{exc},y}_{\vec{x},\omega}||\ll||\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{rec,y}_{\vec{x},\omega}||
for the
y
-component of the shift conductivity [Eqs. (
6
)-(
11
)], because a mirror symmetry (
x\rightarrow-x
) of the model Hamiltonian
73
73

73

M_{x}H(\boldsymbol{k})M_{x}^{-1}=H(-k_{x},k_{y})
with
M_{x}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{3}
.
constrains the
x
component of the shift current to vanish, while the
z
component vanishes owing to the quasi-two-dimensionality of the model; cf. App.
D.6
. Because the shift conductivity is essentially the product of the joint density of states (
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}
) [cf. Eq. (
9
)] with the affinity shift loop [cf. Eqs. (
7
)-(
10
)], one may as well compare the excitation and recombination components of the affinity shift loop:

\displaystyle{ASL^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}\equiv\langle|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}S^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{y,c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle_{\omega}\;\;\;\;\text{vs}\;\;\;\;ASL^{\text{rec}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}\equiv\langle|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}S_{y,rec},}

(224)

for
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\vec{x}
and
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\omega}
denoting an average over the excitation surface; cf. Eq. (
8
).
S_{y}
means the
y
component of
\boldsymbol{S}
, and
S^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{y,c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
is the photonic shift vector defined in Eq. (
28
). The recombination shift
\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
is defined in Eq. (
6
) but simplifies in the present context to
\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
, owing to a mirror-symmetry-imposed dipole selection rule.
74
74

74

Conduction- and valence-band states with
k_{x}=0
transform under different representations of mirror symmetry
M_{x}
, hence
A^{y}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=A^{z}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=0
. This implies for any
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
that is not orthogonal to
\vec{x}
that
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg A^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
.
A numerical calculation of Eq. (
224
) reveals for a wide range of photon frequencies that
ASL_{\vec{x}}^{\text{exc}}
and
ASL_{\vec{x}}^{\text{rec}}
have opposite signs, and that
|ASL_{\vec{x}}^{\text{exc}}|\ll|ASL_{\vec{x}}^{\text{rec}}|
by a multiplicative factor ranging from 1/5 to 1/8, as illustrated in Fig.
16
(a).

Figure 16:
The black curves in panels (a) and (b) represents the affinity shift loop
\langle|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}}_{y,loop}\rangle_{\omega}
(in units of
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
) vs the photon frequency
\omega
, for polarization
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}=\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
respectively. Non-black curves represent the three components of the affinity shift loop: excitation (red curve), intraband relaxation (green curve), and recombination (blue curve). Panel (c) averages the affinity shift loop over two orthogonal light polarizations.

To rationalize this multiplicative factor, we illustrate
|A^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
as arrows in
Fig.
15
(c), for
\boldsymbol{k}
along a representative excitation surface encircling the
\vec{x}
-vortices. The central arrow in Fig.
15
(c) represents
\langle|A^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
. All arrows are drawn with a common scale to allow for mutual comparison. It is evident that proximity to the
\vec{x}
-vortex causes the direction of
|A^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
to rotate along the excitation surface; the average of
|A^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}
over the excitation surface is therefore diminished; this average just equals
ASL^{\text{exc}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon},\omega}
; cf. Eq. (
224
). In contrast, recombination occurs in the vicinity of the extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}
, where the photonic shift vector is roughly constant. Thus follows a general principle: ceteris paribus, the orientational disorder induced by
\vec{x}
-vorticity reduces
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}[\vec{x}]
relative to
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}[\vec{x}]
, for an
\vec{x}
-polarized source.

Ceteris paribus, the same orientational disorder reduces
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}[\vec{x}]
relative to
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}[\vec{y}]
, for reasons explained in Sec.
IV
. This implies a linear disparity of the excitation-induced current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
, which applies to a broad range of photon frequencies; compare red curves of Fig.
16
(a) and (b).

To understand the linear disparity of the intraband current
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
, we have indicated the
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent magnitude of
|A^{x}_{cv}|^{2}
(resp.
|A^{y}_{cv}|^{2}
) by the size of dots imprinted over the excitation surface in Fig.
15
(e) [resp. Fig.
15
(f)]; in both figures, the same Berry curvature scalar field (
\Omega^{z}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
) is represented by a color plot. It may be seen that
|A^{x}_{cv}|^{2}
and
|A^{y}_{cv}|^{2}
are both anisotropic over the excitation surface, but each favors a different segment of the excitation surface, for reasons explained in Sec.
IV
. We deduce for the
\vec{y}
-polarized source that the predominant relaxation pathways are roughly parallel to
k_{x}
[cf. Fig.
7
(f)] and intersect the Berry-curvature hot spots, leading to a larger anomalous shift than the case of the
\vec{x}
-polarized source. Once again, this effect is not limited to a fine-tuned photon frequency; compare green curves of Fig.
16
(a) and (b).

Altogether, the linear disparity of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
results in the net shift current being dominated by
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{intra}}
for a
\vec{y}
-polarized source [cf. black curve in Fig.
16
(b) and claim (II)], and by
\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
for a
\vec{x}
-polarized source [black curve in Fig.
16
(a) and claim (I)]; the net current changes sign if the polarization is flipped [claim (III)]. The linear disparity of the affinity shift loop [i.e., the difference of the two black curves in Fig.
16
(a) vs (b)] is comparable to
-1
(in units of
{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
, the real-space volume of the primitive unit cell) over a broad range of frequencies; this corresponds to a linear disparity of the conductivity:
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega}\approx 2mAV^{-2}
[cf. Eq. (
10
), Fig.
2
(d) and claim (IV)], assuming a generic value for
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}eV)^{-1}
.
75
75

75

We choose
\tilde{Q}=-1
and
\tilde{P}=12
such that
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}}\approx({\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}eV)^{-1}
.

The response to an unpolarized light source is given by
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{x},\omega}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{y}_{\vec{y},\omega})/2
, which
\approx 0.2mAV^{-2}
over a broad range of frequencies [cf. black curve in Fig.
16
(c) and claim (V)].

We end this appendix with a caveat: the calculated values of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
should be taken with a grain of salt. A reliable calculation of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
should also account for the dependence of Bloch wave functions over continuous space,
Alexandradinata 2022
but such dependence is discarded when the Hilbert space is reduced to a two-dimensional vector space at each
\boldsymbol{k}
point, as was done for all model Hamiltonians in this work. A more realistic model would incorporate ab-initio-derived wave functions as additional model parameters.
Ibañez Azpiroz et al. 2018
;
Ibañez-Azpiroz et al. 2022
Reassuringly, our qualitative arguments for vortex-induced shifts do not rely on the two-band approximation and are equally applicable to realistic, continuous-space Hamiltonians.

Appendix G
Chern-vorticity theorem

The Chern-vorticity theorem in Eq. (
15
) relates the Chern numbers (
C_{v},C_{c}
) of the valence and conduction states (over any closed 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
) to the net optical vorticity
(Vort)
within
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.

To prove the theorem, we first recall that if
C_{v}({\rm resp.}\ C_{v})\neq 0
, the wave function cannot be made continuous and periodic over
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{A}_{vv\boldsymbol{k}}({\rm resp.}\ \boldsymbol{A}_{cc\boldsymbol{k}})
must be singular somewhere on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. To be concrete, supposing
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
were a two-torus; Fig.
17
illustrates how
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is decomposed into two patches, such that the wave function in the interior of each patch is analytic in
\boldsymbol{k}
, but
\boldsymbol{A}_{vv\boldsymbol{k}}({\rm resp.}\ \boldsymbol{A}_{cc\boldsymbol{k}})
is singular at the patch boundary:

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}+\int_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\right)\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot d\boldsymbol{k}=C_{b},\ \operatorname{with}\ b=v,c.

(225)

Here,
C_{b}
is the Chern number of the band labelled by
b
, and
\delta
is an infinitesimal parameter illustrated in Fig.
17
.

Performing the same line integral with the Berry connection replaced by the photonic shift vector gives zero for any linear polarization vector
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle{\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}+\int_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\right)\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{c\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow v\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot d\boldsymbol{k}=0,}

(226)

because the shift vector is gauge invariant and smoothly defined except at optical vortices, and one can always choose the patch boundary to avoid those vortex points.

Comparing the last two equations with the definition of the photonic shift vector in Eq. (
3
), one infers that there must be nonzero circulations in
\arg[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}]
to compensate for the singularity of
\boldsymbol{A}_{vv\boldsymbol{k}}({\rm resp.}\ \boldsymbol{A}_{cc\boldsymbol{k}})
. Specifically,

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}+\int_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\right)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}}\arg[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}]\cdot d\boldsymbol{k}=C_{c}-C_{v},

(227)

which means that the net phase vorticity of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
over
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is
C_{c}-C_{v}
. The line integral over
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) equals the winding number of
\operatorname{arg}[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}]
in patch 1 (resp. patch 2), and is thus topologically invariant upon contracting the
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) to infinitesimally encircle any optical vortex in patch 1 (resp. patch 2); this contraction is illustrated in Fig.
17
. This invariance implies that Equation (
227
) is equivalent to Equation (
15
).

Figure 17:
Illustration of the two-patch decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, as well as the paths for the line integrals in Eqs. (
225
) and (
227
). The magenta crosses represent the optical vortices.

Appendix H
Supporting our case study of BiTeI

Our case study of BiTeI is based on a four-band Hamiltonian
H_{\text{BiTeI}}(\boldsymbol{k})
[cf. Eq. (
16
)] with energies ordered as
E_{1}\leq E_{2}<E_{3}\leq E_{4}
. We focus on photon frequencies which resonantly excite quasiparticles from the highest-energy valence band to the lowest-energy conduction band:
b^{\prime}=3
and
b=2
. Minimizing
E_{3\boldsymbol{k}}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{k}
defines a circular ring contained in the zero-
k_{z}
plane; maximizing
E_{2\boldsymbol{k}}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{k}
also defines a circular ring contained in the zero-
k_{z}
plane; actually, the two rings coincide, as suggested pictorially in Fig.
9
. This coincidence may be rationalized: the O(2) symmetry about the z axis, combined with time reversal symmetry (
T
), imply the existence of a
C_{2z}T
symmetry (two-fold rotation composed with time reversal) which maps
\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow(k_{x},k_{y},-k_{z})
; thus if
E_{3\boldsymbol{k}}
is minimized on a single O(2)-symmetric ring, this ring must lie on the
C_{2z}T
-symmetric plane with
k_{z}=0
. Within this plane, the Hamiltonian has a chiral symmetry that relates positive to negative energies:

\displaystyle{\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}H_{\text{BiTeI}}(k_{x},k_{y},0)\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}=-H_{\text{BiTeI}}(k_{x},k_{y},0),}

(228)

which implies that
E_{2k_{x}k_{y}0}
is maximized wherever
E_{3k_{x}k_{y}0}
is minimized. We will refer to this ring as the
band-edge ring
.

H.1
Effective description by a massive Dirac fermion

Near the topological phase transition between a trivial insulator and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological insulator, the two bands that touch are effectively described by a massive Dirac fermion in two momentum dimensions. Here, we provide a detailed derivation of the massive Dirac Hamiltonian in the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane [c.f. Eq. (
18
)].

We start by restricting
H_{BiTeI}
[Eq. (
16
)] to the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane and Taylor-expanding the Hamiltonian around
\mathbf{k}_{0}=(\lambda,0,0)
:

\displaystyle{H_{BiTeI}=&\;H_{0}+H_{1};{\\
}H_{0}=\;\hbar v\lambda(\tau_{1}\sigma_{3}-\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}),{\\
}H_{1}=\;(m^{\prime}-2A\lambda q_{x})\tau_{3}\sigma_{0}-\hbar v(q_{x}\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}+q_{z}\tau_{2}\sigma_{3})+O(q^{2}).}

We have introduced wavenumbers
q_{x}
and
q_{z}
which are the deviations from
\mathbf{k}_{0}
in the
k_{x^{+}}-k_{z}
half-plane;
\tau_{i}\sigma_{j}
is the Kronecker product of
\tau_{i}
and
\sigma_{j}
, i.e.,
\tau_{i}\otimes\sigma_{j}

Wikipedia contributors 2024
.
\mathbf{k}_{0}
is a point where the bands touch during the topological phase transition; by construction,
H_{1}
vanishes at
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
when
m^{\prime}=0
, and the touching bands correspond to the two zero-energy eigenstates of
H_{0}
, which
we label as
|\,1\,\rangle=(i,1,0,0)^{T}/\sqrt{2}
and
|\,2\,\rangle=(0,0,-i,1)^{T}/\sqrt{2}
.

In the low-energy subspace spanned by
|\,1\,\rangle
and
|\,2\,\rangle
, the effective Hamiltonian is given by degenerate perturbation theory as:

H^{\prime}_{i,j}=\langle\,i\,|H_{1}|\,j\,\rangle,\ i,j=1,2.

(229)

Given that

\langle\,i\,|\tau_{3}\sigma_{0}|\,j\,\rangle=(\gamma_{3})_{ij},\langle\,i\,|\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}|\,j\,\rangle=-(\gamma_{1})_{ij},\langle\,i\,|\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}|\,j\,\rangle=-(\gamma_{2})_{ij}\ i,j=1,2,

(230)

with
\gamma_{1,2,3}
being Pauli matrices of the Hilbert space spanned by
|\,1\,\rangle
and
|\,2\,\rangle
,
Eq. (
230
) directly gives Eq. (
18
) in the main text.

H.2
Vanishing shift at the band edge, for
x
- and
y
-polarized light

This section aims to explain why
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{exc}}
dominates over
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{rec}}
in the low-frequency regime of Fig.
8
(c).
This reduces to explaining the smallness of
the recombination shift vector
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
[Eq. (
6
)] relative to the excitation shift vector [Eq. (
6
)], according to the average-shift-loop formula in Eqs. (
6
)-(
10
). Given that
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
is an affinity-weighted average of
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}
over all polarization vectors of the spontaneously emitted photon [Eq. (
6
)], it may be argued that
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}_{\text{rec}}
is small because of the vanishing of the band-edge shift vectors

\displaystyle{\text{On the band-edge ring:}\;\;\;\;\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{y}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}=0.}

(231)

This is equivalent to the vanishing of the band-edge shift connections,

\displaystyle{\text{On the band-edge ring:}\;\;\;\;\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}|\vec{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}|^{2}=\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{y}}_{v\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}\leftarrow c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}|\vec{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{ext}}|^{2}=0,}

(232)

because the optical affinity is non-vanishing throughout the band-edge ring; after all, there are no optical vortex loops intersecting the band-edge ring, as illustrated in Fig.
9
.

For any tight-binding Hamiltonian
H(\boldsymbol{k})
, the photonic shift connection can be expressed as
Cook et al. 2017

\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\vec{x}}_{b^{\prime}\leftarrow b}|\vec{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b}|^{2}=\operatorname{Im}\left\{\frac{\overline{{v}^{x}_{bb^{\prime}}}}{(\omega_{bb^{\prime}})^{2}}\left[\langle\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\partial_{z}\partial_{x}H|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle-\frac{v^{z}_{bb^{\prime}}\Delta^{x}_{bb^{\prime}}+v^{x}_{bb^{\prime}}\Delta^{z}_{bb^{\prime}}}{\omega_{bb^{\prime}}}+\sum_{b^{\prime\prime}\neq b,b^{\prime}}\left(\frac{v_{bb^{\prime\prime}}^{z}v_{b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}}^{x}}{\omega_{bb^{\prime\prime}}}-\frac{v_{bb^{\prime\prime}}^{x}v_{b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}}^{z}}{\omega_{b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}}}\right)\right]\right\}.

(233)

Here,
\partial_{z}\equiv\partial_{k_{z}}
,
v^{z}_{bb^{\prime}}=\langle\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\frac{1}{\hbar}\partial_{z}H|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
,
\Delta^{z}_{bb^{\prime}}=\partial_{z}E_{b}-\partial_{z}E_{b^{\prime}}
,
\omega_{bb^{\prime}}=(E_{b}-E_{b^{\prime}})/\hbar
and
\sum_{b^{\prime\prime}\neq b,b^{\prime}}
means to sum over all band indices
b^{\prime\prime}
which are neither
b
nor
b^{\prime}
.

Let us show that Eq. (
233
) vanishes for
H=H_{BiTeI}
throughout the band-edge ring:

(i) The first term in the square bracket of Eq.
233
vanishes, because
H_{\text{BiTeI}}
depends quadratically on
\boldsymbol{k}
as
k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2}
.

(ii) The second term in the square bracket vanishes, because band energy functions are extremized at the band edge:
\partial_{z}E_{3}=\partial_{z}E_{2}=0
.

(iii) The third term also vanishes, but the argument is longer: firstly, observe from Eq. (
16
) that
\partial_{z}H_{\text{BiTeI}}\big|_{k_{z}=0}=\tau_{2}\sigma_{3}
is simply the chirality operator in Eq. (
228
), meaning that
\partial_{z}H_{\text{BiTeI}}
maps between energy eigenstate with inverted energies. This implies
\hbar v_{bb^{\prime\prime}}^{z}=\langle\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\partial_{z}H_{\text{BiTeI}}|\,u_{b^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\big|_{k_{z}=0}
is only nonzero if
E_{b^{\prime\prime}}=-E_{b}
, but
E_{b^{\prime\prime}}=-E_{b}
cannot be satisfied because of the constraint
\sum_{b^{\prime\prime}\neq b,b^{\prime}}
in Eq. (
233
). A similar argument proves that
v_{b^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}}^{z}=0
, hence altogether the third term in the square bracket vanishes.

The above demonstration holds if one replaces
x\rightarrow y
, meaning that the z-component of the shift connection also vanishes for
\vec{y}
-polarized light, throughout the band-edge ring. This completes the proof of Eq. (
232
).

H.3
Asymptotic behavior of anomalous-shift integrals

One result in Sec.
V
was the
(1/E_{g})
-divergence of the intraband shift conductivity across the topological phase transition, with
|E_{g}|
the band gap and
\text{sgn}(E_{g})=-1
on the trivial side of the transition. This divergence relied on an inequality between two anomalous-shift integrals:

\left|\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}+\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}\right|\ll\left|\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}-\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}\right|,

(234)

which is asymptotically valid as
|E_{g}|
approaches zero;
\pm{\cal P}
are any pair of diametrically-opposite geodesic paths, as representatively illustrated in Fig.
18
.

Figure 18:
Representative pair of diametrically-opposite geodesic paths, with
-{\cal P}
in red and
{\cal P}
in blue. The precise meaning of ‘diametrically-opposite’ is that both paths approach the band-extremal wavevector (
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}
) along the same tangent line, which we illustrate as a grey dashed line with tangent vector
\vec{{\cal P}}
.

We have demonstrated in Sec.
V
that the right-hand side of Eq. (
234
) diverges as
1/E_{g}
, thus to prove Eq. (
234
) it suffices to show that the magnitude of the left integral is decreasing as
|E_{g}|\rightarrow 0
. This is the main result of this subsection.

To begin, consider the Berry curvature within one cross-section of the torus enclosed by the excitation surface, as exemplified by the
k_{x}^{+}
-
k_{x}
half plane. As
|E_{g}|\rightarrow 0
,
\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}/|d\boldsymbol{k}|=\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}
becomes localized to a ‘hot spot’ centered at the band-extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}
, with a spot width comparable to
|m^{\prime}|/\hbar v\propto|E_{g}|
. Indeed, writing the massive Dirac Hamiltonian as a dot product of three-vectors:

\displaystyle{H^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=(\hbar vq_{x},\hbar vq_{z},m^{\prime}-2A\lambda q_{x}),\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}),}

(235)

the conduction-band Berry curvature can be expressed as

\displaystyle{\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{h}=-\frac{1}{4d^{3}}\epsilon_{hij}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla_{k_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}\times\nabla_{k_{j}}\boldsymbol{d}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{\prime}}{D^{3/2}};\;\;\;\;D=&\;(d_{1})^{2}+(d_{2})^{1}+(d_{3})^{2};\;\;\;\;d=\sqrt{D},}

(236)

The
\boldsymbol{k}
-location of the Berry-curvature maximum can be identified by

\displaystyle{0=\partial_{s}\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}=\frac{3}{4}m^{\prime}\frac{\partial_{s}D}{D^{5/2}},}

(237)

with
(\partial_{s})^{m}
being the
m
’th-order derivative in the direction that is tangential to
\mathcal{P}
at the band extremum. The Berry-curvature maximum (of the hot spot) coincides (in
\boldsymbol{k}
-location) with the band-extremal wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}
; this is because the energy spectrum of
H^{\prime}
has an
E\rightarrow-E
symmetry at each
\boldsymbol{k}
, which implies that extremizing the
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent energy gap
(E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}})
is equivalent to extremizing the conduction-band energy:

\displaystyle{E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=\sqrt{D_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;0=\partial_{s}E_{c}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}}=\frac{\partial_{s}D}{\sqrt{D}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}}.}

(238)

The band gap is defined as the extremal value of
(E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}})
:

\displaystyle{|E_{g}|=2d\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}}=\frac{m^{\prime}}{\sqrt{1+u^{2}}};\;\;\;\;u=\frac{2A\lambda}{\hbar v},}

(239)

and the extremal value of the Berry curvature can be expressed in terms of the signed band gap (
E_{g}=\text{sgn}[m^{\prime}]|E_{g}|
) as

\displaystyle{\Omega_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}}^{y}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{m^{\prime}}{D^{3/2}}\bigg|_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}}=4\sqrt{1+u^{2}}\frac{E_{g}}{|E_{g}|^{3}}.}

(240)

That the width of the Berry-curvature hot spot is of order
|m^{\prime}|/\hbar v
can be deduced from dimensional analysis of Eq. (
236
), assuming that
|A\lambda|
is less than or comparable to
|\hbar v|
.

The localization of Berry curvature in momentum space allows to express the anomalous-shift integral as

\displaystyle{\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}+\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}=\int_{-\Lambda}^{\Lambda}\Omega^{(0)}_{s}\Theta_{s}ds+\text{correction},}

(241)

with a cutoff
\Lambda>0
for the integration variable
s
along the grey dashed tangent line in Fig.
18
; we have introduced
\Omega_{s}^{(0)}\equiv\Omega^{y}_{c\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}+s\vec{{\cal P}}}
, with
\vec{\mathcal{P}}
being the unit-norm vector parallel to the tangent line.

\Theta_{s}
is the symmetric step function that equals
-1
for positive
s
, and
+1
for negative
s
. This step function arises because
\pm{\cal P}
are oriented paths beginning on diametrically-opposed points on the excitation surface and ending at the same point:
\boldsymbol{k}_{ext,{\cal P}}
. For fixed
\Lambda
, it is evident that the magnitude of the correction in Eq. (
241
) decreases as
E_{g}\rightarrow 0
, owing to the increasing localization of the hot spot (which has a width
|m^{\prime}|/\hbar v\propto|E_{g}|
).

To prove the main result of this section, what remains is to show that
\int_{-\Lambda}^{\Lambda}\Omega^{(0)}_{s}\Theta_{s}ds
is also decreasing; actually, we will prove a stronger statement that this integral just vanishes. Indeed, so long as the massive-Dirac Hamiltonian has an energy gap (i.e.,
m^{\prime}\neq 0
),
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}
is an analytic function of
\boldsymbol{k}
and hence
\Omega^{(0)}_{s}
is an analytic function of
s
, meaning it admits a convergent Taylor expansion
\Omega^{(0)}_{s}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Omega^{(n)}_{0}s^{n}/n!
, with the
n
’th-order derivative
\Omega^{(n)}_{0}
to be
(\partial_{s})^{n}\Omega^{(0)}_{s}
evaluated at the band extremum (
s=0
).
Only the odd-order derivatives contribute to Eq. (
241
), owing to the symmetric step function
\Theta_{s}
being an odd function of
s
, hence

\displaystyle{\left(\int_{-\mathcal{P}}+\int_{\mathcal{P}}\right)\vec{z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\times d\boldsymbol{k}\approx 2\sum_{n\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}\frac{\Omega^{(n)}_{0}}{n!}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}s^{n}ds.}

(242)

The following discussion proves that
\Omega^{(n)}_{0}
vanishes for any odd
n
.
It follows from a dimensional analysis of Eq. (
236
) that the
n
’th-order derivative can be expanded as

\displaystyle{\Omega^{(n)}_{s}=\frac{1}{D^{3/2+n}}\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{2}\ldots\sum_{m_{n}=0}^{2}c_{m_{1}\ldots m_{n}}(\partial_{s_{1}})^{m_{1}}\ldots(\partial_{s_{n}})^{m_{n}}D_{s_{1}}\ldots D_{s_{n}}\bigg|^{m_{1}+\ldots m_{n}=n}_{s_{j}\rightarrow s},}

(243)

with linear coefficients
c_{m_{1}\ldots m_{n}}
that depend on
m_{j}
.
The meaning of the subscript
s_{j}\rightarrow s
is that after performing all the differentiations
[(\partial_{s_{1}})^{m_{1}}\ldots(\partial_{s_{n}})^{m_{n}}]
, the resultant function of
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
is to be replaced by a function of
(s,\ldots,s)
. The summations over
m_{j}
are restricted such that
m_{1}+m_{2}+\ldots+m_{n}=n
, and each
\sum_{m_{j}=0}^{2}
is capped at two, because
D
is a second-order polynomial of momenta variables, which follows from the linearization of the massive-Dirac Hamiltonian. If
\Omega^{(n)}_{s}
is evaluated at the band extremum
(s=0)
, then one can further drop
m_{j}=1
(in each of the summations over
m_{j}
) because
\partial_{s}D\big|_{s=0}=0
[cf. Eq. (
238
)]. It becomes apparent that the condition
m_{1}+\ldots m_{n}=n
cannot be satisfied for odd
n
and
m_{j}\in\{0,2\}
, implying that
\Omega^{(n)}_{0}=0
for odd
n
. This completes the proof.

Appendix I
Alternative derivation of the anomalous shift

We provide an alternative derivation of the anomalous shift vector [Eq. (
5
)] that aims to demystify the appearance of the Berry curvature.
Beginning from an expression for the intraband phononic shift that was derived in Sec.
III
and is valid for small momentum transfer:

\displaystyle{Phonon:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}\approx-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k})+\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}},}

(244)

with
\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
the intraband Berry connection,
\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}=(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})/2
and
\delta\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}
. We have omitted the band index to simplify notation. We consider the
y
-component of the above shift vector, and express the derivative as the limiting value of a difference:

\displaystyle{\vec{y}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k})=\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
\epsilon\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}\;\frac{(\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}+\epsilon\vec{y}/2}-\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}-\epsilon\vec{y}/2})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{k}}{\epsilon},}

(245)

such that the shift vector component can be expressed as a line integral of the connection:

\displaystyle{(\boldsymbol{S}^{m}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}})^{y}\approx\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lim}\\
\epsilon\rightarrow 0\end{subarray}\;\frac{1}{\epsilon}\oint\boldsymbol{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot d\boldsymbol{k}\approx(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{ave}}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k})^{y}}

(246)

along an infinitesimally-thin parallelogram drawn in Fig.
19
. Finally, one converts the line integral to an area integral of the curvature by Stokes’ theorem. This proof is easily generalized for the
x
and
z
components.

Figure 19:
Infinitesimally-thin parallelogram.

Appendix J
Difficulties of the parallel-transport gauge

It has been claimed in the literature that
\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}p}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=1+O(\delta k^{2})
can be chosen as a gauge choice for the wave function.
Vogl 1976
;
Antoncík and Landsberg 1963
This gauge corresponds to a parallel transport condition [
\delta\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=0
] in the direction of
\delta\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}
.
Soluyanov and Vanderbilt 2012
;
Alexandradinata et al. 2020

It is not uncommon to find textbooks which ignore the wave-function dependence of the electron-phonon scattering rate.
Lundstrom 2010
All Berry-curvature effects (including the anomalous shift [Eq. (
5
)]) are missed if one blithely adopts the parallel-transport gauge.

It is therefore of interest to expose the fallacies inherent in
\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}=1+O(\delta k^{2})
,
76
76

76

There is, of course, no controversy in the claim that
|\big\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\big\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}|^{2}=1+O(\delta k^{2})
; cf. Eq. (
57
).
of which there are two related kinds:

(i) For fixed
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
, it is generically impossible to set
\delta\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for all
\boldsymbol{k}
in the Brillouin zone; this is tantamount to assuming that the single-band Berry phase vanishes for all momentum loops parallel to
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
. This assumption may hold if
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
is orthogonal to a mirror plane, in which case the Berry phase of a single spinless band (in the absence of spin-orbit coupling) is indeed quantized to
0
or
\pi
, but one cannot rule out the case of
\pi
a priori.

(ii) For fixed
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
, it
is
possible to impose
\delta\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for all
\boldsymbol{k}
in a ball-shaped subregion of the Brillouin zone. However, it is generically impossible to simultaneously impose
\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}=0
within the same ball, for
\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
that is not collinear with
\delta\boldsymbol{k}
. The simultaneous imposition is equivalent to assuming a vanishing Berry phase for an infinitesimal loop encircling
\boldsymbol{k}
, i.e., that the Berry curvature
\delta\boldsymbol{k}\times\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{b}
vanishes at
\boldsymbol{k}
. Certainly, one must allow for phonons of all possible wavevectors (
\delta\boldsymbol{k},\delta\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in BZ
) to completely describe the electron-phonon interaction. Without finetuning, the Berry curvature
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{b}
vanishes at a generic
\boldsymbol{k}
-point only in
PT
-symmetric materials with negligible spin-orbit coupling.
Alexandradinata and Glazman 2018

PT
is certainly not a symmetry in the present case study of noncentric (meaning no
P
), non-magnetic (meaning
T
-symmetric) materials.

Appendix K
Energy conversion efficiency

We will derive an ideal expression for the energy conversion efficiency for Pusch et al.’s model
Pusch et al. 2023
of a shift-current photovoltaic cell. Our derivation closely follows that in Sec II of Ref.
Pusch et al. 2023
, which we recommend as prerequisite reading. However, our final expression for the efficiency [Eq. (
255
)] is less heuristic than Eq. (11) of Ref.
Pusch et al. 2023
, in that ours is wholly expressed in terms of kinetic and band-structure parameters which can be extracted from ab-initio calculations.

We adopt the same device geometry that is illustrated in Fig. 1 of Ref.
Pusch et al. 2023
: light falls onto a semiconductor facet with illuminated area
A_{illum}=dw
;
d
is the separation between two electrodes and
w
the width of each electrode. For concreteness, we will fix the facet’s normal vector to be parallel to the unit directional vector
\vec{z}
;
the photovoltaic current flows between the electrodes in the
x
direction, and
w
is the linear dimension of the electrode in the
y
direction. Assuming that the radiation falls onto the facet with normal incidence, the Poynting vector within the semiconductor decays exponentially as

\displaystyle{\text{Poynting vector}\;=&\;{\cal I}_{rad}(z)\vec{z};\;\;\;\;{\cal I}_{rad}(z)={\cal I}_{rad}(0)e^{-\alpha_{abs}z},}

(247)

with an attenuation length given by the inverse of the absorption coefficient:

\displaystyle{\alpha_{abs}=&\;\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{fs}}\frac{\hbar\omega}{n_{\omega}}\langle f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\rangle_{\omega}\,JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}.}

(248)

Here,
\alpha_{fs}\approx 137
is the fine structure,
n_{\omega}
is the refractive index,
f_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{V}-f_{C}
is a difference of the steady quasiparticle distributions, and
JDOS_{\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow\downarrow}}
is the spin-doubled joint density of states. Our semiclassical expression for the absorption coefficient presumes that
\alpha_{abs}^{-1}
greatly exceeds the lattice period; the same type of semiclassical approximation implies that the shift current density has the same exponential decay owing to being proportional to
{\cal I}_{rad}(z)
:

\displaystyle{j_{x}(z)=-|e|\mathfrak{s}\,\text{Abs}(z);\;\;\;\;\text{Abs}(z)=\frac{\alpha_{abs}{\cal I}_{rad}(z)}{\hbar\omega}.}

(249)

\mathfrak{s}
, the average shift per photo-excited electron-hole pair, has been defined in Eq. (
24
);

\text{Abs}(z)
is understood as the photon absorption rate per unit volume, at a distance
z
from the illuminated facet.
Eq. (
249
) is equivalent to
j_{x}=\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}|{\cal E}_{\omega}|^{2}
with
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s},\omega}
the geodesic-approximated shift conductivity in Eq. (
10
); to derive the equivalence, revert to Gaussian units and replace
|{\cal E}_{\omega}(z)|^{2}=2\pi{\cal I}_{rad}(z)/cn_{\omega}
[cf. footnote in App.
C.3
] and
e^{2}/\hbar c\approx 1/137.

Assuming ideally that the contacts with the electrodes do not introduce additional resistance, the energy conversion efficiency is given by

\displaystyle{\text{Eff}=&\;\frac{1}{4}\frac{V_{oc}I_{sc}}{{\cal I}_{rad}A_{illum}},}

(250)

with
V_{oc}
the open-circuit photovoltage and
I_{sc}
the short-circuit shift current. The latter quantity is obtained by integrating

\displaystyle{I_{sc}=w\int^{t}_{0}j_{x}(z)dz\approx wj_{x}(0)/\alpha_{abs},}

(251)

with
t
the thickness of the semiconductor in the
z
direction. In the last step of Eq. (
251
), we assumed
t\gg\alpha_{abs}^{-1}
. The open-circuit photovoltage is determined by the condition that the shift and drift currents cancel out at each
z
:

\displaystyle{j_{x}(z)=&\;\sigma_{ph}(z)\frac{V_{oc}}{d}.}

(252)

Assuming ideally that the temperature is sufficiently low (
k_{B}T\ll E_{g}
) for the dark conductivity to be negligible,
77
77

77

See Sec V of Ref.
Pusch et al. 2023

the drift current is simply proportional to the linear conductivity of photo-excited carriers; this conductivity is assumed to have the Drude form:

\displaystyle{\sigma_{ph}(z)=&\;e^{2}\tau_{tr}\bigg(\frac{n(z)}{m_{e}}+\frac{p(z)}{m_{h}}\bigg),}

(253)

with
\tau_{tr}
the transport lifetime,
n
(resp.
p
) the photo-excited electron density (resp. hole density), and
m_{e}
(resp.
m_{h}
) the effective mass for electrons (resp. holes). Assuming that the semiconductor is intrinsic,

\displaystyle{n(z)=p(z)=\tau_{rec}\text{Abs}(z);\;\;\;\;\sigma_{ph}(z)=\frac{e^{2}\tau_{tr}\tau_{rec}}{m_{r}}\text{Abs}(z),}

(254)

with
\tau_{rec}
the recombination time [cf. Eq. (
64
)] and
m_{r}^{-1}=m_{e}^{-1}+m_{h}^{-1}
. Combining all the above equations,

\displaystyle{\text{Eff}=&\;\frac{1}{4}\frac{.511\,MeV}{\hbar\omega}\frac{m_{r}}{m_{f}}\frac{\mathfrak{s}^{2}/\tau_{tr}\tau_{rec}}{c^{2}},}

(255)

which is equivalent to Eq. (
23
).
